Fish with a Violin
	after Marc Chagall

I.

Look! she says, mom over here--
the horse-woman, and the houses,
and here is the fish with a violin.

She presents each feature like a tour guide,
re-painting the painting with a wave
of her hand--proprietorial, as if she knows

the terrain of this dream-world better
than her own. Mom flashes an embarrassed
smile at me, and hurries her blond head

out of my view. But it is not the view,
I want to tell her, that I am taking in.
It is the music the fish is playing,

music her daughter and I both hear
more clearly than the coughing guard,
as bright as his blue fish-smile.

II.

Cat-gut, of course, is the irony,
with which the piece is strung.

The irony is, of course, the fish,
playing his own sweet scales.

He is shaped like an eye.
And his eye is shaped like an eye.

When he closes his eye, it frowns.
When he frowns, the music is visible.

III.

9/8/09

Sometimes the goat plays cello,
but he isn't very good.

I forgot, also, the fish's teeth--
they are pointed, and he wears his

fanning tail like a full-length dress.
Perhaps, like so many animals,

he symbolizes Christ. But maybe
he's jewish, fiddling to accompany

his own Hasidic dancing in the street.
Whatever the faith of the fish,

whatever his notes of symbology,
he is a tear shed from a rainbow,

a celebration of dexterous fins,
not a prince under a spell,

or a fisherman's guilty dream.
A fish! An actual fish! Look

how he bows that sweet violin.

IV.

"Why are you playing the voilin?"
Because music is my water while on land.

"Are you smiling, or baring your teeth?"
I forgot to close my mouth in ecstasy.

"Have you ever been caught by a hook?"

9/9/09

Only once. Her name was Esmerelda.

V.

The sound
of a single violin,
straining, gut-on-gut,
rosin gripping
and pulling my attention
down the tiled
subway corridor. He is
here, somewhere,
loose change glittering
in his open case,
he is here,
somewhere precise,
yet the music
is everywhere.

VI.

My son would have liked you, I say. Ah,
yes, he replies, we have tea on Tuesday.
At which, I begin to weep.

Can you speak to all of the dead?, I ask.
I can, he tells me, but better than speaking--
he lifts his red violin into place.

How charming, I smile, that you play at teatime,
but won't your own tea get cold? Don't you 
know? he winks, music is all I consume.

I brush the crumbs from the table into my lap.
I brush the crumbs from my lap onto my shoes.
He holds the bow like a teacup, pinkie in air.

VII.

"In our life there is a single color, as on an artist's palette, which provides the meaning of life and art. It is the color of love."

Varian Fry in Marseille

9/10/09

1.  To begin, open your dictionary and randomly turn to a page.  Then choose a word.  Consider the word and its definition(s).  You don't need to include the word in your poem, but you may use the word and/or any of its associations to bring you into the poem.  

presumptuous--overstepping due bounds; taking liberties

sumptuous liberty

2.  Include a pleasant memory.

driving through fields in Burgundy

3.  In a poem of 20 lines or less, include 15 of these words or forms of the words:  shell, lid, plate, eucalyptus, vertigo, flat, ricochet, wisdom, square, lie, clock, mask, crisis, shard, scale, corner, place, wheel, cinder, weather, scar, eye, pillow, shatter, constellation, pallid, leg, axis, tremor, fraction

sumptuous liberty

VII.

We wheel through
a field of wheat, painted
"the color of love"--
tremors of sumptuous
liberty, scaling
our legs, our spine--
the flat lid of the sky
mimicking wisdom,
constellations of whiteflies
scarring our windshield,
the clock stopped,
all crisis averted,
shards and cinders of leaves,
the fraction and refraction
of a cathedral called Burgundy--
terrain Chagall must have 
crept through, masked 
in a lie to survive--
fleeing Paris, pallid,
shell-sick, bound for Marseilles.

9/11/09

VIII

Not now, I say, I have work to do,
but he slaps me with his tail, re-
peatedly, like a dog nudging under
my hand, or a cat rubbing my ankle,
he slaps and slaps as if challenging
a cad to a duel of honor, or smack-
ing a naughty child in a bygone era. 
He slaps me, now, continually, wet
ridges on his caudal fin stroking
my cheek like a nine-tailed lash.

9/14/09

IX

In the dark, only his eye is visible.
Or it could be the eye of a horse,
a woman's breast. It could be the moon.

As if stamped from the same archetype: 
moon, woman, breast, eye, fish--all
together in a chorus of shimmering.

9/16/09

X

"What is the secret of happiness?"
The up-stroke and the down-stroke become the same.

"How do you play when you lose what you love?"
More rosin! More rosin! Then dig in the bow.

"What if noone likes the music you play?"
I am present at every concert. I listen to myself.

XI

Young friends send us hand-made cards
of colored paper, cut and folded.
On one, a hundred teardrops--she must
have counted--in every color available
from her scented coloring set. They rain
down on the words no adult had found
the simplicity to say: I'm sorry
your baby died. Perhaps the rain
washed away extravagant words like 
"loss" and phrases like "pass over."
Perhaps they melted in a pool of color,
leaving the essential message to endure
this rain, this irridescent rain, rendered
so very carefully, in every possible color.

9/17/09

XII

My first kiss was not in the rain.
But in the kiss I remember as though
it were my first kiss, it is raining
in the desert, we are young, fifteen,
unexpected moisture wakes everything
around us, within us, and the moment
after kissing goes on, seems endless,
and in it, between touching, between
breathing, in a droplet of stillness,
I hear the sound of a single violin.

9/18/09

XIII

Woman and horse, entwined, a caduceus
of color, the blended forms suggesting
a fusion of love, of shape, two selves
of different species becoming one.

Perhaps the fish in the background
was once a man, a fiddler with hands
whose love of water and flirtation
with sea-nymphs one day backfired.

No one can tell what he fish used
to be, but the woman and the horse
are giving birth to one another--
the horse springs from her shoulder,

the woman's hair is the horse's mane,
one from the other, the other from
the one, no seams left to distinguish
where once these two were seperate.

9/21/09

XIV

The woman in the background
is holding a bouquet of stars.

She balances with the fish
in the painting's composition.

As a child, I could see whole
galaxies in a darkened room.

A bouquet made of cut paper
would make my mother smile.

I was not practicing adult life,
but the death of childhood.

Now I only see what is right
in front of me. I see numbers,

in columns, and want to add
them up, because I can.

But I have lost the mathematics--
or should I say the astrophysics?--

of turning constellations
into a bouquet of flowers,

and of turning bunches of flowers
into pinwheels of stars.

Then, I see this painting--
a window cut in the museum wall.

The fish begins to play his tune,
and the flower-lady starts dancing.

I lean into the painting,
pretending to examine brushstrokes,

and the stars begin to tinkle,
and I want to fall inside.

9/25/09

Seasonal Disturbance

Winter is coming, even
in the middle of Spring,
that sense of Winter coming.
I feel it on the back
of my neck when walking.
Not Winter, but the sense
of Winter coming, is always
with me, shrill companion,
singing high up in a register
only I can hear. Winter
will be here soon, though it
never arrives. Even the dead
of Winter is not this kind
of Winter, the one that
perpetually approaches,
like an army just out
of firing range. You take
a step back, they take
a step forward. Only shapes,
not people, only threat.
It comes, and it comes,
supplied like the wind
with endless capacity.
Icicles form and glint
in my peripheral vision,
the gravel sounds like snow.
I can almost see
my breath now, but perhaps
I imagine it, like steam
from a kettle not yet
hot. Winter is coming.
Turn a street corner,
it will greet you with
a gust of frozen air.

9/28/09

Dreamworker's Union No. 47

We multiply the dust
in the corner of your eye,
layering dust on dust
the opposite of janitors,
we are the custodians
of lost consciousness,
groundskeepers of the dark.

The wages of death are pitiful,
your payment comes only once.
But sleep is a nightly rehearsal,
requiring our hand-made props--
the dragons of silk we float
on stilts to distract you,
the red-eyed horses fuming.

On our toolbelt, a gun
that fires marigolds and poppies.
We are the gypsy children
on the subways, crowding around.
We gather near the pockets
of your double-lined thoughts
hoping to catch the watch chain.

The rules are strict, the hours
fly by in an opaited haze.
Our boss, the prince of forgetting,
checks the timecards twice.
The customer is never right.
We have a sign proclaiming this
hung on the dumptrucks at dawn.

9/29/09

The Failure of Philosophy

Hedges and dry-stone walls
laid on the green earth
like fallen cobwebs.

Who cut the threads?
Or was it the breeze?
Even the air can be selfish.

Give back some warmth--
we ask this of the wind,
and of each other.

Who are we to demand?
Seen from the air, just dots.
Bugs tracing the hedgerows.

Each one, a viewing point,
breathed awake, and blinking,
tangled in the greenery.

Some would say the source
of our vital energy is food.
I say it is mystery.

The bug on land, the bug
in the air, and in space--
a meaning-making speck

oblivious to its smallness,
overpowered by it smallness,
out for a walk in a field.

9/30/09

Concerning the Vapors

" a dark wind had been rising toward me from somewhere deep in my future"
-Albert Camus, /The Stranger/

The wind at night consoles me
with melodies so frightening
they help to pass the time.

Out of nowhere, we come marching
on our toothpick legs, against
the wind, hair tousled like hags.

The point is to do it, regardless,
to scrape the oak barrel with your
tin filling cup, seeking more wine.

And so we do, we raid the air
for meaning, scour the ground
for grains of common touch.

Everyone who is hungry is alive.
Once you have starved to death,
the starving ends, and ghosts

are rarely seen among our food.
They haunt events, not objects.
And food is a precursor to life.

10/2/09

Stop stating the obvious,
I tell myself, stop wrestling
with facts--they have no limbs,

no special moves to defend 
themselves, such easy opponents.
The pattern of the cieling fan

seems somehow desperately lonely,
obscuring the shine of the floor.
It pummels the air in anger,

always hungry for more air.
I could be like that. I could
circulate the breeze of thoughts,

the high winds of feeling,
whipped up like stiff frosting,
aerated like a well-made fire.

Instead the movment of air
reminds me of my porosity,
blows through my transparent skin.

"The wind bloweth where it listeth." (John 3:8)

The wind at night frightens me
with melodies so reassuring,
I gather them into my sleep.

Schola Philosophae

I came upon the feeling of today
like a stranger, like a dragon,
a drunk in the street breathing
alcoholic fire.

I want a room of stone, a cave,
impenetrable, growing moss
in the slats, some place
I can think.

The form breaks down eventually,
ricochet of music on the rocks,
feet shuffle, churning dust, this
crazy dance.

Who cares what I pretend to want?
Break sticks in pieces, toss them
up, call the grounded heap
a tree.

The myth of someone who can understand:
I've chased it. Commere, kid, listen
close--even nihilism goes out
of fashion.

I came upon my own half-truths today,
archeologically, digging down,
the death mask looked just like me,
eyes wide.

10/8/09

London

"The purpose of poetry is to remind us /
how difficult it is to remain just one person..."
-Czeslaw Milosz, "Ars Poetica?"

The city has a way of convincing you
that all houses should be made of brick.

I tap the enter key twice for a new stanza.
That arbitrary extra pinkie flick

is all I have to guide me, a random gesture
that will determine the shape of this poem.

So, let's talk about London, talk in couplets,
every line either a beginning or an end.

Now, you see, I'm intimidated, by a city
that is itself, so Alpha, so Omega,

and a form which draws attention to itself,
unlike the city, stewing in grays and browns.

What can I say to you, old friend? new discovery?
On Thursday, walking past St. Paul's, wind

picked up the leaves--they could have been
leaves in another city, say, New York.

But this London wind scooped up London leaves,
and thrust them into my arms like a boquet.

The right camera click, at that moment, would 
have caught a whirling cloud of pure surprise.

Was it random? This stroke of city air, the breath
of a thousand builders, teachers, doormen exhaling?

How many times has the air gone through them,
into the trees at Regent's Park, and back again?

The buildings would convince you that people
are their accessory, soft landscape among the hard.

But even they conspire a little, take the tea shop 
in the cathedral crypt--tables placed over plaques 

of the famous dead. You can order tiramisu, 
the spongy layers reminiscent of re-paved streets.

10/9/09

The layers under foot are whispering, not to us,
but to each other--the grave of decimated Romans

murmuring to the Black Plague pit above them,
who in turn sing lullabies to Victorian paupers.

Who, then, really handed me that bouquet of air?
The city can convince you, like a person,

that it is singular, named, organic, fixed.
Do not trust this layered shapeshifter--

the stripes of buried ages still live there.
Bear witness, in parallel lines, to this:

I was handed the wind, by some passing leaves,
each leaf an epitaph, a face of London.

10/12/09

10/13/09

To the Bear in a Neighbor's Tree

How quickly we become accustomed to the light,
blinking through discomfort, standing upright,
when our claws break, we fashion tools, use 
them, and then just as easily put them down.

We discover clumps of hair on the ground,
and see our lack of fur as a great improvement,
stamping and shivering, we like a cold wind!
When our night vision fades, we stumble a dance.

Now, we have lost you too, primeval cousin,
lost the instinct that might have guided us
in shooing you back where you came from.
We can no longer smell what is on the wind.

You sat all day in a tree, learning our gestures.
You waved at the crowds and considered making a speech.
When you became too much like us, we brought you down,
and hauled your massive blackness into the night.

The truth is that we lost you long ago, long before 
our friends loaded up their guns. Look how far 
we have come! Our fingers fit the triggers.
And still we remember not to look in an animal's eyes.

I looked, and became frozen on my couch.
I blinked into the sunlight, and you were gone.
The black spot in the tree is no longer you.
It is the place that you have burned into my mind.

10/19/09

Raindrops hit the ground at precisely seven-oh-two p.m. I
know this because I checked my watch against the horizon.
Everything fixed,
bordering perfect,
weather descends.

At the bus stop a woman whose ankles were ringed with mud
splashes lit a filterless cigarette and became a chimney.
Puffing blue
Puffing white
rising hopes.

Just there I knew I lost the tension of the scene in music
not unlike a nursery song I'd often repeat as a child.
Higgledy-piggledy
roses ringing
wormwood song.

Down like a hatchet these stanzas fell, inventing themselves
for pleasure and disgust, the form filling like crust of pie.
Hatchety hatchet
rickety racket
meaningful joke.

10/23/09

The Autograph

The curtains rush toward each other, framing me,
center stage, dead in her arms. Backstage, I rub 
off the greasepaint wrinkles that have aged me 
from seventeen to seventy. Two freshmen girls

appear in the doorway, holding their programs.
One is dabbing away the shiny tracks of her tears.
The other asks, "Can we have your autograph?"
Confused, and embarassed, unsure who they think

I am, I dash off my name--no salutation, nothing
charming, signing like a check , signing to prove 
who I am, so they'll see--nobody, some kid in a
high school play. The teary-eyed one looks up,

"We know you'll be famous one day." Exeunt.
My girlfriend at the time, also in the play, mutters
something under her breath about "little girls."
Just last week, someone asked for my autograph--

a collector of autographs, no doubt he was placing
a bet that one day one of my poems would burst
on impact like the first full stage production
two small-town teenage girls had ever seen.

I wanted to tell these girls that the one sobbing
beside me on stage, at my noble, five-act life,
was wailing all the louder because I was blowing
on her neck, last-night shenanigans, having been

spit on, and choked, my lines stolen twice, I wanted
to corpse the living, and she, now a pro, singing
opera in Germany, took the eros of the situation
deep into her wounded life, and only cried louder.

I hope she becomes famous one day, signs autographs
more often than overdrawn checks. But that is not for me
to predict, any more than I can tell when art
will strike, and whom, and in what terrible force.

10/27/09

Find yourself sunk in to darkened seats, resisting 
the urge to rattle your bracelets, or cough, 
the proscenium rippling, the curtain an ocean of felt
which parts in a miracle of pulleys and rope,

11/2/09

and there is your life--its vast contradictions
littering the stage like confetti, old girlfriends,
new neighbors, swapping their lines and mingling,
pretending to love, and anger, tickle and wound.

Now I am alone with my poem, pushing at words,
trying to remind them what it feels like to live
the part, dropping hints and improvised sentences,
blowing on them, ever so gently, like a flame.

Human of the Year
    or Paris, Altered

Sure, there are wakes, also sure
there will be butter in the mouths
of the gaping croissants, lemonade
stands on the fringe of the Seinne,
beauty schools opening and closing
their doors, the tick from beneath
a man's jacket ribbed with dynamite,
ululations in the jamboree, swaddled
oarsmen guiding the gondola's clip,
assuredly cruising a sawtooth tack,
the metronome of ripples congesting
along the narrow-mouthed prow, down
and out underneath the gilt bridge,
lettered "N" for nonsense, nuisance,
noisome, the last narcissus abloom.

# # #

I'm too tired, I say
to write this all down.
Tomorrow. When the freeze
thaws, the chill recedes,
the tomorrow representing
spring, 
        a time of re-birth,
for those who believe in such
things.
        I'm too tired, I repeat,
but not in a musical way, just
say it, a second time, it, it, it
--that meddlesome word, nodescript,
unspecific, pointing out the un-
point-out-ability of throwaway
words.
        Exhausted, really, rent
asunder with the weariest grief.

The Autograph

The curtains rush toward each other, framing me,
center stage, dead in her arms. Backstage, I rub
off the greasepaint wrinkles that have aged me
from seventeen to seventy. Two freshmen girls

appear in the doorway, holding their programs.
One is dabbing away the shiny tracks of her tears.
The other asks, "Can we have your autograph?"
Confused, and embarrassed, unsure who they think

I am, I dash off my name--no salutation, nothing
charming, signing like a check , signing to prove
who I am, so they'll see--nobody, some kid in a
high school play. The teary-eyed one looks up,

"We know you'll be famous one day." Exeunt.
My girlfriend at the time, also in the play, mutters
something under her breath about "little girls."
Just last week, someone asked for my autograph--

a collector of autographs, no doubt he was placing
a bet that one day one of my poems would burst
on impact like the first full stage production
two small-town teenage girls had ever seen.

I wanted to tell these girls that the one sobbing
beside me on stage, at my noble, five-act life,
was wailing all the louder because I was blowing
on her neck, last-night shenanigans, having been

spit on, and choked, my lines stolen twice, I wanted
to corpse the living, and she, now a pro, singing
opera in Germany, took the eros of the situation
deep into her wounded life, and only cried more.

I hope she becomes famous one day, signs autographs
more often than overdrawn checks. But that is not for me
to predict, any more than I can tell when art
will strike, and whom, and in what terrible force.

Find yourself sunk in to darkened seats, resisting
the urge to rattle your bracelets, or cough,
the proscenium rippling, the curtain an ocean of felt
which parts in a miracle of pulleys and rope,

and there is your life--its vast contradictions
littering the stage like confetti, old girlfriends,
new neighbors, swapping their lines and mingling,
pretending to love and anger, tickle and wound.

Now I am alone with my poem, pushing at words,
trying to remind them what it feels like to live
the part, dropping hints and improvised sentences,
blowing on them, ever so gently, like a flame.

11/10/09

When We Heard What Happened, We Drank the Last of the Lemonade in Silence

In the wake of the news,
we sat watching each other, sipping
lemonade in winter, unsure
if it was sweet or bitter, bitter
or sour, puckering the buds
of our mouths, scowling at the weather,
swaddled in gray, the tick
of the clock marking time like a metronome,
puckered and scowling, like two
beauty school gossips, one flapping a combs
to dry the blue disinfectant,
the other brushing down the customer's fringe,
each of us shaking our heads 
disapprovingly at this, such terible news.



When We Heard What Happened, 
We Drank the Last of the Lemonade in Silence

In the wake of the news,
we sat watching 
each other, sipping
lemonade in winter, unsure
if it was sweet 
or bitter, bitter
or sour, puckering 
the buds
of our mouths, 
scowling at the weather,
swaddled in gray, the tick
of the clock marking 
time like a metronome,
puckered and scowling,
we sat there like 
two beauty school 
gossips, one flapping 
a comb to dry 
the blue disinfectant,
the other brushing down 
the customer's fringe,
as the radio plays,
"you don't always get
what you want,"
and the comb slips,
and clatters on the table,
and the first one says
"my bad," though really,
how bad is all that, 
considering? And we 
each go back to shaking 
our heads very slowly
at this, such terible news.


11/11/09

key ring, wake, lemonade, cruise, oar, bud, mouth, beauty school, swaddle, tick, fringe, sawtooth, congest, flap, down, ululate, crumpet, clip, metronome, jamboree

Quote a song you like
Quote a phrase you dislike or distrust
Optional: use a long title


You Crazy Kids

We flipped through the years like keys
on a keyring, nevermind-ing the wake, 

making lemons from all that lemonade,
we cruised the high waters, oar in hand.

We not only smelled roses, but popped
the buds in our mouths, sliced ourselves

on the thorns, hard knocks from the
school of beauty, swaddled in reassuring

leaves. We ticked off the friendly, hung
out on the fringe, our sawtooth confessions

jagging through pride and remorse, lungs
congested, knees wagging a flapper dance.

Things were going down. Our ululations no 
longer cheered us, nor crumpets, nor toast,

we clipped through the bushes staggering,
force-marched for a time like a metronome,

our ragged little jamboree! Call us collateral
damage, call us blackbird, singing in the

dead of night. Say burning out is giving up.
Say what you like about us. I know I will.

11/12/09

Human Shade

They are cutting her out of her clothes.
They are cutting the straps of the thick
leather vest, and her jeans, already torn
and soaked with blood, they slit up
the side like an evening dress. I can't
imagine her wearing a dress, any dress.

11/13/09

She asks about the other three--her man,
who broke his ribs over the handlebars,
and the two in front they swerved to avoid,
peeling off the oxygen mask to wonder,
"How is Mike?" "Is Charlene OK?"
With four injured, the big guy unconcsious,
they need more hands, so I help log-roll
her onto the board, stand over her to shade
her eyes from the high desert sun, hold
bottles of saline, a human chuppah with hands.
I am inches from her face when she asks
about the pin--is it still on her vest?
It looks like a plastic name tag, like 
she might waitress at a diner sometimes,
and when I figure out the hell-bent pins,
I take it off to read it: "Queen Shit."
The paramedic asks her, "why 'Queen Shit'?"
and she replies, out one side of her blood-
specked mouth, "because I'm Queen Shit."
I reach for the free hand of the guy
with busted ribs, and he takes it
for safekeeping, while the logic of our--
can it be called a conversation?--strikes
me as either absurd or profound. We're talking
her through this, splinting her wrist,
someone finds a towel, and two of us make
an awning. A passing biker friend offers
to relieve me of my duty as human shade,
and when I turn back toward my car, it comes
down on me--everything I pushed out of my mind
to make room for the first-aid training.
The other three are loaded up, having left
only some fluids and scraps of clothing
on the road. The biker-friend, my relief watch,
asks me who I am. And I tell her, "I was just
behind you. I just happend to be right behind."

It is heavy, and the night wears thin.

Use at least ten of the following twenty words:

key ring, wake, lemonade, cruise, oar, bud, mouth, beauty school, swaddle, tick, fringe, sawtooth, congest, flap, down, ululate, crumpet, clip, metronome, jamboree

Quote a song you like
Quote a phrase you dislike or distrust
Optional: use a long title

Five days in England

My briefcase contains
a heart and lungs. At night,
I can hear its congested
breath, through the long,
locked crevice of its mouth.

When I unsnap the clips,
and raise the flap, it does
not inhale sharply, but,
with suitable restraint,
just lets the air ease in.

The sawtooth band cruises
down the street, flapping
and strumming, ululating
brass, leaving the lemonade-
sippers in their wake, toes
ticking like a metronome.

Crumpets and rosebuds,
swaddled in lace,
a jamboree of preserves,
a keyring lined
with skeleton keys,
that open the beauty
school doors.

Yes, I said, downing
the whiskey, I will
certainly come to tea.

12/3/09

surge, 30,000, service, crazy, nickel, cape, photo, security, dog, rhetoric, vague, temper, bucket.  Use a color that continues to return, a scene that continues to replay, and the phrase, "our resolve is unwavering."

Concerning My Blankie

I will not complain
that the blanket shrank
and is yellow instead
of my favorite color,
tan. I will wear it
like a cape, and you
can take a photo, take
three hours of video,
which is like 30,000
photos flipping fast,
photos of me not
complaining, but surging
forth in a crazy temper,
yellow cape trailing
in a vague, half-hearted
flap. This is my service
to humanity--my security
blanket, which ought
to be tan, transforms me,
my resolve unwavering,
my cause redoubled
by two-for-a-nickel
gumballs and the yellow
sun on my limp blankie,
which shrank, into a square
of not-quite-right colored
happiness, which I wear now,
and parade in, for the camera,
and for liberty, and for you.

12/8/09

In a momemnt of monumental dumbness
the great cellist once left his cello,
lying stunned like a kidnapping victim
in the trunk of a New York taxi cab.

Nevermind it was made by Stradivarius,
made almost three hundred years ago,
and millions of dollars are meaningless.
"The instrument," he said "is my voice."

When he finished playing Bach, we looked 
at each other, as the final chords of horse-
hair and varnish echoed away, leaving 
a heart beat of silence before the applause.

12/13/09

The Instrument is my Voice
	for Valerie

In a momemnt of absentmindedness
the great cellist once left his cello,
lying, like a kidnapping victim
in the trunk of a New York taxi cab.

Nevermind it was made by Stradivarius,
made almost three hundred years ago,
and millions of dollars mean nothing.
"The instrument," he said "is my voice."

When he finished playing Bach last night, 
we looked at one another, notes of horsehair 
and rosin fading into half a heartbeat 
of silence before the applause.

I helped you into the car, and closed the door,
and stood for a moment in the outdoor cold, 
admiring you in the passenger seat, upright,
and buckled in, ready for the trip.

One day, I know, you will leave like this, 
without me--composed like a mannequin, hands
folded napkin in your lap, eyes ahead.
I will watch through a fogged-up window.

My prayer is this: make me capable now
of just a few strokes of the music
I will be granted the strength to bow
when that car door closes finally with a thump.

Let me find a voice worth speaking through,
or, if not a voice, a beat of silence,
squeezing our blood-filled hands together,
in a pulse of comprehension.

Let me reach, through tactile memory, for 
whatever seems left behind, though our palms
are inscribed with the future, and curl
under latches and handles, even when locked.

12/17/09

The Instrument is My Voice
	for Valerie

In a moment of absentmindedness
the great cellist once left his cello,
lying, like a kidnapping victim
in the trunk of a New York taxi cab.

Never mind it was made by Stradivarius,
made almost three hundred years ago,
and millions of dollars mean nothing.
"The instrument," he said "is my voice."

When he finished playing Bach last night, 
we looked at one another, notes of horsehair 
and varnish fading into half a heartbeat 
of silence before the applause.

I helped you into the car, and closed the door,
and stood for a moment in the outdoor cold, 
admiring you in the passenger seat, upright,
all buckled in, ready for the trip.

One day, I know, you will leave me like this, 
composed like a mannequin, hands folded 
in your lap like a napkin, eyes ahead.
I will watch through a fogged-up window.

My prayer is this: make me capable now
of just a few strokes of the music
I will be granted the strength to bow
when that car door closes finally with a thump.

Let me find a voice worth speaking through,
or, if not a voice, a beat of silence,
squeezing our blood-filled hands together,
in a pulse of comprehension.

Let me reach, through tactile memory, for 
whatever might be left behind, though our palms
are inscribed with the future, and curl
under latches and handles, even when locked.

12/26/09

Traumschule
	(Dream-school)

Let us twirl 
like a shoal 
of moonlit fish.
Let the spool
of the real world unwind.

Even the thorns 
have their postulates,
smart-sounding theories 
about pain's
educational properties--
how the lash
helps focus the mind.

The faces of truth
stretched over
false heads, sprouting 
through dark soil
like cauliflower.

The lodestar bears 
the weight of our promises.
Under a fish-bladder pellicle, 
the sound of air.

Even the lies
have their reasons, here.
Even the ghost,
who can raise a hand,
will surely have his say.

1/5/10

7 A.M. Exactly

The bells of the post office tower
sound out, testing the air.

This is no place for anger,
only quiet disappointment,

the kind we're designed to settle
into, laid out in the star charts

as the vague but unavoidable
destination of our lives.

Listen again to the music,
the low, mellow sounds.

If I don't have enough time 
to make something of this poem,

let me make something of my life,
or at least, let me prentend

that the bells are in charge
of their destiny, dong-donging

through the empty space
between the tower and my ear.

1/7/10

Try to include at least ten of these:  vellum, stain, neck, blue, benediction, bowl, sprawl, iris, line, street, shape, dissolve, flay, brass, pearl, fracture, map, tile, olive, key

Also, before you write, look closely at a picture.  This can be any picture ( a personal photograph, a photograph in a magazine, a painting, etc.).  In the poem, you don't need to make a direct reference to the picture, but try to bring some aspect of the picture into the poem.

Another option:  Locate a dictionary of Indo-European Roots (many editions of the American Heritage Dictionary include an Index of Indo-European Roots).  Choose a word, either randomly or intentionally, and then look up the word's root.  Make a list of words from that root cluster.  Include at least five in your poem.

1/8/10

The irises uncurl
their blue benediction

Greek:
porphyr=purple (rock)
iris=rainbow goddess=irridescent

Sometimes I can see the violet
beyond violet, halo on a well-
lit spoon, death-throe of a
flourescent tube, underwater
pearls--each image fractured
by its transportation, flayed 
in transit to the iris of my eye.
It is fitting, after all, that 
Iris ruled over rainbows, and
"porphyr," which led to "purple,"
comes from an irridescent stone.
At the edge of our seeing, there
is another color of light,
a blue benediction, dissolving
to silver, brass, and vellum.
What colors lie beyond? How must
the rainbow continue, measureably
but imperceptibly, to progress?
What do we call the invisible?

1/10/10

Sometimes I can see the violet
beyond violet, halo on a well-
lit spoon, death-throe of a
flourescent tube, underwater
pearls--each image fractured
in its transit toward my eye.

It is fitting that Iris ruled 
over rainbows, and "porphyr," 
which shares a root with "purple,"
is a type of irridescent stone.
At the edge of our seeing, there
is another color of light,

a blue benediction, dissolving
to silver, brass, and vellum.
What colors lie beyond? How must
the rainbow continue--because it
must continue, after all, in-
visibly, through the mathematics
of faster and faster light.

1/11/10

A Place That Seems Unreachable

Last night I dreamt
his eyes were open.

He was present
in them, not far away

as babies sometimes are,
translucencent, liquid.

His eyes were dark,
adult, half-lidded

and fixed on me,
but not in a stare.

The eyes he never
opened, last night

opened in a place
that seems unreachable--

though I was there
when he was born, there

when his heartbeat 
stopped, and now

I have seen him smiling
through his eyes.

He did not seem to want
anything, or have

any message to give.
It was just, perhaps

I had never seen them
open, and now I have.

1/12/10

Faster and Faster Light

Sometimes I can see the violet
beyond violet, halo on a well-
lit spoon, death-throe of a
flourescent tube, underwater
pearls--each image fractured
in its transit toward my eye.

It is fitting that Iris ruled 
over rainbows, and "porphyr," 
in line with our word "purple,"
is a type of iridescent stone.
At the edge of our seeing, there
is another color of light,

a blue benediction, dissolving
to silver, brass, and vellum.
The rainbow continues--because it
must continue, says the mind, 
through the invisible mathematics
of faster and faster light.

1/27/10

Because I love his small body
suspended above the ground
by an architecture of legs,

I kill him quickly, stomping
my heel like the finale of a tap
dancing routine, stomping faster,

I hope, than his neurons can
carry messages of fear or pain,
stomping faster than suffering.

Whom have you loved this much?
Loved with a vetrinanry needle,
loved with a coup de gras,

loved by saying, "No, that's
enough," or loved by saying, "Yes, 
it is time to unplug the tubes"?

Such acts of love appear hateful,
and true to Stanislovsky's form,
when my heel hits the ground, anger

flashes up like an Olympian bolt,
thrown because power is pleasurable,
and I think, for a moment, "take that."

1/28/10

The Spider

Because I love his small body
suspended above the ground
by an architecture of legs,

I kill him quickly, stomping
my heel like a tap dance routine
finale, stepping faster, I 

hope, than his neurons can
carry the message of fear and pain,
stamping faster than suffering.

Whom have you loved this much?
Loved with a vetrinanry needle,
loved with a coup de grace,

loved by saying, "No, that's
enough," or loved by saying, "Yes, 
it is time to unplug the tubes"?

Such acts of love appear hateful,
and true to Stanislavsky's form,
when my heel hits the ground, anger

flashes up like an Olympian bolt,
thrown because power is pleasurable,
and I think, for a moment, "take that."

But I do not hate the spider,
any more than I love what he has
become, a splay of imperfect

geometry, a galaxy for the confused.
I fold him in tissue, dab the floor,
as though I had just found dirt,

and now disdain curls into my
nose, as though I took no part
in the splotch I now rub away.

Use at least twelve of: flap, winter, torch, pail, jug, strum, lever, massage, octopus, marionette, stow, pumice, rug, jam, limp, campfire, startle, wattle, bruise, chimney, tome, talon, fringe, walker

Include something that tastes terrible.
Include some part (from a few words to several lines) of a previous poem that didn't quite pan out.
Include a sound that makes you happy.

Waking Delusion

The last flap of winter,
a torch in a pail, hissing,
a jug strummed and levered,
blown through the hollow,
wind massaging your hair
like an octopus, lifting
trash like marionettes.
Whatever we stowed, we
surrender, turning out the
pumic in our pockets,
flipping the rug, jamming
the emptiness into the space
before us, into which we
limp, toward an imaginanry
campfire still aglow in our
startled hearts. Having
burned the wattle and signed
the thatch, our brused homes
chug up like chimneys
before us, little tomes
of home-knowledge spitting
talons of flame at the fringe,
as we walk, we walkers, never
ceasing to believe, that soon
enough, the winds will cease.

Waking Delusion

The last flap of winter,
a torch in a pail, hissing,
a jug strummed and levered,
blown through its hollow,
wind massaging your hair
like an octopus, lifting
trash like marionettes.
Whatever we stowed, we
surrender, turning out the
pumic in our pockets,
flipping the rug, jamming
the emptiness into the space
before us, into which we
limp, toward an imaginanry
campfire still aglow in our
startled hearts. My briefcase 
contains a pair of lungs. 
I hear its congested breath, 
through the long, locked 
crevice of its mouth.
The cat used to snore like
this at the foot of our bed.
Our tongues can still taste 
the roofing tar, alive
in the brittle cold air.
Having burned the wattle,
signed the thatch, our brused 
homes chug up like chimneys
before us, little tomes
of home-knowledge spitting
talons of flame at the fringe,
as we walk, we walkers, never
ceasing to believe, that soon
the winds will cease.

2/16/10

Recession

The last flap of winter,
a torch in a pail, hissing,
a jug strummed and thumped,
blown through its hollow,
wind massaging your hair
like an octopus, lifting
trash like marionettes.

Whatever we stowed, we
surrender, turning out the
pumic in our pockets,
tossing dust into the space
before us, limping toward 
a cellophane fire still aglow 
in our startled hearts. 

My briefcase contains a pair 
of lungs. I hear their 
congested breathing 
through the long, locked 
crevice of its mouth.
The cat used to snore like
this at the foot of our bed.

Our brused homes chug up 
like chimneys before us. 
The taste of roofing tar
singes the brittle air,
as we walk, head bowed, never
ceasing to believe, that soon
these winds will cease.

Recession

The last flap of winter,
a torch in a pail, hissing,
a jug strummed and thumped,
blown through its hollow,
wind massaging your hair
like an octopus, lifting
trash like marionettes.

Whatever we stowed, we
surrender, turning out the
pumice in our pockets,
tossing dust into the space
before us, limping toward 
a cellophane fire still aglow 
in our startled hearts. 

Goodbye, distant promise,
goodbye promiser, and good-
bye to the promissory note.
Now paper means: paper,
subject to wind and dust.
The eye of the needle is
threaded with cobweb filament.

My briefcase contains a pair 
of lungs. I hear their 
congested breathing 
through the long, locked 
crevice of its mouth.
The cat at the foot of our 
bed used to snore like this.

Now, our bruised homes chug up 
like chimneys before us. 
The taste of roofing tar
singes the brittle air,
as we walk, head bowed, never
ceasing to believe, that soon
these winds will cease.

2/24/10

Politeness, an Ultimatum

Out of the notes of a blue lip-chatter,
the windows spatterd an unknown brown,
we sashay, in our way, through flattery
while outside the tigers keep grumbling.

Come to my bosom, two-headed hope,
I will suckle you proudly and long.
While the wind in the curling juniper
tells stories of the unforgiven past.

Let us poke the night with a sharp stick,
make breathing holes in the jam jar,
and when they drop in a leaf for us,
let us eat it, even though it is poison.

3/1/10

Whole Cloth

One of my colleagues modified
								a pillowcase
to slip over my computer monitor
								preventing dust.
Every time I sweep the hood over,
								I realize
I should be imagining something quaint
								--a birdcage 
covered to let the parrot sleep.
								But all I see
are prisoners from Guantanamo
								kept in darkness
from the unthinkable unknown.
								How closely
they resemble ordinary people,
								though I know
I should be hating them like animals,
								and loving
domestic animals like cats and birds.
								After all,
it is just an intricate machine,
								this computer,
which perhaps is what all animals
								also are.
And wars are just two or more machines
								colliding.
And though they resemble people,
								human pain
is like the channeled electricity
								that makes
the whole computer system run.
								Some people
who wear hoods have all the power
								of God
upon their side, and loaded guns.
								Other people
made up of the same soft covering
								as myself
stand outside the hood, in daylight
								planning how
to make the hooded ones sing.

3/6/10

Use as many of the following words that you possibly can:
 
aquatic, rash, burn, flank, follow, gem, silhouette, jimmy, hike, canvas, muck, placate, violets, gang, skillet, floozy, lap, sinew 
 
Use the phrases:
 
sit tight       working on it
 
Include a historical figure from art, politics, religion or other

A Footpath Underwater Through America

What I say in transit here comes out
a rash of aquatic blubbering, the burn
in my flanks from following fast, gem-
stones, gut-stones, the silhouette
of my forgiver perpetually ahead.
I jimmy open my stamina, hike on up,
canvassing the footpath with my man-
prints in the muck, placating only
the violets by tacking the middle
road, as ever, neither Jeffersonian,
nor Hamiltonian, a press gang of one
marching up the middle of a skillet
searing under foot--true floozy for
the promise held out by compromise,
content to sit tight in Circe's lap,
the sinews straining in my neck
while the jaw flaps on, incohate
and hateful, making only the sound
of bubbles, shoe-muck, and sputtering,
'working on it' as they say, but
only, one must wonder--for how long?

3/12/10

The Anatomy of Mobs
	with a line from "Strange Fruit" by Abel Meeropol

There is a sense to chanting,
beating sticks and stomping--
Visigoths on the far side
of the frozen Danube, whipping
themselves into a frenzy.

There is logic in the group-
mind, pulsing above the crowd,
blood on the leaves and blood 
at the root, the rope's desire
to connect a neck to a tree.

"Tar him!" they chant, "Burn
it down." Terse precursors
to football slogans, battle
cries, whatever can be said
to make the group one animal.

And do we not want to be one?
United in something undeniable?
Think also of the economy--
a small amount of blood
specking so many hands.

But who does not think, swimming
or being swept up within this liquid
organism, that one day that naked
body on the rail, that hooded head
ascending the scaffold could be mine?

In unison, they reach for clubs
and rope, meniscus tightening,
the look in each man's eye, instinct's
pure reason, calculating distance,
testing the stones in their hands.

3/14/10

Jonah
	in memoriam J.B.

It is large, they say, this life,
and however could a dove alight
on the barnacle-armored hull of it
and not fear his own consumption?

You live on through the love
you left behind, in the eyes
of those who speak of you
at the distant whaling outposts.

Although we never met, your story 
is all-too understandable,
since all of us are stowaways, deep
in the hold, tossed on a storm.

How could you not have wanted more?
The rafters creaking overhead,
only a swinging lamp to guide you.
You went out on deck alone.

And when the world found you,
and rammed against your hull,
they say you looked it in the eye
before it swallwed you whole.

Post-Frost

"I have no friends; I make my mind my friend"
-from the Samurai Creed

"Sit tight" was the order, so we did,
flanking Bhagdad in a Jeep
as the city burned.

Behold the muck from whence
Ronald Regan sprang.

Ice on a skillet, yes, be that way.

How can I make my mind my friend,
that floozy, when it only wants to sit
on the laps of cliches?

As Nixon would say, "we're working on it."

I swim the aquatic caverns,
squinting for glints of gems.

"I burn," they all say,
but most are cold to the touch, placating.

Jimmy open the violets. Spring the tulips,
pop poppies, bust azaleas.

No dice. Nobody follows orders anymore.

3/17/10

Use as many of the following words that you possibly can:
 
rash, skillet, burn, flank, sinew , follow, gem, silhouette, jimmy, hike, canvas, muck, violets, placate, gang, floozy, lap, aquatic

Use the phrases:

Include a historical figure from art, politics, religion or other

Plague, Revisited

"I have no castle; I make Immovable Mind my castle."
-from the Samurai Creed

The human rash is spreading.
It spreads like warm oil on a skillet.
It burns in the places we conspire
to eschew, flanking the arms
of the arms-treaty signers,
working the sinews, following
the inking machinery, gemlike
ruby-red. 
           Hung on the walls
are silhouettes of human activity,
freeze-frame lives flattened,
a two-dimensional diorama of Pompeii.
They, too, were told to sit tight.
They, too, were told "we're working
on it." Told to cup their aquatic
dreams in their hands, and sip.

Where is the salve for a species
that hates itself? What castles
or underground caves can shut
it out? The stories we tell
about machinery taking us over
are myths we tell in fear 
of our greatest invention, the mind.

Where are the knights who will do
our dirty work for us, razing
the villages? There is no salve,
no castle, no Ronin, no cave.
We temper our rise to prosperity.
We cushion our descent from fame.
The blisters on our lips
engorge with a lack of speaking.
The parapets all crumble overhead.

3/20/10

Koi Pond
	in memoriam, K.J.

I went to pay a visit to the koi,
to see what they thought of my life,
and how I had been living it.
Beneath the imperturbable surface,
they mouthed the words, saying
"bleb" and "bleb" and "bleb."
Some torpedoed, others swung
a lazy fin, like an oarsman,
turning a casual arc. Some lay
like unexploded mines, chin up.

I became so outwardly still,
the black cat crossing the painted
red bridge failed to notice me,
and the turtle drifted shoreward
like a sail with six points.
Of course, my son, who lived
too briefly for my liking,
was there with me as well,
and my pocket notebook held
against my hip many small
laments, carefully arranged.

A friend of mine is writing
about his melanoma, having reached
bemused indifference that he
could go at any time, hoping
for more life, and smiling
like a child at his future.

What is the future, koi, who
sip the the rare pond surface,
and descend? You are here
to teach me something, I am
sure, just like the tumors
in the dark organs, blooming.

And when our pediatrician
bowed his head, that man
of science became ordained
a priest of human religion.
What was his prayer again?

The water going dark only
makes the orange seem brighter,
as you race, and kiss, and spar
for food, pretending not
to notice me. For this gift
of your indifference, I am
grateful. I will sit until
the pond goes black, the last
orange spark extinguished.

Picnic With Myself

I have brought everything with me--
mason jars of bile clack together,
and a canteen full of self-hatred
sloshes when I walk. The sandwiches
are my favorite: peanut butter and 
loathing, and the trial mix is pep-
perred with small stones of grief
and guilt. I will spread the horse-
hair blanket somewhere as equal in 
unevenness to my thoughts, and picnic
oblivious to the small talk of birds
and the rattle of dry leaves overhead.
I came here to listen only to myself,
and to that end packed in the voices,
shouts and screams tucked tight
beneath the buckled knapsack flap.
Finally, I can eat my fill of doubt,
prove myself impervious to the world.

3/29/10

In Praise of Dark Places

The dark places usually are best--
a secluded corner of the fairground
in which to trade hickeys more dizzy-
ing than the rides. Or coming out from
the eaves of the house, wheeling a 
rumbling garbage bin behind, Orion 
reveals himself a god, worshipped
by a wordless choir of spring frogs.
And here, just before dawn, the cat
curled amiambly at my feet, and you
stretched at my side, this darkness
is our habitat, the place we lay
our bodies safely down, we pilots
of these rented, upright, ambulating
on- and off-road mechanism of fate,
our very skin light-sensitive, here
we crave the dark within the dark.

4/2/10

I live inside a globe of light.
I live in the darkest places I can find.

This is not a riddle. This is
the sound of the riddle being told.

An oxcart mounts a boulder in the path.
The once-padded rumble sharpens, carries

through the stone. A dog barks from
the window of a moving pickup truck.

The barks grow near, grow far. The barks
tighten like a fist, relax like a hand.

I live in a place no light can reach,
deep within the sealed dome of my head.

Even the light that splashes behind my eyes
is soaked up by brain-sponge.

Deep within the center of my head,
the sound changes with the act of listening.

Deep within the riddle I have become,
no answers, only the sound of questions,

dripping like notes of water, humming
like a tree-knot filled with bees,

a symphony that swells but never
crescendoes, building, building.

4/4/10

The Pit Boss' Wife

You drink like you don't
want to live, like living
is a stone you were made
to swallow, a coal in your
belly, to be doused but 
never put out. Once I ran
through the milkweed to find
you, catching pods in my hand.
The sea, at its depths, remains
motionless, while a storm
blows the surface to hell.
No light. No prism. You settle
like dust into darkness,
and once I arrived, it was
too late to know, in the flash
of the storefront glare,
just where, when you went,
that stone in you had gone.

4/6/10

Love Song to the Twentieth Century

Out of the notes of a blue lip-chatter,
the windows spatterd an unknown brown,
we sashay, in our way, through flattery
while outside the tigers keep grumbling.

Come to my bosom, two-headed hope,
I will suckle you proudly and long,
while the wind in the curling juniper
tells stories of the unforgiven past.

Let us poke the night with a sharp stick,
make breathing holes in the jam jar,
and then when they drop in a leaf for us,
let us eat the poison stem through.

The mechanics of living cause friction,
the swinging of joints wears them down.
Outside, the cicadas are pulsing, blue
current threshing the heat's bright sheen.

This is it! The pineapple grenades rain
into the marbled pits of tar and mud,
while the suckling piglets under shelter
huff and grunt, and hoof the haydust air.

Beyond disgust, the smooth whitel metal
of contentment, the well-rosined woods
of character, grooved by the millner's
daughter, before she went blind on love.

4/13/10

Gravity for Poets

Because they thought the stars might fall,
tugged to earth in a final arpeggio
of shattered glass and cosmic fission,
I had to sit them down, recluse after
recluse in a sober row, the formalist,
and the parabolic beatnick, arranged
like variables (since variable they were)
knowing more of paisley apricots than algebra,
veering dreamers grating on my migraine nerves,
while I gesticulate like mudras, fingering
chalk and physics, pawing the obscure air,
reminding myself of Whitman's "learned" man.

The fabric of the universe is as loose as
varnished wicker, soft as mesh, the rafters
parting slowly as layer upon layer of silver
silt comes pouring out, stardust from above.
Here is your vengance, Newton, as I rifle
my enchanted hair, and the surrealist stifles
a giggle, offending the narrarive poet's
quiet composure, and then the spitballs
streak like a meteor shower, unguided
by any principle, a storm of cosmic anarchy,
tipped off, no doubt, by some self-proclaimed
poet from the new avant garde. Let them riot.

Let them float off into space, for all I care.
To a poet, a theory of physics is as dull
as a riddle, obtuse as a dusty bottle, good
for clever titles, strange conceits, but pliable.
I flick a wad of moistened paper from my tomes,
collect my thoughts, snatching the disobedient
ones from where they hover in mid-air, erase
the board, since blank makes as good an ending
as it does a start, and, in a gesture of
deference, the narrative poet opens the door,
and once outside I hop up to click my heels,
suspended just a moment before I fall.

4/14/10

Poets for Gravity

It angers us,
let's make that clear--
parabolic flight,
magnetic trains, riding
a layer of air.

Whatever doesn't stick
when set upon the ground,
tugged back to Earth,
sober as a tarantula,
heaving along on hairy legs,
whatever defies the law,
faults physics, hovers
in the rafters. 
                Gravity is
the laying on of hands,
persistent light press
from above, saying, "be just,
veer not above the heights
of conscience, into vertigo."

Fruitcake, a Recipe

Tug the recluse,
rustle the silt,
grate each acre,
veering your paw
through algebra,
layer each address
with sober arpeggios,
creating fission
for the tarantula.
Add parabolic
apricots, eschew
heaving, mesh in
varnish, lemons,
wicker to a fault.
Paint the rafters
paisley, sign-of-
the-cross, seven
times, like cutting
off traffic, fold
in parallax. Bake.
Slice. Eat.

Self-Portrait in Three Sentences

I am scared. I am tired. I am lonely.
Three sentences never to put in a poem.

Three states so perfectly ordinary, so
plainly said, they will never transcend.

I am scared that the windows will melt,
like a popsicle in the desert heat,

riding bikes red-faced around the block
until Jenni's mom is done with the needles

and men, and can let her come back in.
I am tired of roast beef sandwiches,

driving past city limits, through rows
of bone-headed cows, hoofs sunk in shit,

"the smell of money" wafting for miles.
I am lonely especially with friends,

reading Mrs. Dalloway in a cafe far
from a landscape long since burned,

my new pals the gorgeous atheists
making fun of the Catholic mothers

who fed me posole and homemade tortillas,
reminded me that you can't eat books.

4/20/10

thrice clapping in the bell of truth,
trifecta, trigonometric, tertiary.

"I Was Born to Small Fish."
-from a mis-translated line by Pablo Neruda

4/23/10

They fill the stream with dashes,
clot in dark collections by the bank,
a clutch of minnows sprayed
into life, like torpedoes
from a pregnant submarine.

And I am born unto them,
child of the sardine, goldfish,
pollywog--whatever can swim
in a thimble, dodge change
flipped into a fountain
for luck.

          I am not the son
of the marlin, the sturgeon--
the sunfish, around whom
jellyfish revolve like planets.
The smallest of fish
is sufficent to be my mother.

I am from this line of stream-
swimmers, gulf-swimmers, fish
at the mercy of eddies
and wakes, schooling together,
and bursting apart, confusion
and numbers our only defense,
this line, this arc--

hundreds flashing
through a shaft of light--
I call them "family" and
"comrades," call them
my fish, small fish, birthright.

Semiotics of a Hobo Heart

Take shelter, my vagabond 
heart, read carefully
the hobo signs etched 
on the doorframes and mantles:
a trident for devilish deeds,
two planets colliding
for an unseen event,
a childlike rat with 
an asterisk of whiskers
to warn you of vermin,
scratched in the rust
on an I-beam overhead.

A Windmill Makes A Statement

The Split Up

A gap is torn between us, divorce
is a shallow force that shears
sunshine into apocalypse, words leave
my lips at my own risk, and are jotted
down in wax, but I digress, I find
the planetary test is in this tax
upon our rubble, the quick riposte
to our once daring cuddle, flashing
stroke of luck, this party of two
become an acre divided, and here is
your sheep, and there is mine--we even
divide the rats, the leaks, we find
some crumbs to sqabble over, and rust,
that precious dust, that tells us
once our love was made of shining metal.

5/18/10

Soft Animal
	(with apologies to Mary Oliver)

The cat is soothing herself, smoothing
the long fibers of fur with her barnacle
tongue as I tap my foot in rememberance
of a once-barely-heard tune. I write
too much about my cat, the only safe
object remaining to love, and writing
is a way of smoothing my own tangled 
ruff. And so we define "companionable"
in this way better than anything on
the shelf between two bookends, better
than oil on water, catching flame, or
cheese and chalk, so often confused
by hungry children new to school.
I am a taxpayer, though I only think
of it once per year. I am a father,
though my child is gone. And what to
the cat? Owner? Owned? When I get up,
she will curl into to the crevice
made by my animal body in the couch.

Avuncularia
	(advice to a new nephew)

Young man, at the risk
of pretention, let me say
something to you
in all this sunshine
about the wax
that coats our mercenary
lives, and how even daylight
can't melt it. You are
a party to something in-
describable now, the circulation
of blood from your ancestors,
buildings that loom up
from rubble, all zoos
being safe, but uninhabitable.

Let me say the first diaspora
was of the beetles
who tunnel the pressed dark 
earth, outnumbering us,
the sound of their pincers
clacking. You will learn
this is not important,
and learn that the scores
of our teams and their teams
facing off on the wet grass
matters as though you were there.
Do not believe everything
that is framed in a box,
or printed in columns on paper.

At the risk
of sounding ungrateful,
I say dig in,
to where the earthworms
knot like spaghetti,
and find for yourself which
bars are loose, and what
surfaces are prone to
scratching. Lick the wallpaper,
If you want to know 
what the printed fruits 
there taste like,
and remember the cells 
of your own free body
congregated just here for a reason
as fit as a falling leaf,
as right as the weather.

5/27/10

Sydney Suburb After Rain

The rain glossed the streets,
and the street cars buffed them,
and the daylight rang down
vengefully against the tarmac,
brandishing the white
of summer and snowblindness.
Small children crossed
at the corners, casting
the shadows of their small
dark bodies like moving prints.
But nothing was still, inside
the cell wall of the glass
cafe, or out with the smells
of the township. Once, I, too
avoided the the punch
of a fast-moving ball,
the indecipherable looks
of girls on the sidelines,
as even now they are playing
in the wet school yard
across the street. Some
are learning to become
a part of everything, while
others learn the art of appearing
to be in the game. Again the chirp 
of the crosswalk's electric parrot,
and the prams and bikes press 
forward, each child connected upward 
to a parent or teacher's hand.
And now the play yard clears,
revealing the tar-black pools,
the picnic benches, brick halls
with barred blue windows evenly spaced.
The damp marks are receding,
returning the town's ground surface
to its regular dark patina,
and the crowds are collecting
at the corners, waiting to cross
the now unremarkable street.

5/29/10

In Praise of That Which Turns

Alone in the public private of a loud cafe,
it seems to me I could be happy with myself,
if only I could remain here, among these people
graciously ignoring me, and watch the women
with umbrellas and flowers coned in newsprint
making their way in the outside world, alone
in a quiet and less cheerful way, except
for the roses, which fix the scene in a blaze,
as a single, well-arranged idea can make
reading in public a sweet, inward delight.
I imagine them all coming across their names
in the newspapers, and with it an explanation
for why they have had to struggle, and howcome
happiness is something they feel they can claim,
but not fully, having appetite still though
the meal is finished, and wondering always
if they should have ordered the other dish.
I imagine their mouths turning up perceptibly
at the moment each one discovers this writing,
the perfect words laid down--for the workman,
and student, and bored girl at the counter,
leafing through the gloss of a magazine,
thumbing the newsprint for tragedies,
the textbook on finance, at once aware
and oblivious to what is there and who
is watching, and how a phrase might turn
and turn like a mobile in the mind.

5/30/10

We Find Ourselves Together

You are reading this now on the train
as it speeds past Barcelona, reading

each word in a different place entirely
than where I first punched them in, letter

by tappity letter, following my own thoughts
down to a recess in a life-full of sameness,

where the small dark eyes of squeaking creatures
stare back through the excruciating dark.

And just like that, we find ourselves together.
Just like that we have entered the unreachable,

through a rope-ladder made from the alphabet,
arranged like corkscrew strands of DNA.

Drill in. Push through the cracks in obviousness,
tapping the walls of your bedroom for hidden

doors, levering each book on the shelving--
trust nothing apparent, not even words.

And now you are riding an elevator full
of short businessmen in pressed dark suits,

deciphering the pattern of the backlit buttons,
which extinguish with each reached floor,

you learn how arrival can be as disappointing
as leaving somewhere you once thought to love.

We find ourselves together again, as the phrase
"dark squeaking creatures" makes you stifle

a giggle, looking down at the black eyes
of the minor capitalists, glittering

with fiscal compassion, unaware of the trap
door above them, morse code in the cables

squeaking, punching the buttons repeatedly
as though there were somewhere to go.

# # #

The Sunday girl resides in a half-lidded
cloud full of seed-beans and haystacks.

Do not disappoint her. She will not wake.
Fitted with levers and gears sufficient

to pull a strong dark espresso, take
your money and give back change, she is

competent in the ways of small talk, having
learned to swap stock phrases from cards.

6/1/10

At the Shops, on a Cloudy Day, Recalling Viking Lore

Faces come and go, lamenting the sun. None
have made their bosses happy enough, none
will make the clouds part by complaining.
Not even me, who carries his face outward
like a shield to venture forth, a shield
to come home upon, glinting in the daylight
or darkened like the sea after a storm.
How pliable is the metal of my face, how
unrelenting in its composure, the decorative
eyeballs dancing to catch the partnership
of another pair of eyes. Go and come,
go out again, into the imperturable weather,
still smelling of the raincoat's mud, still
sniffing for the fresh-baked bread of daylight,
emblem pinned to skyscape, round white sun.

6/3/10

A Brief History of Democracy

None of us has had enough success, none
been changed into a hero with a cape
by trophies, placques, or clanking medals.

We will never be the ones our parents wanted,
the ones the country needed in its unrecognized
time of crisis, our own selves unrecognizable.

Here are the faces of of the ordinary future--
a small boy waiting to cross the damp road,
all cheeks and glitter-globe eyeballs.

A sweet shop makes him happy, workmen on cranes
that resemble his toys, a bug in the gutter
drawn into the light by the rain's floodwater.

He deserves a cape and a mask, deserves the chance 
to right what he will discover--the legless man 
begging change outside the liquor store.

Who will listen to the sounds of his defeat?
Who will record the gorgeous light of the ideal?
None of us, coming and going, on the street.

6/4/10

Making Something of It All Down Under

In the right mood,
an ordinary day, populated
with small birds
and trivial people,
can contain the preternatural--
gutters flow
with dragon saliva,
tree limbs sway
against the wind,
and the cars, as they pass
wink headlights in recognition.

Something is afoot,
and not just pedestrians.
The dashed lines
on the street join together,
a long wound re-healing.
Bricks in the walls jostle
like a rugby scrum.
Even the windows want in,
fogging themselves opaque
with the breath
of an invisible breather.

Anything could happen,
but nothing, so it seems, 
really does. Instead the birds
pass secrets in their crack-
pecking, and the laundry
waves along the line,
practicing its dance routine.
And the citizens pass
the red post box, oblivious 
that just yesterday
the slot was facing the street.

Desdemona's Apology

Is my secret safe with you?
Come, let us sing to the mirror,

our luck is sweet, the bed
is made, the last sand drips

down the hourglass, and because
of this, therefore we rack

our minds for apologies, and if
what I have said is true, then

come, let us sing like sparrows,
my sweet black Orpheus, no matter

if truth begets apology, or sorrow
makes what's precious come to peril,

let us sift the sheets through our
fingers like shadows, repeating--

if what is true brings us sorrow,
if what sorrow brings is truth,

then sorrow and truth are cousins,
bonded like winter and spring,

and who can tell one from the other
in the dark bedchambers of night?

7/7/10

Poetic Theory

A poem undresses the mind. Undress it
carefully, with a lover's appreciation,
not so much for the cast-off blouse,
the lace and elastic, but for what
is revealed to the nighttime air,
panting, moisture rising to the skin 
of our thoughts, with nothing to hold
but soft bedclothes and shadows.

7/8/10

There is a beacon guiding my thoughts.
In the darkest places, still a light.

The rubble of our complexion, of
complicity and complexion.

Nothing has come of it
Nothing has come to pass

The needle of the second hand
bounces back almost imperceptibly,
stuttering the progress of time.

7/12/10

Elegy for the News
	whith a nod to John Lenon

I have used up all the good news, and who
will make some more? Braiding shock-words
and hope-words like dough for French bread.

I lash my faith to the mast of a boat
made from newsprint, bobbing out with ducks,
taking on pond water, which darkens the words.

Nothing good will come of this, the use
of news for flotation, because they need me
to sputter a little in the whirlpool's spiral.

Lenon said "time wounds" and he was right.
Who will learn to monopolize the air? A profit
can be turned on the out breath, let's try it.

John was a prophet when he wrote "War is over"
on billboards in the typeface of news. Sisyphus,
too, pushed a rock as round as the "o" in "over."

Children are news, as is grandma's passing.
The ink makes it official, like when the last 
seed of a dandelion departs, and you can wish.

I wish, and I wound. I am read all over.
Someone made up the news before it was news.

7/19/10

Include a few of: strain, undaunted, presential, pew, demur, dirty, relator, pause, naught, tend , mayor, sighting, slushy, senator, hoard

Adelphophagy
"The ingestion by a developing larva of other eggs or larvae from the same brood." -OED

Sometimes I Wonder What I Do

The hand that snaps the twig
from the branch is my hand, not tending
or pruning, but pausing
while walking to grab, and grasp,
and strain the bough thought-
lessly as I pass. I regard
the minor damage undaunted,
some juniper berries shook free
and crushed to a fibrous pulp,
and I do not demur to question
why my curling hand would hoard
up leaves, if it could, all along
the short path home--grabbing
sticks to smack the fenceposts
slats and picking the tight buds
of neighbor's roses, unrepentant,
my five fingers the whole city council,
judge, jury, and executioner,
and my thumb elected mayor, nay
senator, nay monarch, of the wax-leaf
picturesqueness, the presential
pride and hard-pew reverence
of well-kempt suburbs, sleepy
hamlets--the cow that knows
that grass belongs in the chambers
of her stomach, the goat that nibbles
tweed and rush and tarpaulin,
the gentle, absentminded anarchy
of plucking at the neighborhood
like fur for fleas, like cotton
for seeds, picking, and picking
the whole damn place apart.

7/20/10

Brother-eater, you are the one
who survived the jailhouse womb

by devouring the other inmates.
Two nurse shark will emerge

from a conceived brood of dozens,
kept apart in separate chambers,

feeding on their younger kin,
once free, the eldest lies in wait.

7/22/10

My colleague tells the story
of taping her younger sister

into a cardboard box, then denying
any knowledge of where she was.

Do sharks know that to be eaten
is terrifying and painful?

Or are they simply doing
what delights the teeth and jaw,

what delights the throat in passing,
and cools the stomach's furnace?

Brother-eater, you survived.
This much we can say for sure.

8/4/10

I do not recall what you sound like
after twenty-five years apart, only

being older, I wanted to do whatever
you did, eat your food, mimick your walk.

Half-brother, I am partways you,
mirror held at an angle, consumer

of similar resources: language, music,
film, art, estrangement, speckled hope.

But I was the one our father raised,
printing my flesh with his fins,

while a stepfather taught you to swim
through the kelp-dark waters.

8/5/10

Half-brother, brother-eater, son
of a separate womb, no less dangerous

than the open currents, deep down
that pulled us together, apart.

"I am responsible for some of her trauma,"
my workmate laughed, making it joke.

I have made a meal of suffering,
of you, of siblings real and imagined.

Brother-eater, we survived. We know.

8/16/10

Scenic View, Northern New Mexico

In the mountains above Taos,
the old gods still vie,
unattended, where a pine bough
will never make an olive branch,
a visit of churned clouds
to the monochrome blue
means Air and Water are at it again.

Hear the droop and plunk 
of a woodpecker's warning
as he rhapsodizes, upstanding
in the tops of trees like
beacon on the towers
of New York, Chiciago
a light to guide the thoughts
of airborne visitors, victims
of the legendary sweep of liquid
green, of naturalized blue,
of earth as red as a stew pot,
saying "come, come closer
to the ledge." 

               It is weakness
that keeps us behind the railing,
the excuse of an idling engine,
work shoes, sunscreen thinning,
victims of our sensibility.
We like you best behind glass,
O mother, like your war stories
better than the wars.

Scenic View, Northern New Mexico

In the mountains above Taos,
the old gods still vie,
unattended, where a pine bough
will never make an olive branch,
a visit of churned clouds
to the monochrome blue
means Air and Water are at it again.

Hear the droop and plunk 
of a woodpecker's warning
as he rhapsodizes, upstanding
in the tops of trees like
a beacon on the towers
of New York, Chicago
a light to guide the thoughts
of airborne visitors, victims
of the legendary sweep of liquid
green, of naturalized blue,
of earth as red as a stew pot,
saying "come, come closer
to the ledge." 

               It is weakness
that keeps us behind the railing,
the excuse of an idling engine,
work shoes, sunscreen thinning,
victims of our sensibility.
We like you best behind glass,
O mother, like your war stories
better than the wars.

                      We slide
edgewise along the railing,
spiritualize the scenic view,
merchandize the vistas on clay
pots and keychains, silver
bracelets specked in turquoise,
tawny tom-toms with beaded fringe.

We are legendary, here above it all.
And yet we lean a little further
than is prudent, tasting the pine-
scented air for clues, our mortality,
a rush of blood to the head.

8/23/10


Robert's assignment:

Also include a type of extreme weather or geological phenomenon (eg typhoon, volcano), and a composer's name.

Letter to John Keats

Pale, that is how we remember you,
a face narrowing into its nose
like whittled ivory, the span

of your life short, and unreturnable--
the snarling critics, whirligig
of industry, rusticated masonry

giving way to brick-on-brick,
unmodulated, a performance of math-
ematics straight to the steeple-top.

They built walls around you, John.
They built walls wherever you stepped.
Beethoven will outlive you, going deaf.

Who paints you bloodless as the snow?
A blizzard is not a still life, nor
is the "holiness of the heart's affections"

to be bricked-up neat or stuffed,
like a tissue, into a ruffled sleeve.
We perfume you in your grave.

They say you howled like an animal
the day your sick mother died.
Do not make like an angel, Johnny boy.

I want a man to understand who spit 
out his own blood from time to time.
I want a Keats who's mad at God

for behaving like the monarchy,
and angry at the monarchy,
for acting just like God. I want

to know you thought it wasn't fair,
and wrote when you were hungry
instead of asking friends for bread.

I want you, John, to churn the honeypot.
To tell me it was never done for them.
I want to see your cheeks go rosy red.

8/24/10

Double Agent

Each morning, I make myself up.
I make up what I like: oranges,
say, or rhubarb, how I will walk,
I make up my friends, and reasons
for friendship, make up my love
for collecting small cars, I make
up a me someone can relate to,
and try to keep the "facts" straight.
It's easy, like being a double-
agent, except that both countries
are you, and besides, the penalty
for treason isn't the chair anymore,
but a death much slower, inside.

# # #

Misplanted Affection

America, I used to be your biggest fan.
There's me in my Boy Scout khakis planting
flags beside the headstones, careful
not to let them droop to the dirt.
I should have planted flowers instead.
Their leaves refresh the air with oxygen.
Their petals fall and are eaten by bugs,
reminding us of where the petals came from,
and how dangerous it is to be alive,
in love with anything, red, white
or even the sky overhead, which can
not always stay the way it is, blue.

8/30/10

In the middle of my life, there was a tunnel.
It was lined with brick, and damp.

Today I am thinking of walls.
The soft sound of mason blocks
hoisted onto each other by sweat.

Our earliest endeavor was to separate
air from air, space from space.

Well-built, a wall is hard to climb.
Bombed and crumbling, it makes
a nuisance to the aims of suburban minds.

There is so much terrible poetry
going around these days.

9/5/10

What is to become of fill-in-the-blank?
Surely, someone with a flair for science
should know this answer by now. Will fill-
in-the-blank (or "Phil," as I call him,)
end up in the place where we left it?
The seargents are marching their men,
made of bootblack and gunmetal, canvass
in camoflauge, nothing exposed but teeth.
What is to come of the blank they trample?
What of the blank the artilery aims at?

9/14/10

Shall I Compare the Red-Eared Snapping Turtle to an M1 Abrams Tank?

So long as men can breathe, or eyes can see, / So long lives this, and this gives life to thee.


The turtle is baptised daily in his bowl.
In a blizzard of algae he swims,
prying the mush with flippered hands,
then clambers the bark of a sideways trunk,
and strikes a pose, his nose held high,
like the canister of a gas-mask, but he
is sucking in the chemical air, stifling
the sound of a bourgeoise yawn, or is it
rifling the wind for the smell of dinnertime
salmon, bubbling in a pan? Our man turtle,
armored and ready, drives his numb self
everywhere, callous to the tapping fingers,
oblivious to the lamplight on his back,
he can sleep anywhere, be ready to strike.
He is less lovely than a war machine, though
some would say more temperate

Shall I Compare the Red-Eared Snapping Turtle to an M1 Abrams Tank?
	After Shakespeare's Sonnett #18

The turtle is baptised daily in his bowl.
In a blizzard of algae he swims,
prying the mush with flippered hands,
then clambers the bark of a sideways trunk,
and strikes a pose, his nose held high,
like the canister of a gas-mask. Is he sucking
in the chemical air, stifling the sound 
of a bourgeoise yawn, or maybe rifling 
the wind for the smell of dinnertime salmon, 
bubbling in a pan? Our man turtle, armored 
and ready, drives his numb self everywhere, 
callous to the tapping fingers, oblivious 
to the lamplight on his back, he can sleep 
anywhere, and be ready to strike. He is 
less lovely than a war machine, though
some would say more temperate. So long
as he can breathe, and swim, and see,
he plods an armored living with a smile.

# # # # #

The bruise goes indigo
in yellow light. The light
is mild, a friendly beacon,
a duck in waxy feathers.

Here come the needles.
The white faces worn like masks.
Was that a comet just now?
Or the burning dust we breathe?

Nothing suggests fire out here.
The little glass pipes clacking.
Tell us how long we have to live.
Make it quick, a painless prick.

# # #

The tunnel in the middle of my life
was lined with brick, and damp, the sound
of mason block on block, the mortar's slop
gives back with every step as I pass through.
I hear also the train-sound in the track.
It is distant, beneath a tremor, the drip
of weeping moss above the arch drowns out
the sound of pleasant conversation
from passenger cars long-past.
