Front teeth (old or young)
Noon

Telecommunications

They are unspooling cable in the midday sun,
passing the copper through gloved hands
like currency, the vinegared sweat beading
as the siren-less yellow light blinks on.

A postcard from Nicaragua is held down
to the truck's wheel well by a metal canteen.
In the photo a woman with a manequin's smile
shows jewelry and a torquise-handled knife.

The orange safety vest flaps open in the warm
desert breeze like a pair of wings, or curtains
framing the man's chest like a portal. Perched
on the dash, a large plastic Jesus reveals 

his anatomically-correct, glowing heart.
His companion in the cab has a signature smile, 
missing two front teeth. But unlike a child, when 
he blows out candles, he no longer wishes them back. 

10/19/10

They are threading cable into the new world,
passing it down into the hands of their inheritors,
like climbers on a sheer face hitched together.
He pulls an uncle from the spool, then a cousin,

ushering them down the guy-wire into the dark.

10/20/10

The man in the cab is snoring now, and the woman
on the postcard starts to sway her hips.

Heat rising from the asphalt makes the horizon
shimmer like glass. The dead unspool alongside
the living, chasing the gleaming cable into the shade.

Who can tell where this will end? The junction box?
Or perhaps it, too, has become enchanted, a home
to banana leaves, and crystal skulls, witchcraft

10/21/10

that makes it possible to project the human voice,
and the moving image of a dimly lit ancestor,
from a screen in North America to a screen

in the jungle, desert, or beach--to see, as if haunted,
the faces of those you left behind, saying, like the radio's
song, "one day, one sweet day, we'll be together."

10/25/10

Let us go down to the water's edge,
to watch the sand-birds working,
like infants work at learning a task,
with the purposefulness of survival.

We have time, and units of time,
can measure the distance to shore.
We have come a long way from pecking
the sand, and even wear shoes nowadays.

Let's take off our shoes to feel the sand,
and spread our toes like webbing.

I am the finger pressed to your lips
to stop the chatter, and begin the love-

making. Nothing makes sense but the shadows,
touching under the curtains hem, brushing

slow across the varnished wood, each
other, held in this halfway place

by an absence of wind, as even we
are defined by the space between us.

10/26/10

to defend us against the darkness.

hair spread on the pillow like wings,

these are currencies we gather,
growing rich on what we trade.

11/4/10

Use several of these: fork, splayed, jungle, tune, fox, paradox, bonfire, overthrow, armistice, perspective, pumpkin, cavalier, side-step, pop, snowball, lunge, nutty, monochrome, droop, plunk, edge, upstanding, rhapsody.

Include something fragrant or smelly.

Optionally steal a few lines from a previous poem that never quite shaped up to be finished.

Prodigal

You come to me, cavalier, and soaked in gin,
waggling your pumpkin of a head as you warble 
a tune, lunge and droop a puppet's dance, breath
a monochrome of reek and rancor, smiling
through those teeth. I loved you once, old fox,
sly upstanding mutineer, your gait a perpetual
side-step, throwing snowballs in the bonfire
just to hear the plunk and pop of matter-meet-
anti-matter. We were friends, under armistice,
two halves of a paradox, splayed on the floor,
in a room with no furniture, passing the bottle,
and swapping lines, and lies, and the names
of girls into rhapsody and blackness. The dream-
less sleep of criminals. You come to me again,
patting my pockets for change, eyeing what I've
made of myself, and all that you passed on to me.
Well, dear brother-in-arms, camerado mio, well.
I have grown competent with small talk, and can
fend with it like a foil, as you jab and jab
at the shared disasters I keep behind my sternum.
We are older now. The windows are painted shut.

# # #

Love Tokens

I am the finger pressed to your lips
to stop the chatter, and begin the love-

making. Nothing makes sense but the shadows,
touching under the curtain's hem, brushing

slow across the varnished wood, each
other, held in this halfway place

by an absence of wind, as even we
are defined by the space between us.

An elbow becomes a knife in lamplight,
to defend us against the darkness.

It is late. The vinegar of our skin,
hair spread on the pillow like wings,

these are currencies we gather,
growing rich on what we trade.

11/6/10

Not a Love Poem
	for Valerie

You don't have to be in a good mood to write a poem.
Let us, therefore, write a bad-mood poem, right now.

But with each passing line, I find my mood improving.
Write quickly! While there is venom in the fingertips.

Nothing about the lamplight interests me. Not the way
a stripe of light from a cracked lampshade can lash

a spark to the window-sill, reminding us of coalfire,
the trills of light from fireworks left burned

into our eyelids once the sky has cooled down black.
What else, pray tell, uninterests, or even aggravates me?

I care nothing for the shush of passing traffic, the boxed-
and-filed lives processing--a slow sound for rapid movement!--

the outward monastic solemnity, the inside blaring radio
tunes and gossip shows, pretending to treat our politics.

There is so much in this world I care so little about, so much
I refuse to miss, as I cling to the vestiges of crankiness.

But you I will miss, who look bright in bad weather, even
mine, and you will one day be the subject of this poem.

For now, let the swish and flash of all-I-can-not-hate
stand for the one I love, seated beside me in the sun.

When they turn the volume dial of my ears down to a whisper,
and my lids descend like window blinds one final time,

I will search out the sound and spark of you to cheer me,
my dry thought in a downpour, another name for love.

11/8/10

Mini-Matins

I write a lot of morning poems.
Some people pray before bed.
Me, I ask a poem to keep me
as I head into the daylight.

"Nothing will come of nothing."
Wise words from a holy fool.
We imitate and amplify, calling
our own small part "original."

Let me imitate the mirror-man.
Let me shout beautiful whispers.
Come, feel how rough my hands,
calloused by a life of leisure.

11/9/10

Rejection Slip

I'm sorry your dog died. Or your mother. Or your belief in the world as a safe, sane place to live. My stamping a red "rejection" on the tear-soaked page you sent me is hardly the response you deserve. Find someone who likes you in spite of this poem, who sees the poem you will write twenty years from now, on the selfsame topic, that brings us down to our knees. Never let that person go. Cling like a barnacle. Cleave also to the belief (which is true) that because you can be hollowed out, as with an ice-cream scooper, by the poems in dogeared volumes on your shelves, that someday, someday, you will have that effect on another. Today it is not this poem. Today it is not me. Though I refer to your piece by its assigned number when delegating to a subeditor the task of contacting you, consider this now my most personal attempt at reaching back. I'm sorry. I live here, too. This place overwhelms my instruments also, pegging the needles of sorrow and beauty on the gauge at the center of my chest. I decided, like you, long ago, to learn the device's more subtle measures, no matter how often it surged and blew. Keep learning. Note by note. In the future, I will not have to look for you to know you have revised your fate. You will send this message back to me. It will not bring me comfort, even as now I am sure you are not consoled. But maybe this will encourage you to shovel coal into anger's furnace, and ride upon that heat to a better poem. I do not like this any more than natural disasters. Yet I must believe that Nature loves us in her way. Go write. Go write some more. Be gorgeous, despite it all.

11/10/10

Rainbow Thoughts

I flounder around in search of new forms. I am mackrel.
Holy carp. Even the most insane sometimes make sense.

Tell me you love me in a language I don't understand.
Tell me this rickety plank is really a dance floor.

I have been throwing darts at an empty picture frame.
Yes, our hate is transferrable. Even sober we lie.

The wind is no friend of mine. But the water will give
me a reference. I worked hard to make the molecules stick.

Believe in the glass partition, even fogged with the breath
of the ones you could not save, it may act like a prism.

Here come the rainbow thoughts...

11/16/10

Procrastination, 6AM

The last thing I do is write.
I wash dishes first, make coffee,
check all messages real and electric
from friends, acquaintances, and those
I spy upon. I use the toilet 
twice, when I didn't need it
the first time. I sit. I contemplate
navel lint, which I spelled
just now as "naval," like boats,
and suddenly, I'm on to something,
gray froth cresting the bow,
the waves of my soft belly,
and the bellybutton, that whirl-
pool made by my tummy's Charybdis,
ancient little lapsarian scar,
reminding us we're all cut off,
adrift at sea, untetherd from the
mother vessel, rowing hard against
distraction, and the word for tipping
over is "capsize," origin unknown,
perhaps corrupted from the Spanish
word for "head," as boats go down
like slow divers into the dark.

Use several of these: fork, splayed, jungle, tune, fox, paradox, bonfire, overthrow, armistice, perspective, pumpkin, cavalier, side-step, pop, snowball, lunge, nutty, monochrome, droop, plunk, edge, upstanding, rhapsody.

Old Man in Winter

I am the last of those I thought I knew,
a jungle full of drumming at my heart.
I held my breath until my tune turned blue,
side-stepping paradox my lonely art.
I held my breath to sound the blue-edged note,
against the monochrome of winter skies
the leaves upon the snow made little boats,
and witnessed all the snowmen's slow demise.
I held my breath into a rhapsody,
the droop and plunk of snowballs hitting snow
overwhelmed the inner drums' cacophony
and overthrew the me I thought I knew.
Tonight they'll make a bonfire in the woods.
I'll smell the smoke, and hold my breath for good.

11/22/10

Pilgrim's Progress

The doorframe of my great-grandmother's house
hung below my eyebrows at sixteen, testament
to generations of marrying taller men,
and feeding them like plough-horses, heaping
the meats on platters, vats of potatoes,
until one Thanksgiving morning, the oak-legged
table just collapsed. The story makes us
who we are--farm people who maybe thought
that farmers, being the source of food, could
never themselves go hungry--like my wife
wanting to be a nurse, since she thought
the ones who gave the injections never had
to feel the prick and sting themsevles.
It is good to be the source of something.
Everyone called great-grandma "mom," even 
her younger siblings, whom she raised along
with the goats and egg-laying chickens.
"More corn?" she'd ask, "More pancakes?"
shovelling rice and beans onto our plate, 
the words behind the words proclaiming, 
"Here, here, you will never go hungry here."

What Was The Name of that Movie We Saw?

Their love is like a photograph,
shoes touching on the street corner--
absentminded placeholder for what
their bodies want to do. The marquee
is illegible through the raindrops
on my windscreen, expressionless
white halo pouring down its color-
less light. Gusts of wind undress
their need for tenderness, rain
swarming around their feet, two
candles tipping closer, blending 
the yellowish light from their face.
Here we insert a bookmark
into history, a pause against
ordinary time. It is the moment
d'Andre kissed me in the schoolyard,
the first time the Grand Canyon
emerged into view through the trees.
It is a wink they will remember
when the movie's plot has faded,
the footprints of two travelers
in the already-melting snow.

Here's an assignment for January, 2011:

Write a two part poem, whether in two stanzas or in two sections. The whole poem cannot be more than 28 lines plus title (length of two sonnets).

The overall theme is, "What's it about?" But don't ask more than two questions.

First part, use most of these 14 words or variations thereof: 

*bread, *tax, *breast, *suit, studio, *mouse, *roar, *card, *hungry, *guitar, *textile, *cardiac, *fraction, *ticket

plus 

a tool or implement 

and 

a specific place.

Second part, use most of these 14 words or variations thereof: 

*cover, *brain, *ghost, *broadside, estate, *rival, *radio, *dagger, *outpost, *mop, *wrinkle, *chip, *surge, *glow.

The words for the first part came from the NY Times of Dec. 17, 2010. The words for the second part came from the Dec. 18, 2010 NY Times. You are encouraged to make some of the monosyllabic words into multisyllabic words, so as to evade what could be an over-reliance on the feel of "masculine" endings.

Views from the Atrium

I. Taxation

Play me a tune on that hungry guitar
it suits me like bread for a mouse.
Fractured or stale, the crumbs are
the ticket to ride, and who cares?

The notes are a tax on my heart.
Who will arrest me for cardiac
back-pay, garnishing blood
from the roar of the ventricle?

Beneath my breast, they are milling 
steel or textiles to the sound of 
a drum. They are turning the cards, 
swirling the leaves of the tea. 

Here is the chamber of secrets--
most internal, most inland of agencies.

I will swab clean the outpost, then leave.
The mouse is still strumming a hungry guitar.
And the broad side of hope needs repair.

The mouse wrinkles up his mouse-nose
as a fresh surge of saline rains down.
The Beatles emerge from the warm static
glow, singing blackbird

III. Woodsman

Songbird, out of tune. Whistle me sideways
from the slit of your patina beak.
I know the words to stop the rain, but sputter 
like dry toast, throat-caught, thinking them.

Hack away at the limbs of the outstretched,
hum to the sound of the wet saw blade.
A yellowish footprint of sap will remember
us both to these amber-bathed hills.

We are snow-blind without any snow. How 
intimate to listen in the atrium of leaves,
the branches of the cage bathed in gold.
Hear the woodblock sound and sound one note.

Musicians of the chamber, scratching twigs,
scraping space to make the silence real.

Second part, use most of these 14 words or variations thereof: 

*cover, *brain, *ghost, *broadside, *estate, *rival, *radio, *dagger, *outpost, *mop, *wrinkle, *chip, *surge, *glow.

The Mouse Responds

Here is the dagger that chips
at my brain, the ghost at my
sleeve, the wrinkled surmise:
I cover up my nakedness, under
the lamplight's glow, my rivals
surge with jealousy, radio
for help. But who will mop
the brow of your troubled estate
when the broadside cannons fire?
At the outpost of my thoughts,
a bird is circling the dead.
Bend me now around you.
Like the sea to a starboard injury,
rush in with no pretense.

1/25/11

This is the last day for rum and goofy grins,
the end of the road for the clunker we drove

Letter From My Migraine

I got a letter from my migraine yesterday.
The penmanship was impeccable.

It mentioned how things just weren't
working out, me with the drive

to win all the prizes now gone.
It had found some nice neurotic

who could keep on working long after
her eyesight went, would vomit

into the sink, rinse once, then go
back to writing that grant proposal,

typing blind against the deadline.
So I gave them my blessing

to cohabit--the happy couple--
but offered to let him visit,

once in awhile, for old times' sake.
After all, we'd been together

since the concussion, gulping pills
and moaning, begging to die.

I tell myself I can still enjoy
a darkened room, alone, still place

a cold cloth over my eyes, not bulging,
or backed with needles, just two eyes.

I imagine coins laid on the lids 
of the dead, who cannot feel the coldness.

What is life without pain, or the threat
of pain? It is not quite death, I know.

I will not pretend to miss him,
but I sign my response "with love."

1/28/11

It is easier to steer the pen when moving. Starting 
the flow of ink is the hardest part. Fractured
or whole, the page has a way of telling. Saying
what it means, the globe goes on spinning. Hinging
on the thought that we all might die. Mortified
by the pen, which is stationery. Still.

I need to read more, clearly.

1/31/11

White Pigeons

are not doves. They do not stand
for peace, but flock and swoop
above my head in the blue before dawn.

They are a liquid in the air,
elastic, bunching and swarming
like oil drops on water.

I do not want to know the physics.
I do not want to make a documentary.
I stand and watch them ripple like a flag.

The soldier inside me wants to salute.
The prophet takes it for a sign.
They double-back, like a bedsheet, folded.

And then they dip below the tree line,
leaving their absence to hang in the air.
I never wished they were more than they were.

A morning dove now sounds his call to prayer.
A red-tailed hawk lords over mousing fields.
I have heard some call all pigeons "wing'ed rats."

But these were different, bred to home,
which means that they were practicing,
and work never seemed as elegant as this.

Tie a message to my foot. I will assume
my place in the aerial formation. Let me 
be a single snowflake in that flurry.

Over the Atlantic

It is late, and she is dark,

We are the only visible stars,

Our ethereal overhead captain

of "rough air" ahead. Seatbelts
click in succession, like a gun-
drill salute. I have seen rough
air whip the water to a frenzy--

Corpus Christi
    for S.P.

That visit, I slept in your princess bed,
which would have fit me perfectly
if only my legs were removable.

Little cousin, you spent most of your
time on the "naughty stair," ostensibly
thinking about what you had done--

but I could tell that really
you were thinking about what prank
or stunt to pull in front of me next.

I was mostly done with puberty.
You were mostly done with diapers.
We each felt older in our way.

Your father built a rowboat in the garage.
I saw how much a man could love smooth wood
when it is all that is between him and the sea.

Planed and lacquered like an instrument,
I wondered whom it might one day save,
and from what doldrums or shipwreck.

As it was getting dark, we'd load you in
to the back seat and go driving by the docks
to fill your dreams with waves and ocean sounds.

I'd gather you up, still sleeping,
long before I knew what fatherhood meant,
and place you in the manger of the couch.

2/28/11

Evidence of Maternal Love

1. Powdered bleach in a metal cylinder, whose holes would clog with the rising steam from the sink. 
2. A long-handled mop with gray strands like a stuffed doll's hair.
3. Yellow and brown linoleum, laid in squares.
4. A blush brush as soft as a cloud should be.
5. Pinto beans spread on the counter, sorting out the bean-sized rocks.
6. Afterward, plucking out bay leaves from the pot, so no one would choke.
7. The small construction enterprise each time she made lasagna.
8. Coils of the electric stove fading from red to black.
9. The smell of pine on the floor just after mopping.
10. Books so old they smelled like dust.
11. Garden tools dulled in a single day's frenzy.
12. A plastic plate with separate compartments, so my sister's food wouldn't touch.

3/3/11


Use as many as you can of the following:

zombie, overthrow, side-step, snowball, saltire, nutty, olive branch, bash, particle, soil, hours, shades, belongings, backdrop

Also include: something about money, coins, or currency; a color; a reference to someone you admire.


Soldier at the Tomb of Alexander

They are laying the coins on your eyes,
for passage across the river, into death.

Your hands could never hold an olive branch,
zombie-like expression, face a pale shade.

So this is your overthrow, unable to
side-step mortality, and a sarcophagus

will be your war tent, heaped with 
your belongings, raised up from the soil.

What good will they do for you now?
Better to bury a snowball in the ground,

or the nutty scent of almonds, a particle
of sand from the banks of the Akropotamos--

or lay you out saltire in the sun, to be
picked at like Prometheus, down to bone.

May I call you "Alexander?" Will you bash
my helmet in, barefoot crush a scorpion?

The backdrop of your life was always fire.
Is Persepolis still burning in your mind?

The hour is getting late, and by now
you must want rest. I have paid

what some would call respects, two coins,
but forgot my tears in the village at home.

3/4/11

The Wood Rooster

He sits there, comb at attention,
wings at his sides, beak curved
downward in mild disdain. Proud 
rooster made of wood on our piano.

Above him is a painting of the muse.
I want to remember this place as it 
is, dark orange like the insides 
of a pumpkin. Peeling red door.

Here, we said, "we will be happy."
And sometimes we were. Sliding my
arms around your waist as you cook
sausages in the morning, fry eggs.

Cradled on three sides by artsy
neighbors, all of them with dogs
who know we can't resist the trick
of nudging under our hands for pats.

I am thinking of the lyric, "how can
I miss you if you won't go away?"
Soon we will be gone, and let
nostalgia touch up our paintings.

We will pine for the sun in the trees,
the balmy winters that feel like spring.
We are going to a place that is gray.
But will take the wood rooster with us.

3/5/11


A thing of beauty is a pain in the ass.

Beans in Tins

A paen to beans: they are orange,
and sweet, and lovely. They wash down
with tea as though made to be washed
down with tea and only tea. If the whites
of your eggs are bland, and white, they
will make them sweet, and yellowy.
You can dip your chips in them if you
like, and if not, why not? They exist
in a suspension not unlike ketchup,
exist as our primordial predecessors,
beans are the protean beginning of
something great; on other planets
I have it on good report they envy
us our tins of beans, or would if they
ever made contact. Really, what have
you been missing all these years?
Did I mention how well they go with tea?

the page and coughs. Turns again.

21 May 2011

British Matches

On the matchbox, there is a child,
frown like a downturned slice of melon
flames decorating her thin stick arms.

A single red wing arcs in salute
by the epigram: "Fire Kills Children."
Above it is the royal coat of arms.

I wonder why only children. Fire kills
trees, and adults, too--anything alive,
once enfolded in that wing will soon

hear the "shhh" of death's librarian.
My father taught me games with matches.
We used to make rockets by wrapping

the match head in foil, propping it
on a bent paperclip, then tickling
the shiny underside with a flame.

It would fizz into the air, dangling
smoke like a dancer's scarf. I went
through whole booklets that way.

Now we are six thousand miles apart,
and the pale light of an unfamiliar
place lights up this new-to-me warning.

[Vietnam war?]

In the absence of photojournalism,
the idea of a child on fire
is as cartoonish as a queen

whose family symbols are national
symbols, propped up by a unicorn
in a place that will never be home.

# # # # #

Points North

It is always trains, and us
making our way through the neighborhoods
to catch them.
Evening lays out its proposal
in the fluid song
of birds and blackbirds.

We hear only the clatter
of a train approaching
from the wide space in front of it,
then threading the tunnel
like a parable:
make straight the way
of the nighttime train
as it casts its darkness before us.

28 May 2011

"Experiencing Minor Delays Due to a Person Under a Train."

The windows of the train are mirror-black,
ghosting the backs of their heads,
when suddenly the tunnel wall vanishes,
and a parallel train, lit with people,
presses next to us, at equal speed.

For a moment, both trains seem as still
as the turning world, the surprise
of a long-lost twin, the other train
we might have taken (in the mind
of a physicist sipping tea like a god.)

Earlier, coming down the steel slats of escalator,
a voice spoke into the ceramic chamber below,
apologising for "minor delays due to a person
under a train." The escalator kept ticking,
setting  us down like our first day of school.

The businessmen straddled their briefcases
under the glow of the sign for "way out," 
and when the first train came, it was 
already full, but we toed up to the edge 
of the platform, dutifully, hoping 

a few of the bodies might exit, hoping
the train was not bound for a station
guarded by dogs and by razor wire,
that we might sit and read a newspaper,
instead of poetry, philosophy, history

books, where one train arrives early,
and another emerges dripping in blood,
the Polish soldiers who clung to the top
having misjudged the height of the tunnel,
passenger windows glazed stained-glass red,

celebrating the torment of God-in-flesh,
the power of light to refract or reflect,
as even our thoughts go on branching,
slipping down diverging switch-tracks,
conductor-less, bound for the terminal.

13 June 2011

It is meant to overwhelm you, the scale of the place.
These are not my people, though they look like me.
A small squirrel gathers nuts in a field.

We have never been as lonely

Whoever you are, I want

she was putting on

She was so excited.
I wanted to meet you
myself.

# # #

Rats of the London Underground
    Recent estimates put the population of rats in the London Underground railway system at half-a-million.

Every day, I look out for you--the movement
of shadow under a rail, along the ribbed
wall of cabling that spans the tunnel.

Some estimate you are half-a-million strong,
rats of the London Underground, which 
sounds like a call to unionize, and see

how fast a childish fascination can turn
political? I only wanted your soft, coal-
black presence in my commuter's life.

I only wanted to know how you coexist
on a mouse-wide bridge of cable, under
a humming metal track, when the train

announces its arrival by pushing the wind
into your conical face, and do you suppress
the urge to flee, and freeze as it goes past?

This is what it is like to live with a god.

15 June 2011

A wife stiffens at the late-night key in the lock.
A soldier salutes his orders into no-man's land.

And you, soft sickly creatures, born
in darkness, live invisible and shit in places 
that angels, on pin heads, could never go.

The train squeaks to a halt in the charcoal tunnel.
The driver reassures us over the speaker. I listen 
for you at the window, like a mother at a crib.

20 June 2011

Sunday at the Beach

Let the wind heckle us, tossing our hair.
We are together, with no plans, licking

gelati and watching the sea otters pelt
the stones with abalone and tug out the meat.

Soon enough, mankind will overtake us,
a galley of slaves rowing in the nearby boat,

shipmaster curling his whip into little spirals,
watching for the first signs of protest.

For now, we are swimming free, offspring
of an antique need for salt-pricked air,

we prattle along the dock, conversations overlapping,
filling the breeze with sound to stretch the day.

11 July 2011

On Hearing the News

Write angry. Write from the bowels of the gravedigger's
trench. Write anything that mimics the clack of bone
on bone, the click of life's clock running out and out.

We are at sea. Look how the gulls chime and the low sails
flap out in counterpoint, giving their prognosis to the wind.
The phrase "I'm sorry" gathers up salt, like a licked wound.

What can we say? Our predictions won't stop even the rain.
We run the uncharted course. Scoop a patch of hallowed ground.
A coffin slides into the water, same splash as a lifeboat.

18 July 2011

The King

His beard has been combed to the curve
of his frown, chiseled in lanes like
his durable fate, the torrent of robes
spilling down his steep legs in ivory.

Once, he was a prince winging the 
battlefield, inhaling the brittle air.
His orders fled on clouded breath.
Now his sword rests in his lap like

a grandchild, lips thinned to a line.
He watches as his brothers, servants,
friends march down the checkered rows,
hears the clink of a side-swiped piece,

a syllable like "oh!", sees the hand that
lifts them slowly from the battlefield.

20 July 2011

The Queen

She is worry cut from walrus tusk,
marbled and polished crystalline.
She rests her pale, crown-heavy head
in a slender, spider-cracked hand.

Her eyes bulge out from their baggy
lower lids like the far-sighted woman
in spectacles I sat next to on the tube.
She too had been up all night, fretting

her features down to the long ridge
of nose and puffy jowls, spectator
of her offspring's passion plays
with only a teacup left to listen.

One hand on her face in disbelief,
her thoughts the recurring casualty.

28 July 2011

Rook

Neither elephant nor castle, he couldn't
be further from it, our man is a cone
of confidence, or foolishness, a dunce-
cap for a helmet, long triangular shield.

And he holds his sword like an unlit torch,
as though on some dark expedition, or at
attention, pointing upwards, signaling God.
He watches the arrows swarm like flies.

He will wait at the door to the king's
new abode, keeping the pawns in order
like a combat-ready butler, commando Jeeves.
He waits behind an inscrutable expression.

When the time comes, he will trade blows
with an equal. The sacrifice makes him grin.

Bishop

"What do I know..."

Knight

He rides a man-sized hobby horse,
stirrups nearly dragging on the ground.
His spear extends to the ear of his steed
who looks down resolutely, biting hard.

His coattails, tack, and livery hang down
like his mouth, his beard, a river
of cloth roiling beneath him to suggest
swift, decisive, hooks of movement.

A horse in battle is a weapon of war
more protective than helmet and shield,
hooves more deadly than a blade.
But power, sideswiped, can smother you.

He perches on his mount, a shy lover,
wearing costume confidence, hoping to last.

Bishop

His robes curve like his shovel-faced jaw,
clutching his sceptre, like a skeleton
his scythe. "What do you know about death?" 
he asks, pressing his holy book close.

He has seen the statues of Odin pulled down,
and a new god raised up on the crucifix.
What matters is to know what happens next
when one of us is taken off the board.

Do we feast on pancakes and beer? Or does
the chisel come again, to erase our features?
Answer for them, our bishop, with a verse
taken from this game's official rulebook.

His mitre is smooth. Lift it to move him,
and you will leave your print on the lustre.


Pawn

Most unlucky lump, already a headstone,
a milestone of your own progress, which
must always be forward, facing the hell.
Did they say going first was a privilege?

Tusk-tips and flat-bottomed stones come
to life, you live for the lucky side-swipe
of your betters, facing the same-height enemy
in impasse, double-step, and en passant.

Three of you together make a fort. Three
can mob a knight and pull him down.
Standing in one row, beating your shields,
"the last shall be first" seems plausible.

Peons turned to soldiers, bear up proud.
Round lumps, you are the fire in the coal.

Carluccio's in London, Friday Night

This is not your nan's Sunday dinner, a fish-and-chippy
or Chinese buffet. Tonight, this is Italy, no haggis
here, no bottled beers, just pasta, fresh, tailor-made.

The mincing waiter gooses the posterior of the brawny
man in the scullery, then inverts his frown, glides
over to the long table of single women, and flirts--

at first, you think, he hears the clink of coins
on his silver tip plate. But their laughter opens
his face like a daffodil, peeling back the outer petals

to reveal the golden middle of a man surrounded by nieces
and sisters, their heartaches, children, and deadbeat men,
recommends the right rosè to wash it away for awhile

24 August 2011

and they comply with his performance, casting their eyes
over his handsome face and fit physique, investors
in a scheme that yields only the thrill of investing.

But isn't this happiness? William Blake would whisper
in each ear an accolade for joy caught on the wing
and when they are at home, curling the stockings

from their legs, a little drunk, and over-full,
their smiles that say "you never know" and "could
have been" will smile on them again, shaking out

their hairpins, clink, on the makeup mirror,
a sound our Romeo won't know or hear, scrubbing
the stubborn Bolognese from his stiff apron,

slamming the tongue of the register back into place.

4 September 2011

La Campagna, London, Friday Night

This is not your nan's Sunday dinner, a fish-and-chippy
or Chinese buffet. Tonight, this is Italy, no haggis
here, no bottled beers, just pasta, fresh, tailor-made.

The mincing waiter gooses the posterior of the brawny
man in the scullery, then inverts his frown, glides
over to the long table of single women, and flirts--

at first, you think, he hears the clink of coins
on his silver tip plate. But their laughter opens
his face like a daffodil, peeling back the outer petals

to reveal the golden middle of a man surrounded by nieces
and sisters, their heartaches, children, and deadbeat men,
recommends the right rosè to wash it all away

and they comply with his performance, casting their eyes
over his handsome face and fit physique, investors
in a scheme that yields only the thrill of investing.

But isn't this happiness? William Blake would whisper
in each ear an accolade for joy caught on the wing
and when they are at home, curling the stockings

from their legs, a little drunk, and over-full,
their smiles that say "you never know" and "could
have been" will smile on them again, shaking out

their hairpins, clink, on the makeup mirror,
a sound our Romeo won't know or hear, scrubbing
the stubborn Bolognese from his stiff apron,

sliding the tongue of the register back into place,
the backstage routine always tinged with sadness,
the afterglow of smiles, the space between applause.

Use some of these words:

coalesce, shiver, cope, adopt, bill, gird, navigate, uphill, backhand, kettle, precocious, lush, jury, pepper, scarf

The Long Walk Home

Here comes winter, with a grudge on her back,
tugging scarves like nooses, serving backhanders
of air on the uphill climb. Gird yourself, she 
aims for the head. This is no way to navigate,
through pepperings of grit, the trash coalescing
in nooks as you shiver, and cope, and adapt
your desires down to the thought of the lush
kettle steam at the end of your walk, giving up
all ambition to see or be seen, make money, give
gifts, and go golfing--your kingdom, in fact
for the clink of a cup, and the drown-victim bag,
milk to lighten its amber to shoe-leather brown,
one thought: it is hot, it is tea, it is nigh.

Two women in heels walk briskly toward the train platform

It is twenty-seven against forty-one,
ball-peen hammers on a shingled roof.
Let the stars reign down their spears
on our bald, uncovered heads. Cuckoo 
clocks of the world, assemble and compare 
notes. "Fall out!" says the seargent 
before the men cross the bridge,
and the mill-worker's deafness, later
in life, sings on an angle-pitched note:
<<tinnitis, in excelsis Deo>>.
Seeds tumble down the rainstick.
Matchheads bounce on a hardwood floor.
Beach-hut windows fend off sand-laced wind.
They are marching through the gates,
they are hammering at the doors, cumulation
of every polite rap, salesman's tap,
the call to muster courage for the pitch.
This is the sound of braille read aloud,
the ice in your teeth, 21 guns at will,
unwrapping the bubble-packed parcel.
Let us observe a moment of loudness, 
give it all a round of applause.

28 September 2011

The train is in our neighbourhood, the train
is in our town. It riots like a masked accomplice,
teaches algebra as it goes, the x and y of
breeding, always lumbering over the track.

The train is a fever, and we need to be bled.
The train is a gorgon to be slain in our bed.
It rattles the bars of or caged-bird ribs.
It makes us feel hollow, then fills us again.

I want to be the conductor. I will hang flags
from the caboose. The slats are speckled with
human waste. The rails are greased with blood.
The train is in the town inside our head.

20 Oct 11

The Birthday Candle
    for Valerie

All day you were with me
like a birthday candle
in my pocket. I slid my hand
down to feel its smoothness
how pliable it had become
warmed against my leg.
The wick had become brittle
from being lit and blown,
lit and blown, but I knew
there was enough black stub
to take up a flame in case
of power cuts or a chill.

# # #

The last train from Sienna to Florence

runs a single carriage
of boys in leather jackets
playing cards, a four-year-old
shouting nursery rhymes in Italian,
and German students
sleeping in the aisles.
The interior is mint green
and gray, the curtains and seats are
royal blue.
It is not electric.
It humps along the track,
hissing at every start and stop.
At one station, we hover before
an alcove where the Holy Virgin
extends her thin white arms
to bless us, and the plastic flowers
arranged on the platform edge.
We enter the tunnel.
The pram full of coats and shopping bags
inches down the aisle
with every jolt.
The boys are laughing and slapping
each other's backs.
Older people shield their eyes
from the overhead light
and the man with the huge metal watch
wears a mask of preoccupation.
Most of the shoes are lace-up canvass,
some moccasins, some leather heels
shaped like the severe curve
of the peninsula's boot.
The sand-haired German girl
has a pink fabric carnation
stitched to her purse.
She frowns up from the floor,
and tiny pearls poke through
the curtain of her hair.
The sign above the door proclaims,
2de Classe, and beside the glass-
cased emergency hammer, there is
a sign with the exact silhouette
of the hammer inside. The woman 
in the flamingo pink
annarack is swiping the light
from her mobile phone across
her long face. The tunnel is 
no darker than the night.
The engine and the voices fill the train
like creme in a pastry roll, a thickness
of sound. I can not tell if it is 
too hot, or simply too bright.
The man beside me doesn't apologize,
in fright and bemusement,
like an Englishman might,
when his leg brushes up against mine.
A breeze cuts through the body-warm air.
Someone strokes initials--theirs? another's?--
into the moist window pane.
Sometimes there are headlights, street lamps.
Mostly, the trees obscure them.
Mostly, people get on. But sometimes,
they leave, saying "Buona..." and "Ciao"
to the friends they just met.
The girl with the pink-rimmed glasses
cuddles her rucksack. She wears
tights and a coat and an an antique-
pink ribbon pins back her thick hair.
Everyone vibrates. 
Mostly, we either accelerate or 
decelerate, leaning
as appropriate toward or away. Sometimes,
we shuffle side-to-side as if riding
a giant millipede on the march.
It is dangerous to lean out, or so
the sign says. But isn't loneliness
dangerous also? The four-year-old
paints lipstick on her mother's lips,
who is beautiful not unlike a skeleton,
her perfect nose rising in slits.
The girl zips and un-zips her white 
plastic purse. The man in the lavender
v-neck pullover blinks his amber
eyes, while his daughter chats to her mum.
The woman in gray wool wears the cracks
in her face like a renaissance statue
Everything I see I want to touch.

# # #

The shadow across the courtyard
gives off shadow-smoke.
as the heat drifts off the opposite roof,
the shadow-edge seems to evaporate,
but never dissolve.

Some thoughts are like this,
always drifting away but never gone.
The smoke crawls up the wall,
across the window,
black gossamer, widow's lace.

Hermes, god of invention, traded
his lyre to Apollo.
Across the street, someone is playing 
motown, "You make me feel like a
natural woman." Before the bells
of the Duomo furiously interrupt.

30 October

London Bridge

is a thief bristling with change,
a chain at the dock, a jar for tips, 
shoes worn down to trench rot,
slanting at the angle of a fin.
Some people fall through the floor,
others key up their marks,
knock-and-nab in pairs, grow bamboo
tough, the stunt pulled off
in russet sunsets threshed
with starlings and the silhouettes
of other bridges inching up the skyline,
tufts of trees exfoliating, the porous
clouds on dusky watch, staring
down our swollen fingers, bloody plums.

9 November 2011

Pubescence

I was a child in the tourniquet of youth,
scalding my tongue with ginger candies,
my backbone an intricate fluid mechanics,
the jigsaw of life still lingering in the box.

Nothing was cast in granite, not even my hips.
I was protean jelly, hematoma wrapped tight,
twisting the umber-stained bandages, blotched
with Africa, dragons, and moon-round spots.

We delved into Wright's aerodynamics with glee,
and always woke up before hitting the ground.

15 November 2011

What would it ask of me?
Black trees fracture the evening sky,
lit faces in the window of the train.

"Nothing will come of nothing," said
the king, so I will speak again:
the night is a keeper of secrets
placid as a frozen lake.

The people on the train wear expressions.
The tracks dissolve behind, rise up ahead.

The Birthday Candle
    for Valerie

All day you were with me
like a birthday candle
in my pocket. I slid my hand
down to feel its smoothness
how pliable it had become
warmed against my leg.
The wick was brittle
from being lit and blown,
lit and blown, but I knew
there was enough black stub
to take up a flame in case
of power cuts or a chill.
And so we went together
up the street to the train,
and sat in a row among people
with their own thoughts burning,
then onto the street again,
past the warmth of kebab shops, 
newsagents, all the while 
you were with me, smelling
of frosting and wax.
It seemed, any minute, they
might all shout, "surprise!"
and you would come out,
in that dress, and we'd go
dancing or to a matinee.
It never happened, of course.
But what might have happened,
happened in my mind all day,
and I smiled because you were there.

2 December 2011

Notes from the Underground, Revisited

# # #

Calling All Stations Blues

What could I tell to the sleepless dark?
What would it ask of me?
Black trees fracture the evening sky,
lit faces in the window of the train.

"Nothing will come of nothing," 
said the king, so I will speak again:
the night is a keeper of secrets
placid as a frozen lake.

The people on the train wear expressions.
The tracks dissolve behind, rise up ahead.
The indigo of sleep rinses my muscles,
my bones glow with a weak, phosphorous light.

Men on the platform in funny hats
were shaking their cans of change at me.
Chug-chug for the third world orphans.
Chug-chug for the veterans maimed.

The train does not favour the left rail
or right, but I touch the third rail
in asking, like a child, Who caused the orphans?
Was it the veterans? And who caused them?

Growing up, we would lay pennies on the track,
a form of worship, then collect the flattened
copper--more precious than money--a sign
that the inevitable had touched our lives.

4 December 2011

Chug-chug calls the train to the widening night,
chug-chug its lullaby to the people inside.
Black trees fracture the evening sky
The tracks rise up ahead, dissolve behind.

# # # # #

Calling All Stations Blues

Black trees fracture the evening sky,
lit faces in the window of the train.
The night is a keeper of secrets.
The tracks dissolve behind, rise up ahead.

Men on the platform in eye-catching hats
shake their cans of change at me.
Chug-chug for the third world orphans.
Chug-chug for the veterans maimed.

The train does not favour the left rail
or right, but I touch the third rail
by asking, Who caused the orphans?
Was it the veterans? And who caused them?

Growing up, we would lay pennies on the track,
a form of worship, then collect the flattened
copper--more precious than money--a sign
that the inevitable had touched our lives.

Chug-chug sings the train to the widening night,
chug-chug its lullaby to the people inside.
Black trees fracture the evening sky.
The tracks rise up ahead, dissolve behind.

# # # # #

Prime the Pump

Husk up the wet
from the gasping
well with bicep-pumps
and long, slow coaxing. 
Get to know the sound
of water coming up
it is guttural--Yiddish, Flemish,
a deep song in the throat
of the pipework junction,
an animal struggling for breath.
Rain comes down the gutter-pipes.
A gauze-faced man, pinned down,
writhes, as the body 
crescendoes against death.
My sister turns blue
with a grape in her throat.
Nurses jab adrenaline
into my spider-veined arm.
Who will be there
when the lungs well up
and the tears we have been
choking down
finally     sputter     out?

16 December 2011

Include as many of these words as you like: senator, horde, slave, cud, raspberry, peel, chaser, stunt, firm, morbid, luminary, load, baffle, jar

I am not the gangly president the world remembers
I am the smooth-talking rival senator from Illinois,
hording rum from the slave trade and wagging my jaw
like a cow chewing cud, my complexion stramineous,
morbid, unmanly, leaving the scent of paraffin behind.

20 December 2011

London Blues

A tune is playing in the tambourine streets.
In the streets, they shake out a jingling song.
Since you left, the only lyrics I hear are "gone."

The people on the bus wear their faces like masks.
The mask-faced bus people wear smiles like a shield.
Without you, now I know how that armour feels.

Underground, the trains are galloping along.
The train humps along the track somewhere deep down.
Since you're gone, "goodbye" is my favourite sound.

On the pavement, people cluster waiting to cross.
Clumping like a school of fishes, watching for green.
I stand there in your absence, not wanting to be seen.

Since you left, all the songs say "long gone."
And without you, now I know how a soldier feels.
When that train leaves, "goodbye" is all I hear.
I am nowhere, and you are everywhere, a song.

for example this tactic: distraction: 
take a lump of coal in your throat
and light it, sputter-wise, rickshaw
God Mess Ablerica.

29 December 2011

Keep Your Chin Up

All day, I was conscious 
of not having a beard.
I sat stroking the space 
beneath my chin.

Crumbs tumbled all the way
back down to my plate.
Birds found me unsuitable. 
Mice as well.

I couldn't join the Marxists 
or even the Trotskyites.
Bass choir sections assumed 
my voice was too high.

I thrust my naked face 
into the night
shirt-collars cupping
the air against my throat.

30 December 2011

She leaned forward as though life's thumb
had pressed the back of her white head
long enough for the spine to remember.
But her blue eyes whispered, "retreat."

What could I tell to the sleepless dark?
What would it ask of me?
"Nothing will come of nothing," 
said the king, so I will speak again:
the night is a keeper of secrets
placid as a frozen lake.
My muscles are rinsed with indigo,
my bones glow with a weak,
phosphorescent light.

2 Jan 2012

Piece Work

Winter, and the loom 
of the sky has been 
picked to wire.

Light etches its memories
through the long strands
of twilight. 

            We inhabit
the shell of the world,
and carry it gently.

It carries us too,
the echoing stairwell,
the empty glass aflame.

Look what I have brought--
sand from a bullet-pocked
beach, ribbon from the dead
girl's hair.
                It reaches
through shadow play, gesture,
the conspiring laughter 
of birds strung high overhead.

We dwell here, suspended
in ether, vibrating
the strands of the web.

7 Jan 12

Upon Arrival

Longing dabbles in shadows
as the day doubles back,

offering honey and vinegar,
wine to the already drunk.

Memory, that bricklayer, stirs 
its slush with a trowel.

Glazed squares shriek their re-
flected light. It is never enough.

Crevices hoard the darkness,
and hiss, "never enough."

We rub against newsprint
until our thumbs go black.

Steam chafes against its pane of sky.
We can never go back.

13 Jan 2012

Street Level

Life's thumb had pressed the back
of her white head so long the spine
remembered, and she leaned into
her journey like a racer in slow-
motion, leading with her purse,
both hands folded over the top,
a gesture of offering (or defense?)
while the crowd swarmed around,
each step placed determinedly,
dropping, left, right, ever ahead.
But her blue eyes whispered, "retreat."

14 Jan 2012

Same-Day Return

What could I tell to the long twilight?
What would it ask of me?
The dusk is a keeper of secrets
placid as a frozen lake.

My muscles are rinsed with indigo,
my bones glow with a weak,
phosphorescent light.
The darkness can't fully arrive.

Nothing will come of nothing, warned
the king. So I will speak again:
the moon pours down her tenderness
the city glows back in praise.

The skyline stretches its fingers,
reaching to the tips of the glove.
Even the trees are clambering,
black lightning sprung up from the ground.

16 Jan 2012

Seraphim

You think of us in bright medieval paintings,
our flat profiles ascending and descending ladders.
And slender, robed in cinnabar, announcing from stage
right or praising God in cartoon bubbles flipped
upside-down to be read more easily from above.

But come with me to the bridge at night, where couples
stroll over the brown-black Thames, haloed by domes
and spires, the spoked and spinning blue Eye.
Look closely down the railing. There she is.
We travel the chilled air, whispering: don't do it.

We are the shiver of thought, that the money or lover
might return, the painful illness subside, be cured.
And when they jump, we are the warmth in hypothermia,
the ones in the brain's control room, turning the knobs
of the visible scene, hastening the fade to black.

25 Jan 2012

Written During Depression: Twelve Reasons to Go On

The cat nipping my ankles when I emerge from the bath.
The notes above the notes in blackbird song.
The moment before falling asleep, when you are free.
The way bad jokes make my side hurt when I laugh.
The pleasant geometry of bathroom tiles.
Fancy coffee that tastes like bourbon.
That one brave pigeon that won't be shooed.
Red phone booths in a wash of beige and grey.
Irrational numbers thumbing their noses at our sums.
Trees much older than me or any person alive.
Sticking an arm in my coat and finding a scarf.
Street lamps globed in mist like Chinese lanters.


28 January 2012

A Game of Sevens

The rain drifts in on murderous wings
flapping a game of sevens, flapping
in crêpe-paper tunes and tinfoil waltzes.

Perspehone ducks under an awning. An old
man holds the lift. Cabs pass black as puddles.
The cables groan out their circular trip.

She shakes out her hair by the fireplace.
The kettle roils, an underground spring.
She shoulders her umbrella like a gun.

What we remember becomes us, when the lights
go out: a glance, a shiver. This time,
she promises herself, I will try to be happy.

1 February 2012

Job Loses It

God help me. I don't want to debate about
theology anymore. I want the breeze
to sing my name through the trees, I want 
the ducks to laugh at all my jokes, turtles
to make a rare emergence just to catch
a glimpse of me strolling the mossy carpet. God
damn it why can't I have what everyone
wants?--an orchard of ripe fruit surrendering,
goats in the field and kids in the cradle,
a fridge full of ready-made meals, clean sheets
and a salve to clear up this bad case of poison ivy.

5 Feb 12

Snow Den

You say that you know me, like spring 
knows the trees. But will you still
love me when the path becomes covered, 
and the birds stop singing my name?

You must become a student of winter, carry
a dagger of ice near the heart. Only then
will you see where the snow dens are made
to wait out the blue world in darkness.

Will you remember the green buds to me
when the brambles are mounded with snow?
Will you remember the twigs we can weave
and which branches will give underfoot?

Let us shelter awhile in the low overhang
of these trees bent down by their memories.

Schneeengel
    for William Blake

You seem them in the spaces between leaves,
industrious in praise of light and shadow.

androgynous as starlight, still as a match.

Look up and see--they perch on every branch-tip,
bespangling with cold, insistent light.

Losing It At Leicester Square Station

Their perfumed bodies bounce along
exuding whiskey, vodka, gin
the bite of antiseptic wafting in,
low guttural pronouncements of wet shoes,
the whiff of hairspray, choke of ash
all here, contained, and roiling through
a tunnel tube, the awkwardness of body
under museum-glass display and flickering
light, the people draped from poles
and straddling luggage, blocking
for a post-apocalyptic cabaret,
as though at any moment the bearded man
and mobile-obsessed teen might ease
into a close-harmony duet, while the
man in the hi-vis jacket taps a dance.

8 March 12

Treatment in Outline

It is the shape of things to come I like:
the edge of a puddle, describing its dryness,
the thud of a drain cover signifying snug fit.

I like the mortar as much as the brickwork,
see the leading as equal to the held panes of glass.
And the sky is described by the branches that fondle it.

To consider a droplet of rain, we consider meniscus,
and all that is held by its borders together,
or limned by proximity to what it is not--

a clothesline dividing the sky from the sky,
leaf-work stitching the horizon's patched quilt.

17 March 12

undaunted, sharpie, bladder, stem, volatile, bushy, church, niggle, powder, middle way

Politico in Extremis

There is no middle way anymore, says the bushy camel
undaunted to the needle's sharp eye. The situation
has become volatile, a church full of gunpowder,
saddlebags and wine bladders swollen with gasoline.
It all stems from a niggle, wincing at the crude remark,
a gleam in the eye that ignites the touch paper, 
machinations in place of intimations, gun barrels
where daisies should be. And us? We get it on
screens at increased resolution, our own resolves
less crisp than a counterfeit twenty, soggy
on the idea of global anything, just one granule
caught up in the dust devil's spinning, dizzy
on the edges and hollow at the center, the eye
of the storm kept empty, no middle, no way.

21 March 12

23 March 12

Dream-Logic

Out of the lurching dark a secret. Out of the secrets
we press to our chests, a shadow is lapping our ankles.

Our hankies we hold to our noses and bellow, the sputum
of a sperm whale staining that royal blue dress.

Out of secrets, we make names. One for Wisteria, two
for Kalamazoo. This is snot, what you think, my dear.

Trains and taxis lurching, carrying an army of women
to the front in their royal blue dresses with dark corsage.

The front is in Kalamazoo, but this is a secret, we're
up to our ankles, our garters, and pressed for time.

A sperm whale breaches, humped like a black cab, waves
lapping the spot where he descends on his fins.

An army of noses salutes him in three rounds of seven,
targeting the Wisteria-shaped clouds with tracers of snot.

In the distance, a shadow hangs over, a dark corsage
against the royal blue skyline. My dear, press on!

The trains are on time, but the cabs are all late,
traversing the snot-slicked lanes like terrible secrets,

in a book on a shelf somewhere deep in Kalamazoo,
someone has pressed Wisteria into the pages.

The dark stain makes the shape of a whale in a dress
wearing garters, pith helmet--and apparently--combat boots.

1 April 12

On Regent's Canal
    after Hieronymus Bosch

Wench up the lock gate, the water ascends.
I, too, have been seeking my level. The flat-
bottomed boat I have called My Own Life bumps
the canal edge in rhythm to the channeled flow.

These, and other signs, are everywhere, waiting
to be deciphered from dots and dashes into words,
and words into action--the trickiest part--
as all translators of well-meant intentions know.

Gate by gate, I descend to the river, tributary
by tributary I crawl my way closer to the sea.
Cup a hand to the frigid water, slurp up a taste 
of motherly salt in increased concentration.

6 April 12

I print my name on the life-preserving ring,
try my head through the hole, just in case.
Oil films the surface, a thin collodion mask
on which the sun prints snapshots of fish.

Dinghy and rowboat, barge and single-mast sail,
come with me, down the stairwell, step by step.
A dog on the bank tries to warn us, but drunk
on the ripples in sunlight, we glide on.

11 April 12

April
    (with apologies to Eliot)

So, fine, get warm and comfortable again, I don't 
mind a little cruelty, I myself have thumbed
ants into the tilework, teased a dog with a stick.

But must you wear that pale green mist, like a low-
cut dress and paint your toenails yellow? Take
your blue eyes off me. Unclouded, they burn.

It all starts up again, and like Job you expect
me to be grateful for the new, stop arguing
points of theology now that the weather's nice.

I think we need some time apart, you know--
to see other seasons, stir our dull roots.
Don't even get me started on the lilacs.

    an acrostic abnomial for Mark Burnhope

21 April 2012

Pastoral

The wind was made for you. The breeze, a gift.
Do not linger on the stairwell, finger-in-mouth,
but come out and lie in the tufts of grass
while the trees shake berries from their hair.

The bright persimmon, the dull aching fig,
have smell and taste for their thoughts,
the aggregation of dew instead of sweat.
They perspire little. Unlike us.

Cast off the floral-print duvet, a mockery
of pleasant rearrangement. That is starlight's
job, and the happenstance of negative space
is what we select from chaos and chain reaction.

My bright thoughtful pebble. Warm sentient clay.
Where once we observed, now we shape. Our self-
image the printing of fern-leaves on hands,
blue smoke from a chainsaw, iridescent black tar.

Upstairs/Downstairs 2012

Sound the drums and bash the doors!
Clatter the cupboards, stomp the floor.
Here comes the king of the upstairs flat,
make way, under-dwellers for his noisome prat.

We'll dance on the ceiling 'till dawn,
flicking butts from our porch on your lawn,
with missives attached: "Begone!"
And then we get on with our song.

Hammer the floorboards! Strum the guitar!
Shatter the glass door, rev up the car.
We can't hear a thing from up this high,
and jab thumbs in our ears when you cry.

We'll clang you into submission, we'll
stamp you out of your skull; for the well
is deep where our mercy ought to dwell
and we'll chime to the bottom like bells.

Bash the doors and batter the drums!
Stomp the floor to shake the sun.
The upstairs flat has its cacophonous king
who tortures the birds to make them sing.

8 May 12

Martyr's Cross
    Old St. Pauls' Church, Edinburgh

Here is an icon to brand the brain,
flat-hammered iron, irregular, dark--

A rail spike for the journey ahead,
shackles uncurled like a foetus.

Here is the flat blade that dubbed,
helmet's nosepiece, horseshoe for luck.

With this you could bolt a door, wedge
out a draft, scrape a dust like dry blood.

Arms tied behind you, remember the reflex--
flung wide when your child runs toward you.

Some die curled, some splayed, some dance.
And when you drop, this image ascends.

Here is a symbol to singe in the eyes,
spiking the track, hinging the door.

cephalophores

Cephalophores

You navigate the Earth, head in hand.
Sometimes supplicant, precocious,
night-walkers among the damned,
but there are advantages: raising
your head in outstretched arms 
to see over hills--no neck to strain.
Though mostly, you meet us waist-high,
hard-case for the brain tucked under 
one arm, striding industriously down
the platform beside the purring train.
Yes, there was that show-off who twirled 
himself by the hair, kids playing frisbee
with their haloes and such, but most prefer
the subtle intrigue of casual speech
issued from a mind detached of corporality--
the lofty air, the easy frights, how people 
stare and envy saints who can truly lose
themselves, and be found in two places at once.

26 May 12

Stigmata

It begins as a prick in the palm, an ache 
at the roof of the foot, thorn at the brim 
of your ball cap. The unseen needles enlarge 

to wood screws, making the fingers want to curl, 
feet rub together, smoothing the mosquito itch.
Soon your arms refuse to come together,

even momentarily, to give applause. You drink 
vinegar and play dice, indigestion stabs 
at your side, arms outstretched you lie in bed,

reminiscing about lovers who abandoned you,
wiping the beads of sweat on your bedclothes,
smearing the warm beads of blood.

Martyrs' Cross
    Old St. Pauls' Church, Edinburgh

Here is an icon to brand in the brain,
flat-hammered iron, irregular, dark--

A rail spike for the journey ahead,
foetus uncurled, shackles bent open.

30 May 2012

snotty-nosed, swilling, scruffy, inheritance, ardent, helipad, fairy tale, rookie, treachery


Underground Mice

Scruffy, ardent, fairy-tale dustballs,
your inheritance is meek--

precocious vermin, grey as soot,
you thrive on our absentmindedness--

a pastry crumb is as good as a seed,
soda stains as sweet as spring water.

There is black grease for mud,
trains instead of thunderstorms.

Snotty-nosed kids look on, swilling cola
and popping gum in ceramic echoes.

A crisp packet descends, relief aid
touching the helipad too late--

the rookie makes a treacherous dive
to dislodge it. The train passes over

like night, like war, unstoppable.

8 June 2012

Million-Dollar Rain
    for E.K.

It is hardly there at all,
this feather-rain, suffusing
the air with casual descent

pooling in crevices of husk
and trickling down the yellow stem,
dampening the topsoil sponge.

It is the antidote to drought, but also
to floods of Biblical scale, this
Providence and proof of tenderness--

each droplet a tiny silver dollar
skating the side of a piggybank,
reclaiming the mortgaged barn.

How strange to discover it here,
leashing an eager Retriever for his
pre-dawn hike through a London park,

four thousand miles and an ocean away
from where the saying first took root
in your keen farm-girl's mind.

Strange how what is hardly there
is there all the more for its gentleness,
dampening the head of your blonde companion,

who, when you unclip his collar, races
as fast as ever through clay and mud
at the doves he will never catch.

The neighbour dressed in misery still won't
return your smile, unaware he's breathing
money-mist, shaking gold-dust from his hair.

So you walk with this secret knowledge,
burning like a gas lamp inside, while all around
you the the land keeps soaking, gently, soaking.

paper, the rectangular beds and hinged doors.

Here are two eggs, jiggling like breasts.
The obscene bacon, and protean beans.

I write couplets to fend off the darkness.
I write couplets from my lunar cell.

Unidentified Photo on the Internet

The seaweed men patrol the icy town
with sticks wrapped in bumbergrass
their hooked beaks hissing steam,
eyes painted open against the twilight.

They trudge the streets like shaggy
marionettes, boots cracking glazed tarmac,
past tiny houses asleep in banks of snow.
The white mountain looks down on suburbia.

Bracken-clad and thistle-shod, the watchmen
pause, two fuzzy pillars holding up the sky.
Who are these fur-girded sentinels, guarding
the precarious stillness, listening for a shot?

Despots and their henchmen never dress up silly.
The uniforms, medals and gold lapels--each button
sewn tight against beatings, collars crisp
as the edge of a blade, shoes black as bruising.

The green men mock us with their small parade,
marching in line, then two abreast, officious--
bumbersticks perched on their left shoulder,
palace guards unblinking in time of jubilee.

Legume-coloured pantaloons, be proud.
I couldn't find your names on Wikipedia.
If you shed tears from those golf-ball eyes,
I'll be none the wiser, and very far away.

Everything has a name, a place, a purpose.
But when two green life-size puppets appear
in an elderly neighbour's hedgerow, do you
ask them in for tea? Or hide the china?

The green men mock us with their small parade,
trudging the streets like shaggy marionettes
their hooked beaks hiss, they clutch bumbersticks,
each eye painted white against the dawn.

2 July 2012, Ojai, CA

Runt

Unlucky pup thirteen. When I was seven,
I watched your long-suffering labrador mum
chew and lick your bubble glazed in scum,
gnaw you free from your tether like the rest.

But as you lurched through darkness towards
the double-row of teats, already occupied,
I watched her move you carefully aside
with a back paw hinged like a garden gate.

Insistent, nonchalant, each time you approached
she pushed back like a lady refusing more tea,
"Not mine", "Too small", "No, thanks", "None for me"
while her disciples held on toothlessly tight.

I stabbed a pin in the tip of my sister's doll bottle
and swilled it with a mix of powdered milk and water,
palmed you on your back and fed you like a new father,
nervous, doting, repulsed, stroking your minuscule paws.

Once, I tipped the bottle too high, and like that joke
we told to Mikey at lunch recess, milk came bubbling
out of your nose. You kicked and writhed, struggling
under the sleight-of-hand, unintended interrogation.

I righted you, and dabbed you micro-muzzle with a cloth.
Two days later, the writhing stopped, and you died
bald and toothless, having never opened your eyes.
I buried you in the yard, along with the bottle.

I spent fourteen months in England, working hard to make the odd feel normal. When what is foreign feels odd, it is understandable. But when what was formerly normal now feels odd, that is perhaps the oddest feeling of all.

9 July 12

18 July 12

Rockets' Red Glare

Laughter makes the stars explode.
Warm nights, the smell of grill-smoke,
match heads and kerosene. We have always 
been a people in love with flames.

The stars explode with laughter.
Roman candles fizz a purity of light.
Strawberries, blueberries and cream
salute "the Republic, for which it stands."

The sky is blown apart with laughing stars,
bottle rockets and tracer bullets,
the black smoke of anti-aircraft flack,
tin toys and pinwheels spewing sparks.

Laugh, and the bombs laugh with you,
the stars laugh, too, the flyovers
and brass parades, ancestral gun smoke,
brimstone, the red and hissing fuse.

19 July 12

24 July 12

Beating Your Father at Chess

The grey once at his temples
now rings his head in tonsur
as if your mother's death
had prompted holy orders.

You sit by him, Pater Noster,
and help to affix stamps
--email is so impersonal--
with your younger, wetter tongue.

There was a time when blodlust
clouded your eyes like cataracts,
and you slammed your fists
on the board, pieces jumping.

You vowed "one day", and then one day
came, with all the frisson of Oedipus,
followed by a prescient migraine
as the crown passed to your head.

His hands bruise easily now, prefer
to sharpen his neurons on Sudoku,
pencilling in likely numerals
in a ransacked leather armchair.

Sometimes he nods off, head heavy,
and you end your visit on tiptoe,
Pyrrhus unable to meet the gaze
of an opponent so easily overcome.

3 August 2012

Times I've Loved / People who said "no" to me

School Trip

The black waters churn up stars and city lights,
hands on the railing, Juliette at her balcony.

"Do you like astronomy?" asked the girl, and I
failed to design or discover a clever reply.

What I learned on that boat, chugging through
puberty, is how to love the space left behind.

Two blackbirds ascend from a silhouette tree,
the old shape more elegant for being transformed.

Watermarks--in the bath, on buoys and locks,
tell the once-water's story perpetually.

She came out from the dance to check on me,
and the music became strange with distance.

How often have I stood in the rain, admiring
the space between raindrops, the dry spots

on pavement filling up, like a mapped-out
invasion?--sweet, imperious time-lapse war.

Even then, I loved love's absence, it's hiding,
the dark liquid tugging of something so grand.

7 August 2012

Why I can't eat ___

Ciliac's Lament

I can't eat scones or biscuits for tea,
I can't eat cake or crumpets or buns,
nor even slip crackers under my brie.

Food made with wheat is typically fun,
tempting, like croissants and crepes
but the truth is, one crumb and I'm done.

And so, life is full of narrow escapes,
sauces so often are thickened with flour,
and rye is not funny if it touches my plate.

From these grains, ancient Rome came to power,
But for me, it's life-sapping malaise
in my belly, sweet breads turns sour.

8 August 12

parc fermé, halberd, stepper, swindle, smuggle, drape, dirty, tend, mayor, slushy, senator, cud, raspberry, origami, oiling, posterity, baffle, jar

Ninth Legion Camp Outside Camulodunum

So here we stand, in parc fermé, halberds racked
just a two-stepper away, trying to swindle
each other at dice, smuggle an extra ration
back to our Hessian tent, dirty and dun--
while boys tend the fires like little mayors,
presiding over slushy coals, giving speeches
to the embers in their senatorial chambers,
black sod and smoking turf glowering like a bush
pocked with raspberries. We make origami
of the playing cards, oiling our readiness,
slipping an extra joker in our sandals
for posterity, baffling the gods with nonchalance,
tamping our eagerness down in a wax-sealed jar.

11 August 2012

One man is equivalent to all Creation. One man is a World in miniature.
Albert Pike

15 August 2012

I know the oaks on the far side of the meadow
like I know the hairs on my thigh in the bath.
So much of one day is mine alone--washing plates
and boiling water for tea, picking dead flowers
from a window-sill vase. The birds won't tell
how I look in my robe, tapping out poem scraps,
and the cats on garden patrol can't smell me
behind two panes of glass. This will never make 
it into an email or online update, these small 
moments pass without comment, only the silent 
narrator of my mind, wondering about the roses
and what's for breakfast, deciding the coffee
is good this time, yes I'll buy that brand again.

22 August 2012

Waltz of the Morning

I know the oaks on the far side of the meadow
like I know the hairs on my thigh in the bath.
So much of one day is mine alone--washing plates
and boiling water for tea, picking dead flowers
from a window-sill vase. The birds won't tell
how I look in my robe, tapping out poem scraps,
and the cats on garden patrol can't smell me
behind two panes of glass. This will never make 
an email subject or status update, these moments 
pass without comment, narrated by only my mind, 
which admires the overblown roses and wonders
what's for breakfast, deciding the coffee
is tasty this time; yes I'll definitely buy 
this brand again. Even the headlines, designed
to shock, only carry a few volts through
the old gray spaghetti. Cow parsley is my
audience, swaying to the sounds plucked out
in my music-box head--a familiar tune, the
waltz of the morning, meant to be danced
'round the room, partly clothed, and alone.

23 August 2012

bedlam, unworthy, black, cheapen, ghost, paling, snarl, turkey, modulation, span, pinion, puffy, denude, forget, gravel, zombie, bonfire, paradox

Lady MacBeth Takes Sodium Pentathol

What's done is done. Need I say more? No bonfire paradox
there, zombie revelations tramping up the gravel path,
we can't forget, denuded of pretension, how the puffy
jowls of fate have snatched us, pinioned between two
molars and the wet incisors' span--we try to modulate,
prevaricate, turkeys flapping wattles on the Senate
chamber floor--but all we get back are the snarling
facts before us, paling the ghosts we have cheapened
by discussing them, blacking our hands like so much
ash, unworthy to be called "conversation", this bedlam
of words circumnavigating what's done, done, and done.

28 Aug 12

Autumn

Mist, there's a bit. And I suppose the fruit
is ripening, though the blackberries still tang
like lemon, and the mown wheat stubble bristles
like a patient in remission or a military brat--
nothing mellow about it, slugs in the hedgerows
desperate, the lushes, for just one more drink.

2 Sept 12

Mice of the London Underground


I. Covent Garden

Scruffy, ardent, fairy-tale dustballs,
your inheritance is meek--

precocious vermin, grey as soot,
you thrive on our absentmindedness--

a pastry crumb is as good as a seed,
soda stains as sweet as spring water.

There is black grease for mud,
trains instead of thunderstorms.

A crisp packet descends, relief aid
touching the helipad too late--

the rookie makes a dive, on instinct
to dislodge it. The train passes over

like night, like war, unstoppable.


II. King's Cross

Every day, I look out for you--the movement
of shadow under a rail, along the ribbed
wall of cabling that spans the tunnel.

Some estimate you are half-a-million strong,
mice of the London Underground, which 
sounds like a call to unionise, and see

how fast a childish fascination can turn
political? I only wanted your soft, coal-
black presence in my dull commuter's life.

I only wanted to know how you coexist
on a mouse-wide bridge of cable, under
a humming metal track, when the train

announces its arrival by pushing the wind
into your conical face, and do you suppress
the urge to flee, lying stiff as a railroad tie?

This is what it is like to live with a god.
A wife stiffens at the late-night key in the lock.
A soldier salutes his orders into no-man's land.

And you, soft sickly creatures, born
in darkness, live invisible and shit in places
where angels, on pin heads, could never stand.

The train squeaks to a halt in the charcoal tunnel.
The driver reassures us over the speaker. I listen 
for you at the window, like a mother at her crib.


III. Farringdon
    (after a prank notice about attacking mice)

Tuck your trousers in at Farringdon.
There, tiny lions will nibble your toes, 
run up your suit leg and scar pantyhose.

They're eyeing your soft ankle flesh,
gnawing through news of the Eurozone debt.
Pull up your socks to avoid the attack.

Don't be a victim, secure your slacks.
The scavenging creatures outnumber us all,
just waiting for someone to fall.

Believe what is written on the train notice board,
and eye with suspicion the fluff on the floor,
the vermin at Farringdon are barbaric hordes.

Farringdon first, and then the whole world.
It starts at your ankles and ends at your purse.
Parasites in the shadows, waiting to lurch.

Tuck in, tuck in at Farringdon. Tuck in 
on your way to the bank. Believe what you read.
Small creatures glare out from their greed.

14 Sept 12

Apologies for the Seasons

Autumn
    (with apologies to Keats)

Mist, there's a bit. And I suppose the fruit
is ripening, though the blackberries still tang
like lemon, and the mown wheat stubble bristles
like a patient in remission or a military brat--
nothing mellow about it, slugs in the hedgerows
desperate, the lushes, for just one more drink.
...

Winter
    (with apologies to Shakespeare)

Here she comes, shouldering her own discontent,
tugging scarves like nooses, serving backhanders
of air on the uphill climb. Gird yourself, she 
aims for the head. This is no way to navigate,
through pepperings of grit, the trash coalescing
in nooks as you shiver, and cope, and adapt
your desires down to the thought of the lush
kettle steam at the end of your walk, giving up
all ambition to see or be seen, make money, give
gifts, and go golfing--your kingdom, in fact
for the clink of a cup, and the drown-victim bag,
milk to lighten its amber to shoe-leather brown,
one thought: it is hot, it is tea, it is nigh.

Spring
    (with apologies to T.S. Eliot)

So, fine, get warm and comfortable again, I don't 
mind a little cruelty, I myself have thumbed
ants into the tilework, teased a dog with a stick.

But must you wear that pale green mist, like a low-
cut dress and paint your toenails yellow? Take
your blue eyes off me. Unclouded, they burn.

It all starts up again, and like Job you expect
me to be grateful for the new, stop arguing
points of theology now that the weather's nice.

I think we need some time apart, you know--
to see other seasons, stir our dull roots.
Don't even get me started on the lilacs.

Summer
    (with apologies to Neruda)
    
Consider the bicycle. Tires warming in the sun.


18 Sept 

22 Sept

Autumn
    (with apologies to Keats)

Mist, there's a bit. And I suppose the fruit
is ripening, though the blackberries still tang
like lemon, and the mown wheat stubble bristles
like a patient in remission or a military brat--
nothing mellow about it, slugs oozing in hedgerows
desperate for a drink, marrows swelling like goiters,
and the page keeps curling, brown at the edge then 
black as the poppies shaking seed from their hair,
the air as sour as cider and filled with the sound
of bugs and swallows convening their secret societies.

23 Sept

Summer
    (with apologies to Dylan Thomas)

The moon might rise forever, out of sight,
a balloon ascending in fresh-made clouds

and the fruit trees go on curtseying,
while the willow sweeps low in obsequy.

Clear water from who-knows-where will bathe
the pebbles in white-gold light,

and the lamb's coat give off its holy glow,
unstained, unstainable, and sustained.

But the distant hills and hedgerows blaze
with a green fire as brief as childhood.




Apologies For All Seasons

Spring
    (with apologies to T.S. Eliot)

So, fine, get warm and comfortable again, I don't 
mind a little cruelty, I myself have thumbed
ants into the tilework, teased a dog with a stick.

But must you wear that pale green mist, like a low-
cut dress and paint your toenails yellow? Take
your blue eyes off me. Unclouded, they burn.

It all starts up again, and like Job you expect
me to be grateful for the new, stop arguing
points of theology now that the weather's nice.

I think we need some time apart, you know--
to see other seasons, stir our dull roots.
Don't even get me started on the lilacs.

Summer
    (with apologies to Dylan Thomas)

The moon might rise forever, out of sight,
a balloon ascending in fresh-made clouds

and the fruit trees go on curtseying,
while the willow sweeps low in obsequy.

Clear water from who-knows-where will bathe
the pebbles in white-gold light,

and the lamb's coat give off its holy glow,
unstained, sustained, and unstainable.

But the distant hills and hedgerows blaze
with a green fire as brief as childhood.

Autumn
    (with apologies to John Keats)

Mist, there's a bit. And I suppose the fruit
is ripening, though the blackberries still tang
like lemon, and the mown wheat stubble bristles
like a patient in remission or a military brat--
nothing mellow about it, slugs oozing in hedgerows
desperate for a drink, marrows swelling like goiters,
and the page keeps curling, brown at the edge then 
black as the poppies shaking seed from their hair,
the air as sour as cider and filled with the sound
of bugs and swallows convening their secret societies.

Winter
    (with apologies to William Shakespeare)

Here she comes, dragging a sack of discontent,
tugging scarves like nooses, serving backhanders
of air on the uphill climb. Gird yourself, she 
aims for the head. This is no way to navigate,
through pepperings of grit, the trash coalescing
in nooks as you shiver, and cope, and adapt
your desires down to the thought of the lush
kettle steam at the end of your walk, giving up
all ambition to see or be seen, make money, give
gifts, and go golfing--your kingdom, in fact
for the clink of a cup, and the drown-victim bag,
milk to lighten the amber to shoe-leather brown,
one thought: it is hot, it is tea, it is nigh.
