9 Oct.

Ideas:
War Paint (putting on makeup in the Tube)
Black Pig (ballerina?)
Three Sisters (geese)

Country Walk

Black pig in silhouette, ploughing a tea-saucer 
snout, the neck bends low like a martyr at the chop,
but closer up, peering through wire via tiny black 
dots, she seems to know our voices by now, know 
that we bring blackberries to scatter in the mud.

Now the three sisters arrive in white satin,
telling us off through their sculpted orange beaks,
inspecting us sideways from each blue marble eye,
long necks at the ready in serpentine curves
the smallest concealing a splayed broken wing.

10 Oct.

The Shetland can see us through a long shock 
of fringe, but decides that we aren't worth 
the long trip from across the ankle-high heather,
so she stands at ease on four pillars, swishing
the ropes of her tail and grinding her teeth.

The berries have stained our hands to a bruise,
but the pig in us keeps rooting our pockets for more.
We see with a glassy-eyed clarity now the clouds
gathering white into the Provia-toned blue, ourselves
becoming a memory, formless shape in an unnamed field.

20 Oct 12

How You Were Conceived

Sheets twist through shadow.
Oleander pummels the moonlight.

The sea takes in the bodies
of swimmers like floating debris.

We make love until shaking
with hunger, eat from one plate,

music playing in a distant room,
hands submerged in dishwater.

I loved your mother before 
she had to be brave. Before the ashes

and wailing--the arch of her shoulder
in shadow, our breath in unison

becoming the eruption of light.
This world bulges with tenderness.

Mockingbird sings all night,
and if she did not answer,

I too would become frantic,
baroque, filling the air with trills,

to shorten the distance between silence
and the silence that has no reply.

She steps through the door, out of sight,
and a song gathers up in my throat.

23 Oct 12

stutter, dogwood, cough, canyon, strung, gut, apparition, claw, dissolve, plum, noon, cedar, shame, dazzle, choleric, wire, pit, amaryllis, border, lever, emit, charcoal, fluorescent, bourbon, meringue

The Virgin Mary Sits Across from Me, Applying Mascara on a Northbound London Tube

There she is, across the stuttering canyon,
apparition as brief as a cough, deriving
long arcs of blackened curls up from her
plum-coloured eyelids into dazzling lashes.

Perhaps she just wants to ride for awhile,
applying kohl through the charcoal tunnel,
dissolving whenever a pregnant woman boards 
in rush-hour panic, to reveal an empty seat.

No shame in looking nice, even with nowhere
left to go, having exchanged the cedars
for dogwood, the dry Jerusalem winters
for drizzle and fluorescent overcast.

Crying only smudges, so she holds herself
together with a bourbon-watery gaze, emits
only the slow, steady notes of cat-gut,
pulled into wire, grazed by a resined bow.

She hums her cello-concerto while the tube
claws through a cabled underworld, passages
of memory as brittle as meringue: her Son
not gone, but everywhere, the empty crosses

raised in wood and stone, shadowless at noon.
But here, she can almost remember to forget,
watching her face in the black glass behind me,
penciling her lips to a crimson wound.

29 Oct 12

Virgin Mary: a Jewish mum who lost a son

Begin anywhere, stuttering like a motorboat.

My father twists the choke and pulls the rip-cord.
The outboard motor sputters, exhales blue smoke.

We will spend all day in the boat, sunrise
through dusk, squinting into the water's glare.

I will spend a lifetime drifting, wondering who 
that man really was who decorated treble hooks

with chicken livers and homemade cheese-and-garlic
"stink bait", clicking the fly-reel, squeezing

bobbers, pinching open and shut lead weights
and snapping 20-pound-test lines with his teeth.

This is the man he wanted me to know--the sturdy,
well-versed fisherman, who could spot an eddy

likely to teem with minnows, cast in to the cool
dark side of a reed-bed with practiced grace.

Life was easy on instinct, even when wrong

9 Nov 12

Sent assignment to Jean and Marcene

Robin

Bold and scruffy, slashing into view,
tiny courtier teeming with invidious mites,

we welcome you at the waterspout, bent sprig,
weaving a maypole out of long oat grass.

You wear a red-crossed breast, poor Templar,
far from healthy with your half-feathered head,

blandishing the cold-snapped air, informing
and interpreting the seasons like a wild-eyed monk.

You preach through the frost and meandering drizzle,
moot points of theology and tactics with squirrels,

piecemeal captain of our sinking green ship,
perched like Nelson at the tallest crow's nest.

Guide us, our skipper, saint and troubadour, head-
long into the Winter that you know will be your last.

14 Nov 12

The Fisherman

My father twists the choke and pulls the rip-cord.
The outboard motor sputters, exhales blue smoke.

We will spend all day in the boat, sunrise
through dusk, squinting into the water's glare.

I will spend a childhood drifting, wondering who 
that man really was who decorated treble hooks

with chicken livers and homemade cheese-and-garlic
"stink bait", clicking the fly-reel, squeezing

bobbers, pinching open and shut lead weights
and snapping 20-pound-test lines with his teeth.

This is the man he wanted me to know--the sturdy,
well-versed fisherman, who could spot an eddy

likely to teem with minnows, cast into the cool
dark side of a reed-bed with practiced grace.

To love him was to fish with him, where time
and instinct are all that is needed over water.

Glancing down into a puddle on a London street,
I sometimes see his ragged face look up at me.

25 Nov 12

Please use as many of these words as poss, plus an ancient monument, a plant and a custom.

parget, sovereign, popper, profession, parse, relict, unattended, olive-branch, remedy, possess, crock, marrow, ma'am, starch, machine, bumper, plural, strawberry

Parget

Here a sovereign, here a ducat for the slopper
and popper of his profession, parsing the cool
plaster into relict forms and faces, his ritual
all but unattended as he pulls into form an olive
branch, trowels a crocus into relief, his remedy
for self-possessed ruddy-black brick and the crock
of half-cracked chimney pots, their bones filled
up with ashen marrow. Yes ma'am, 'twill be lovely,
the flues starched up in their best white collars,
the machine of the furnace pumping black soot sky-
ward like the column of a gentleman's top hat, 
everything done evenly and plural against the fear-
less symmetry of the city skyline, already blushing
into evening, a strawberry that is sailor's delight,
while one more column of the Parthenon gets sluiced
and gussied for the pleasure of a night on the town.

5 Dec 12

I can be nice. Some have even called me "fun".
Coincidentally, those people were all found dead
in an unrelated series of what my good friend 
the police chief called, "most unfortunate accidents."

I write in all weathers, including the warm ones, at
a cock-eyed angle, at a balmy degree, with latitude
stretching like a sock across toes, I am writing 
a new first-person historic account of my greatness.

1 Jan 13

chippy posterior pancake head-banging mince stoppage frown brawn overstrike educate letter appall legerdemain leap-year daffodil 

Include a profession, a destination and an emotion.

Despot's Progress

I can be nice. Some have even called me "fun".
Coincidentally, those people were all found dead
in an unrelated series of what my good friend 
the police chief called, "most unfortunate accidents."

I dictate in all weathers, including the warm ones, at
a cock-eyed angle, at a balmy degree, with latitude
stretching like a sock across toes, I am writing 
a new first-person historic account of my greatness.

Do not frown, my downcast daffodil, we will educate
the appalling masses out of their brawn and head-banging,
forcing the miners to march in light, mincing steps
and eat the thinnest pancakes dusted in icing sugar.

We will drag them into the buoyant train stations
of tomorrow, letter by letter and note by note,
coercing the birds to sing from our national songbook
and shit on the fallen statues of lesser men.

2 Jan 13

Only the most beautiful women from the most beautiful
villages will be allowed near my coffin to mourn, to shed
tears on demand with an approved mineral content, pageant 
veterans turning the good side of their anguish to camera.

11 Jan 13

Growing up, I loved to win. By fifteen, I was playing cash-prize chess tournaments in Las Vegas. Fortunately, I never won enough money to offset the angst, or I might still be doing it. To this day, I associate the bright lights of Las Vegas Boulevard with migraine headaches.

What happened in Vegas stayed in Vegas.

18 Jan 13

The Gathering Exercise

1.  Write down a dream (or a few moments from a dream)
2.  Describe a tool or kitchen implement
3.  Write an early childhood memory (in a line or two)
4.  Make a list of ten things you did today, in order of doing them.
5. Write a forbidden or strange thought, as if to someone who would not understand.
6.  Write a forbidden or strange thought, as if to someone who would understand.
7.  Write down three colors. Be specific.
8.  Write down four evocative or favorite words ( words you love for what they name, for their sound etc.
9.  Make a list of three things you've seen since your arrival here, then choose two and describe them in detail.
10.Physically describe briefly a person who comes to mind.
11.Write down three slant rhymes.
12. Remember a time of day. How visually do you know what time of day that was?  Describe.
13. Write an inner feeling by describing the weather.
14. Name three foods.
15. Begin three statements with "because . . ."

Allow this material to determine your poem . . . You don't have to include everything mentioned above.  Don't force an idea, plot or any particular language on the material.  Allow its music and sense to speak to you.

1. Explaining the subtleties of the British class system to an aristocrat friend.
2. It is angled just right for slipping under the browned side of fried eggs
3. Those inflated red rubber balls had a particular smell. And they were texturised so that when they hit you in the face, they left a patterned mark.
4. I woke up, sniffled, groaned, went downstairs, splashed cold water on my face, made coffee, scratched, sniffled, went upstairs, took a sip of coffee.
5. I don't actually want to die. You wouldn't understand.
6. Sometimes I want to give up on being a man.
7. The white of new snow, blue in the shadows. Quilted with a red like dried blood. The pine is beige, streaked and knotted with brown.
8. Asinine, indigo, wattle and daub.
9. Nelson's Column, Shetland ponies in the rain, a one-pound coin.
10. Her face is round and pressed forward, always into the light.
11. Fish, list, wash.
12. The sun is not just going behind a cloud. It casts low and red over the fields, in its way of saying, "Goodnight."
13. Nothing you could say will carry far. Everything muffled by snow.
14. Coffee, creamed corn, and hot dogs.
15. Because there is no more, I scrape the bowl. Because I scrape the bowl, a sound like metal. Because it sounds like metal, I think of you.

A Few Alternatives

What would I be if not a man? The white
of new snow, and blue in the shadows.
The low, red light of a descending sun,
a herd of Shetland ponies in the rain,
the dull reflection of a one-pound coin, 
the hollow sound of a red rubber ball,
the smell of coffee and nicotine on hands.
I would be creamed corn, baked beans,
chunks of hot dog stirred into spaghetti--
I would be a fish, a list, awash in light,
the metal sound at the bottom of a scraped
porridge bowl--be asinine, indigo, wattle 
and daub. I would be the moment you left, 
suspended like an insect in resin, a bubble 
in blue stained glass, breezy and light with 
apparent mobility, a photo labelled "free will",
turning and turning the bronze-handled door.

Like a salaryman under pressure, after too much karaoke,
impossible to rouse in the morning without cold water.

25 Jan 13

The walls are damp with worry, and the pipework
bangs and moans with discontent. No wonder Alice
was found with a sheet around her neck, no wonder
the crossbeams are drilled through with lice.

The tiles of the roof are padded under with moss.
The mud goes black beneath the scummy surface,
the bricks blood-damp, the paving stones weep.
No wonder we moved in, one limb at a time.

I snap twigs with my thumbs like chicken bones.
I peel oranges in a long spiral strip.

30 Jan 13

A Few Alternatives

What would I be if not a man? A herd
of Shetland ponies snorting in the rain,
the dull gold reflection of a one-pound
coin, the hollow sound of a red rubber ball,
the smell of coffee and nicotine on hands.
I would be creamed corn, baked beans,
chunks of hot dog stirred into spaghetti--
I would be a fish, a list, awash in light,
the metal sound at the bottom of a scraped
porridge bowl--be asinine, indigo, wattle
and daub. I would be the moment you left,
suspended like an insect in resin, a bubble
in blue stained glass, breezy and light with
apparent mobility, shutter-click of free will,
I would be the bronze handle of the unopened door.

# # #

We moved in, one limb at a time. Lice drilled
through the crossbeams and the paving stones wept.

Small Birds on a Newly-Felled Tree

"When we try to pick out anything by itself 
we find that it is bound fast, by a thousand 
invisible cords that cannot be broken, 
to everything in the universe."
    -John Muir

They alight on the fresh-cut stump,
flat altar to the space left behind,
each stands a moment while others circle,
aerial variant on the funerary queue.

But are they mourning? Do they know tree-death
from tree-life, sap-infused from cinder block?
They know it is gone, all hundred feet, spiralled
by vines and squirrels for as many years.

It is gone in a day, to the growl of saws,
the body stacked in a pyramid of trunk-cords,
neater than house-fire ashes or earthquake rubble,
a once-tall mast brought down to green sea level.


5 Feb 13

True, they will find other perches. Standing flat
on the dais, orating in a language all their own,
bewilderment is their tribute, deferring instinct
and the hard-scrabble ahead for this moment.

They can, and will, and do move on, eventually,
leaving a sundial's memory of shadow, moon face,
the intimate concentrics rimmed in bark and moss--
still further out, slow ripples in the air.

12 Feb 13
 
Small Birds on a Newly-Felled Tree

"When we try to pick out anything by itself 
we find that it is bound fast, by a thousand 
invisible cords that cannot be broken, 
to everything in the universe."
    -John Muir

They alight on the fresh-cut stump--
flat altar to the space left behind.

It is gone in a day, to the growl of saws,
the body stacked in a pyramid of trunk-cords,

neater than house-fire ashes or earthquake rubble.
But are they mourning? Do they know tree-death

from tree-life, sap-infused from cinder block?
Orating on the dais in a language all their own,

bewilderment is their tribute, deferring instinct
and the hard-scrabble ahead for just this moment.

They can, and will, and do move on, eventually,
leaving a sundial's memory of shadow, moon-blank

face, the intimate concentrics rimmed in bark 
and moss--continuing as ripples through the air.

14 Feb 13

Of course we drink to forget.
And is not death the final amnesia?
Lap up the black water of Lethe, kiss 
away the dreamless hours, drowning
two fingers at a time. It takes
only inches, says the magician
behind the bar, stops our heart
with a white bird in one sleeve,
diamond queen in the other. Is that 
your trick, turning into another man's 
invisible woman for awhile? Even
cockroaches know how to seek out the
shadows at the flick of a bulb.

17 Feb 13

energy, chorist, plan, heckle, otter, pelt, gradation, numeration, aye, project, row, mankind, grey-back, whip, jive, tug, love-letter

Happy Hour

Of course we drink to forget.
And is not death the final amnesia?
Lap up the black water of Lethe, kiss 
away the dreamless hours, drowning
two fingers at a time. It takes
only inches, says the magician
behind the bar, who stops our heart
with a white bird in one sleeve,
diamond queen in the other. Is that 
your trick, turning into another man's 
invisible woman for awhile? Is that 
your joke, a chorister's drone,
the low, persistent energy of sound?
Is this your plan, to heckle the
peanuts and line the tip jar
like a canoe draped in otter pelts?
Have you done the numbers? Can you
make the grade? Say, "Aye" to the
project of your mislabeled life,
and row your little boat forward
into the grey-backed drudgery 
we call "mankind", whipping the swirl
of your gin with an impaled olive,
jiving and tugging at your sleeves
like the crisp fold of a mislaid
love-letter. Of course we drink.
We forget. It takes only inches.

17 March 13

What have I been wanting to write about?
Who wants to write in me, under my skin?

We moved in, one limb at a time.
The walls are damp, and the pipework
bangs, the crossbeams are drilled with lice.
The tiles of the roof are padded with moss,
the bricks blood-damp, the paving stones weep.
I sit at the kitchen table, snap twigs 
with my thumbs like chicken bones, peel 
clementines in a long spiral strip.

Now the questions are gathering dust.
Now the willow sleeps, bent double,
her fingertips dangling in water.

I feel dry, though am wet to the touch.

like the nostrils of kittens, like
the corners of the eye. The corneas.
Cornea borealis. Where the hell am I
going with my writing these days?

her fingertips dangling in water.

Erasure poem from Joan Didion prose:

Of course it might have been some other city, had circumstances been different and the time been different and had I been different, might have been Paris or Chicago or even San Francisco, but because I am talking about myself I am talking here about New York. That first night I opened my window on the bus into town and watched for the skyline, but all I could see were the wastes of Queens and big signs that said MIDTOWN TUNNEL THIS LANE and then a flood of summer rain (even that seemed remarkable and exotic, for I had come out of the West where there was no summer rain), and for the next three days I sat wrapped in blankets in a hotel room air conditioned to 35 degrees and tried to get over a cold and a high fever. It did not occur to me to call a doctor, because I knew none, and although it did occur to me to call the desk and ask that the air conditioner be turned off, I never called, because I did not know how much to tip whoever might come—was anyone ever so young? I am here to tell you that someone was. All I could do during those years was talk long-distance to the boy I already knew I would never marry in the spring. I would stay in New York, I told him, just six months, and I could see the Brooklyn Bridge from my window. As it turned out the bridge was the Triborough, and I stayed eight years.


Of course it might have been,
had circumstances, the time, and I
been different. I am talking first,
opened window on the bus, the skyline,
Midtown Tunnel, summer rain for the
next three days, high fever young.
All I could do was talk.

But what strikes one most about the Strip chapels, with their wishing wells and stained-glass paper windows and their artificial bouvardia, is that so much of their business is by no means a matter of simple convenience, of late-night liaisons between show girls and baby Crosbys. Of course there is some of that. (One night about eleven o'clock in Las Vegas I watched a bride in an orange minidress and masses of flame-colored hair stumble from a Strip chapel on the arm of her bridegroom, who looked the part of the expendable nephew in movies like Miami Syndicate. "I gotta get the kids," the bride whimpered. "I gotta pick up the sitters, I gotta get to the midnight show." "What you gotta get," the bridegroom said, opening the door of a Cadillac Coupe de Ville and watching her crumple on the seat, "is sober.") But Las Vegas seems to offer something other than "convenience"; it is merchandising "niceness," the facsimile of proper ritual, to children who do not know how else to find it, how to make the arrangements, how to do it "right." All day and evening long on the Strip, one sees actual wedding parties, waiting under the harsh lights at a crosswalk standing uneasily in the parking lot of the Frontier while the photographer hired by The Little Church of the West ("Wedding Place of the Stars") certifies the occasion, takes the picture: the bride in a veil and white satin pumps, the bridegroom usually a white dinner iacket, and even an attendant or two, a sister or best friend in hot-pink peau de soie, a flirtation veil, a carnation nosegay. "When I Fall in Love It Will Be Forever," the organist plays, and then a few bars of Lohengrin. The mother cries; the stepfather, awkward in his role, invites the chapel hostess to join them for a drink at the Sands. The hostess declines with a professional smile; she has already transferred her interest to the group waiting outside. One bride out, another in, and again the sign goes up on the chapel door: "One moment please– Wedding."

Strip Chapels

Wishing wells and stained-glass paper windows.
No means a matter of simple convenience.
(I watched a bride in an orange minidress
stumble on the arm of her bridegroom,
the facsimile of proper ritual, wedding parties
under the lights at a crosswalk, standing uneasily,
flirtation veil, a carnation nosegay.) The mother cries.
The sign goes up "One moment please--Wedding."

Paduasoy

Curtains, napkins, tablecloth, dress
wicks up red wine, repels dripped wax.

What's good for the bridesmaid is good
for the rest. Candlemas, table grapes

crêpe de chine best. Puffy-sleeved,
hourglass, empire waist. Bustier,

nosegay, v-line velvet vest. Sunset
over Vegas in an orange mini-dress.

Ritual, flirtation veil, awkward guest.
Tissue held close to the bride's mum's chest.

26 March 13

A Robot's Understanding of Friendship

27 March 13

One is special because they are specialised,
able to make the shell-beads you cannot.
By swapping your dried Caribou meat,
which is an everyday part of your life,
for strings of polished abalone and conch,
you add iridescence to your worldview,
and the bead-maker gains tasty strength.
Each tribe profits from the trading, each
goes away with "a bargain" in his own language--
and so they meet, at equinox and solstice,
where the forest joins the black-sand beach,
pile driftwood into bonfires and celebrate,
join hands with their strage counterparts,
hair and clothes and piercings different,
not-quite relations, not-quite family,
bringers of gifts, and barterers, who
come to be called "friends", a category
of fellow man somewhere between the ones
we try to kill, and the ones we struggle
to birth, raise and protect from killers.
Even now, though the stakes have been reduced
to a single glowing click on an imagined button,
and instead of goods, we now swap sensations,
the economic dynamics remain--I let you in,
you let me in, just a little more than
perhaps Machiavelli would have advised us,
and in this minuscule enlargement of two 
barely existent realms made of thought
parading as words and images over the sockets
that once held our eyes, we join what used
to be our hands across the flickering glow
of a fire so cold, it must be man-made.

28 March 13

Revised above.

29 March 13

1 April 13

Bruges

The drone strike leveled the town,
and then the Vikings moved in, wearing
horned helmets and body armour, dogs
sniffing the ground for explosives ahead.

There was nobody left but me, and I hid
where I could watch, beneath the rubble.
Any wounded who moved, they clubbed
to death, not wanting to waste ammunition.

No slaves. No concubines. They silenced
every moan, scanning the corpses for warmth.
At the edge of the forest, a dark shape
paced on its fours, watching over them.

A raider lifted his crossbow, taking aim,
but someone made him lower it, and from then
the bear was protected by rank, by custom,
and it watched them into nightfall.

They piled the bodies and burned them,
stripping off jewelry and anything edible.
Sometimes a younger recruit would vomit,
spit, and then resume his undertaking.

They radioed in the good news to the oar ship:
beachhead established. They brought in supplies,
put up banners, and drank into unconsciousness.
I crawled into the forest. The bear kept watch.

Amuse-Bouche Nostalgique

A dollop of cream from your own
mother's milk, seasoned with tears 
from the first girl you kissed,
garnished with coarse-cut parsley,
served in the shell of a snail.

Lint from your favourite old jumper
dotted with grains from your childhood
sandbox, placed on your luckiest penny,
soaked in rum and set alight, sprinkled
with a powder of cinnamon that sparks.

fingertips drifting through water

Challenge: write poem to Robert Peake the Elder

5 May 13


To My Elder, Robert Peake

"Every portrait that is painted with feeling 
is a portrait of the artist, not of the sitter."
-Oscar Wilde 

"Know thyself" the ancients advised
long before Wikipedia. You made images
of icons, made danger your business
by painting the princess who became Winter's 
Queen, the prince who blazed into infamy,
one hand stroking the notable features,
the other rubbing blemishes to a glow,
giving the public a profile to recognise,
suitors an image to scar in the mind,
enemies an opponent worthy to despise--
smoothing and lightening, long before
Photoshop, a picture of nobility half-
studied in the impatient gaze of human
sitters, half made in gathered symbols
poured over the comely calves of gentle-
folk in gleaming silk, pearls dotted
with a single hair of liquid gypsum,
making each oyster's teardrop shine,
lace collars laced with linseed oil,
washed into translucence like a dream.

But we have no image of you, if you 
even were one man, held up by apprentices,
long before "l'art pour l'art" gave
us permission to make self-portraits.
Yet whenever I look for myself, I find
you, in the smirking red lips of a lady,
the high foreheads plunging into narrow
noses, unmarred by frown lines, eyebrows
raised enquiringly, hand-on-hip or hand-
on-chest, close to the sword, the heart,
each making the art of dominion seem 
effortless. I ego-surf my way through
glowing pages, my own head shots mixed
with Jacobites and Tudors, Bohemians
fancying themselves as Grecian deities,
enmeshed and immortalised by a computer's
comprehension of same symbols in same 
order, nothing more, while faces real
and half-imagined peer back from 
beneath a banner of alphabet letters
I sometimes like to think of as "myself".



9 May 13

Namesake
    (to Robert Peake the Elder, c. 1551–1619)

Whenever I look for myself, I find you, 
in the smirking red lips of a lady,
the high foreheads plunging into narrow
noses, unmarred by frown lines, eyebrows
raised enquiringly, hand-on-hip or hand-
on-chest, close to the sword, the heart,
each making the art of dominion seem 
effortless. You made danger your business
by painting the princess who became Winter's 
Queen, the prince who blazed into infamy,
one hand stroking the notable features,
the other rubbing blemishes to a glow.
You gave the public a profile to recognise,
suitors an image to scar in the mind,
enemies an opponent worthy to despise--
smoothing and lightening, your own icons
of nobility half-studied in the impatient 
gaze of human sitters, half made in gathered 
symbols poured over the calves of gentle-
folk in gleaming silk, pearls dotted
with a single hair of liquid gypsum,
making each oyster's teardrop shine,
lace collars laced with linseed oil,
washed into translucence like a dream.
I ego-surf my way through glowing pages, 
my own head shots mixed with Jacobites 
and Tudors, Bohemians fancying themselves 
reclining on chaise longue as Grecian deities,
enmeshed and immortalised by a computer's
comprehension of same symbols in same 
order, nothing more, while faces real
and half-imagined peer back from 
beneath a banner two ordinary words
I used to like to think of as "myself".



Namesake
    (to Robert Peake the Elder, c. 1551–1619)

Whenever I look for myself, I find you, 
in the smirking red lips of nobility,
high foreheads unmarred by frown lines, 
plunging into narrow noses, eyebrows
raised enquiringly, hand-on-hip or hand-
on-chest, close to the sword, the heart,
each making the art of dominion seem 
effortless. You made danger your business,
painting the princess who became Winter's 
Queen, the prince who blazed into infamy,
one hand stroking the notable features,
the other rubbing blemishes to a glow.
You gave the public a profile to recognise,
suitors an image to scar in the mind,
enemies an opponent worthy to despise--
smoothing and lightening, your own figures
of nobility half-studied in the impatient 
gaze of human sitters, half evoked in symbols 
poured over the comely calves of gentle-
folk in gleaming silk, pearls dotted
with a single hair of liquid gypsum
(making each oyster's teardrop shine),
lace collars laced with linseed oil
(washed into translucence like a dream).
I ego-surf my way through glowing pages, 
my own head shots mixed with Jacobites 
and Tudors, Bohemians fancying themselves
as Grecian deities, enmeshed and immortalised 
by a computer's comprehension of same letters 
in same  order, nothing more, while your
faces peer back from beneath the two words
I used to like to think of as "myself".

19 May 13

Amuse-Bouche

A dollop of cream from your own
mother's milk, seasoned with tears
from the first girl you kissed,
garnished with coarse-cut parsley,
served in the shell of a snail.

Lint from your favourite old jumper
dotted with grains from your childhood
sandbox, placed on your luckiest penny,
soaked in rum and a roiling blue flame,
sparkling with cinnamon gunpowder.

Shaved wax from your preschool crayons
to make your lips curl up at the edges,
nettles from the banks of the pollywog pond
to brand the tongue with slime's fascination,
all arranged in a small favourite lunchpail.

Yes of course for desert we have madeleines,
to dip in a tea made of vapour and dust,
sweet-smelling, like the home of your elderly aunt,
which dissolve upon contact and waking. Go on. 
Have another. You will never be full.

22 May 13

Britton, South Daktoa

"F" is for future, and just enough fame
to break the hold of the township's circle,
be a foot soldier, ferrier, corn cob
in a kilt. One day you will tear
all the patches right off, and be somebody,
"S" is for somebody: anything, hoped for.
Be strong, brave and good all at once,
and face-forward. "F" is for facing
the music, the camera, looking up eye-
to-eye with the maker of portraits,
showing off your new teeth, ribbons, buggy,
showing one for each letter of the alphabet.

Learning the Letters
    Britton, South Dakota, 1939

"F" is for future, bright as a lens,
bubbles in the scrubbing basin,
thin as the skin on aunt Agnes's hand,
the breakable surface of a pollywog egg.

It's no shame to be poor, but a shame
to be dirty, since soap is cheap
and water is free, and hats last a lifetime
for those who can't afford the ribbons and pomade.

One day you will be gee-whiz gone,
just like "T", like "that", the last
Cracker Jack in the box, the last farrier
in a town full of town cars--the touchdown
you scored, the gloves, plaques, and blue ribbons
boxed up for safekeeping, which is never quite
safe enough. 
                Outside, it is bright. It is "B"
and you are abuzz at the start of things,
though you "H" and mother says he who "hesitates"
is "L", which you were once, at the fair,
"lost" in a petrified forest of trousers and skirts,
and will be again job-seeking in Des Moines
or Detroit, the hot, big "D" of Dallas, looking 
to make a name that will make the town paper.

There is always a way, when you square up 
straight, "F" is for facing the music, the camera, 
looking up eye-to-eye as your portrait
gets taken, showing yes, you were "S"
for somebody, looking, direct and uncertain
down the long barrel of whatever is ahead.

27 May 13

As If

It is
too late
in the year
for such weeping,
I tell you, be still,
the winds of our sighing
to have left the hills in disarray
and it is late, now, for us
to be singing like this,
undressed, together,
speaking quietly,
as if to forget
just how late.
It is just. 
How late?
As if to forget
speaking quietly,
undressed together,
to be singing like this.
And it is late, now, for us,
to have left the hills in disarray,
the winds of our sighing.
I tell you, be still
for such weeping.
In the year
too late.
It is.
Too late
in the year.
For such weeping
I tell you. Be still,
the winds of our sighing.
To have left the hills in disarray.
And it is late. Now, for us
to be singing like this,
undressed, together,
speaking quietly.
As if. To forget.
Just. How late
it is.

4 June 13


Listening to Sibelius on Your Birthday
	(to J.V.P, b. Jan. 24 2006, d. Jan. 27 2006)

Out of the pools of winter, skating light,
an apple on the table lit like a new planet,
the transom parcelling darkness,
our kitchen under water before dawn.

Four lines ago, we spoke of winter.
Four years ago, we would not recognize
ourselves, under the ice, lip-veins bluing,
eyelids frozen open in disbelief.

And who should say we now resemble
ourselves, blinking in the early light
of a long winter passing casually?
We have come to believe we know

what the flicker of dust on a page,
the wide scowl of sun through fence
slats, and all the swimming minnows
on our porch step, cast by the prism,

actually mean. What does breathing
mean, except--you're still alive?
But a held breath does not mean death,
any more than lamplight is the sun.

Where do we go from here?--the age-
old question. Shall I answer us
in riddle, or in rhyme? Shall I grope
through music blindly? Here are notes--

the bold and soaring G-chords, stitched
to a heart, stitched, in turn, to a
shirtsleeve, on the outside, exposed
to the chill of a latecomer spring.

Take us away, Sibelius, you have earned it.
The darkness recedes one icicle at a time.
Our breath becomes transparent again,
footfalls cease to crackle and crunch.

We go, from here, as quickly or slowly
as thawing requires. We go from here,
through darkness, dripping good intention,
easing ourselves into the smouldering dawn.

5 June 13

Use as many of the following words as you like/can:

inscribed, canine, blight, fang, scorch, tonal, mote, authentic, martyr, pair, spider, brother, parasite, scarf, night-walker, strain, purr, twirl, guy, council, gown

Include a friendly person from your childhood
Include an unusual smell

Watching Mr. Rogers in East LA

The streets are scorched and webbed with tar.
Your brother has become a night-walker.
Your mother is a martyr at her stove,
scarf twirled round her head like a bruja.

Mr. Rogers' jumpers smell of cardamom.
Girls names inscribed on telephone poles
with bent nails, sharp as canine fangs
tell what they do to guys for money.

The wires overhead tone out their tune.
The spider on the window is your friend.
Dust motes fall through the quartered light
as you strain to see the glare-cast screen.

He takes his shoes off, counting out the pair,
and sings the song you know about feeling blue.
Then he looks through the screen, at your life,
and declares, "I like you just the way you are."

11 June 13

two poems, each max. 40 lines, typed on A4, with your membership number, not name and address, at the foot of each

On Regent's Canal
    after Hieronymus Bosch

Wench up the lock gate, the water ascends.
I, too, have been seeking my level. The flat-
bottomed boat I have dubbed Vita Mea thumps
the bank in rhythm to the channeled flow.

These, and other signs, are everywhere, waiting
to be deciphered from dots and dashes into words,
and words into action--the trickiest part--
as all translators of well-meant intentions know.

Gate by gate, I descend to the river, tributary
by tributary I crawl my way closer to the sea.
Cup a hand to the frigid water, slurp up a taste 
of motherly salt in increased concentration.

I print my name on the life-preserving ring,
try my head through the hole, just in case.
Oil films the surface, a thin collodion mask
on which the sun prints snapshots of fish.

Dinghy and rowboat, barge and single-mast sail,
come with me, down the stairwell, step by step.
A dog on the bank tries to warn of what's ahead, 
but drunk on the ripples in sunlight, we float on.

# # #

Burst Pipe

The house is transformed. A black Tudor beam
becomes the lip of the waterfall, our tapestry
a sponge. At the bottom of this newly-made pond,
wood flooring. All we need now are fish. Lily pads.

Who knew we lived just a few hundred litres away
from inhabiting a painting by Renée Magritte?
It could be called  "The Aquarium". No? Too
on-the-nose? My slippers have reached saturation.

We found the stop-cock, but the water kept coming,
remembering its origins in dark groundwater.
Send brooms, mops, wet vacs and dehumidifiers.
The scale inhibitor was not inhibited enough.

Now the wallpaper blisters like a burn victim.
Seams in the ceiling well up with sentiment.
I am building a lifeboat in the conservatory,
planing down good cedar, watching for rainbows.

18 June 13

The Rouchomovsky Skeleton
c. 1896, recently sold at Sotheby's for $365,000

http://www.retronaut.com/2013/06/the-rouchomovsky-skeleton/

So like us, we want to chat with him,
this tiny human skeleton in gold. Perched
on the edge of his velvet-lined coffin,
his jaw swings open like an easy smile.
Then laid to rest, the jaw is set, hand-
bones crossed where his heart would be,
as if clutching a bouquet of flowers,
determination filling his sockets.

What does it say about vanity to replicate
our own human bones in golden filigree?
A Roman general once forced his slave to march
behind him in his triumphant hour, whispering
above the roar of the crowd: "You will die."
Unparalleled, irreplaceable, this little status
symbol reminds us that our status also dies.
Now who is laughing? The heirloom lives on.

Petite memento mori, minuscule vanitas,
we cover you with a lid depicting Death
shouldering his scythe, leading a harvest
of souls into a mist-choked Netherworld--
or is it smoke and fire? An ocean wave? 
The ground is littered with skulls, 
the border alternates roses and thorns,
infants laughing and crying, both grotesque.

The headboard depicts a lyre, paintbrush, book,
and above it a baby gargoyle laughs in delight 
at the delicate craft of the bare human hand.
The footing is the shield, helmet, and halberd,
and here a tiny thorn-wracked face contorts,
reflecting the art of war, carefully wrought
in precious metal, gurning back the consequence
when that same hand takes on the gauntlet.

19 June 13

So like us, we want to own him, the priceless 
man-made skeleton and his box. But was the slave
pouring humility into the general's ear? Or was
his reminder somehow to make the moment sweeter,
more precious for its transience, the echoing cheers--
the perfect pure-gold ribs, kneecaps, and teeth--
the chance to spend a lifetime's earnings cheating
death for once by taking it up, carefully, in hand?

Use as many of the following words as you like/can:

What Spider Brings

Night-walker, my old friend
do not strain yourself with talking
do not twirl your cane
or claw at your hospital gown
our man Spider will be here soon 
to make the old blight go away
and you will purr again
down the backs of council flats,
part martyr and part parasite
pissing out the metal tang of methadone
in the arc of a canine's fang,
inscribing the scorch-mark initials
from your authentic birth certificate
onto a pair of breeze blocks
before zipping up your dignity.

19 July 13

The Avuncular Variations
    For Fergus, Age 3

I. "I Don't Love Holly"

I don't love the points on her shine
of green tongue, how she barks like a dog
then threatens with a lawnmower's whine.

She hides her prickles under rotting logs
which give under foot like a boobytrap
plunging you into a sudden dark fog

smearing your leg with red beads of sap
snapping the twigs of your bold outward face
in a move so sudden you can't fight back

when the gutters find tears to embrace,
etching in the not-love of this once-unknown foe
until "Holly" is everything you want to erase

but also to see, and to spot, and to know,
asking "Holly?" at every furled leaf that we pass
crying "Holly!" with each stick-sword blow.

20 July 13

21 July 13

27 July 13

III. Measures and Sums

How many kilometers are you? And how can just
one meter be plenty? I thought I was three:
but was that years, feet, or dimensions? Take
forty-three and seventy-one, is that a lot?
Is it so much to ask for two ones, not a pair,
for another another when just one won't do?
Which is the biggest piece, the tallest glass?
I want to be the more one, growing in my sleep.

28 July 13

IV. Sippy Cup

I find it in the cupboard after he's gone
a little duck-billed drinking vessel
flightless, comical, with a gurgling song
pausing for rehydration when we'd wrestle.

Helping him pop off the top made me feel strong
and filing it up with milk meant story time
he'd negotiate the tale and I would play along
sipping down a sleeping potion while I rhymed.

Briefly, it was my nickname in graduate school
when at a dinner, I tipped an icy glass too high
streaming the contents onto my shirt in a pool.
"Prefer a sippy cup?" asked a student on the sly.

I'd gladly trade the high things I have learned
to hear again the tweeting of that bird.

II. Ivy

She clings to the trees in summertime.
Paired with Holly, makes an English song.
The only Ivy in Australia, gold-blonde.
Five centimeters taller, friend and rival.

Passing green creepers that mum called the same,
his face spun into a perplexed kind of knot,
knowing girl before plant led his thoughts,
so he picked an Acacia bud and called it "Louis".

Second Adam, naming the world as you see fit.
Redraft the story we know on a new continent
drive out snakes, find springs, make us content
to know the world anew through knowing you.

30 July 13

America, Its Elements

I. Fire

Laughter makes the stars explode.
Warm nights, the smell of grill-smoke,
match heads and kerosene. We have always 
been a people in love with flames.

The stars explode with laughter.
Roman candles fizz a purity of light.
Strawberries, blueberries and cream
salute "the Republic, for which it stands."

The sky is blown apart with laughing stars,
bottle rockets and tracer bullets,
the black smoke of anti-aircraft flack,
tin toys and pinwheels spewing sparks.

Laugh, and the bombs laugh with you,
the stars laugh, too, the flyovers,
brass parades, gun smoke, and brimstone, 
rockets' red glare and hissing fuse.

II. Water

A splash to prime the pump,
then get to know the sound
of water coming up--
in the pipework, gasping
with each handle-pump.
In the throat of the well,
the bucket rings out.
A gauze-faced man contorts
against the straps
his body crescendoes
a song against death.
My sister turns blue
with a grape in her throat.
Nurses jab adrenaline
into my spider-veined arm.
We become the colour of water,
we return the sky to the sea.
Rain comes down the gutter.
Our lungs fill up with tears.

III. Wind

" a dark wind had been rising toward me 
from somewhere deep in my future"
    -Albert Camus, /The Stranger/

The wind at night consoles me
with melodies so frightening
I gather them into my sleep.

We raid the air for meaning, 
circulate the breeze of opinion,
the high winds of feeling,

whipped up like stiff frosting,
pummeled into a malleable shape,
aerated like a well-made fire.

Beside the stream, the reeds
are calling the name of the wind, 
the broken reeds, their open mouths

make a sound for this emptiness, whistling
as it blows through my transparent skin.

IV. Earth

Only the tusks of the village remain.
The stars frown down with their
penitent rage, their penitent power
while the moon changes masks
at her pleasure. The starlight remains.

The azans complain their cinder notes,
a smoky haze in which to drown
the boat-like stretchers floating by,
upon a street of sky, screeching
with the traffic of the dying, dying dead.

Only the night can cover this bruise,
the game of chance, we played, this game
a ruse in rosy glasses bending light
into the windows of the soil-stained
cacophony. The sound sounds, a stain.

Remains of tissue, remains of bone, a stone,
the walls of wells sunk in, the water
black as blood, a bruise within the circle
of the town, its walls torn down by fire,
and by water, and the fissure gushing red.

I give you my word, absurd, my hand, my
both of them to have and hold, the bold
inscription on the turrets of my tomb: here
was a witness, pitiless, stone-cold, crumbled
sand, a man caught in a sandstorm, storming.


11 August 13

Ursus Americanus (Noir)

Black hair. Red claws. That's all
you need to know. She left
the cubs a long time ago,
and now all she wants is a man
to drink gin and play snooker.
She keeps a gun in her purse
and two ex lovers in jail,
signs her letters with a kiss
and a dab of cheap perfume.
She knows how to use a letter opener,
walk upright like a lady,
forage berries in the forest,
bandage a gunshot wound,
claw her way out of the trunk 
of a speeding car, and roll away.
She's my kind of gal. She hunts
back when she's being hunted,
growls obscenities when hit
by a tranquilizer dart.
She noses through the city's garbage.
She knows you by your smell.

13 August 13

The Flies

Why the long face? I'll tell you.
The maggots in my shit become flies.
The flies seek out my weeping eyes.
They cluster in silver-black tears.

I have scavenged the field to its stubs.
The dust whips my face, causing tears
the flies excavate my nostrils and ears
seeking sweat, saliva, mucus, and blood.

I toss my head for a moment's relief,
spraying them off like droplets of tar,
they cloud, congeal, return to mar
my face with switchblade wings.

Why the long face? You tell me.
Do you cry out for a breath of wind
to blow the flies from your skin,
carrying the scent of your mother?

15 August 13

I have scoured the field down to dust
while the files pore over my face
knitting a veil of silver-black lace
to cover my eyes from the day.

19 August 13

The Curse

I touched the golden goose,
she laid a rotten egg.
Come kiss my leper's lips  
while I spin gold into straw.

An Anglicised American in Scotland

Call me "mate", call me "pal",
asking "friend or foe?" in a glance
even the stones want to know
what kind of shoes they scrape.

Speak gentlemanly words, but carry
a dagger in your sock, another
under an ancestor's ribs, forged 
on an anvil in London.

Don't ask where I am from
since there is no answer
that will meet your approval.
Like you, I am an outsider.

Mornings with J.B.

Drink O.J. from a dusty mason jar
then tongue jam from a crumpet's spongy scar
twice woken by the wake, pealing at starboard
tugging at the anchor, the morning's pull-cord.

Stay slipped, you ride a rail-less sleeping car,
invert vessel, with one loose clapper aboard
tinkling willow plates, tipping port-holes ajar
swaying the sound bow in place where it's moored.

Afloat, a bell jar chimes against the lock,
a boat becomes the poet's most alarming clock.

1 Oct 13

Use at least 10 of these in a poem, along with a style of architecture, a mood and the name of a famous individual.

Tea Break for the Judge

Antique protest against the sexless world,
he tips a cup and draws breath through a fag,
our malem of the painted toe and curvy thigh,
an eikon-length away from fish-netted flesh
this greyback of the congregation, lip-stick row,
baroque in a 60s cabaret style, flirtatiously
indifferent to our Zeus atop his wobbly chair--
the actuality of hot refreshment, tea and smoke,
prescriptive of his counter-claim to objectivity,
straight as the buttons lining his cuff, smooth
as the kerchief perched in his breast pocket--
or is it a love-letter, stitched in white silk?
Tug it to find out if our man for all mankind
has sewn it to the inner pump of his placid being,
since a man made his clothes, but his clothes
now make him, as if spotlit on the cafe floor,
the still point of the gawking world, a vortex,
skipping heartbeats before the haka, pageant,
strut and hoot, he sips and thinks of what to come,
draws a puff and snuffs his cigarette, muttering
a single word in smoke, something we'll never know.

Painting the Eiffel Tower
    http://blog.europeana.eu/2013/10/painting-the-eiffel-tower/

Doubler for a body, transposed shadow of a man,
your pumpkin head against the skyline, cap doffed
cavalier, carnic sovereign of a realm in steel, you
side-step the railing, pop out for some air eight
hundred feet above, and this is your profession.
It snowballed up from climbing trees to towers,
parsing the spiked horizon for your next nutty
stunt against the relict sky, unattended by ropes,
offering your olive branch limbs to the war effort,
venting your spleen like Baudelaire, en plein air
among smokestacks, not haystacks, this is your art--
to make us uneasy by rattling your wide metal cage,
as you quiver like a mousse in a cold metal bowl,
we see the man of you, the outline of your splat,
how all that industry built is made to crack what's
hard and bruise what's soft, you float the middle,
Mr. Egg Yolk, rolling your eyes for the camera.

29 Oct 13

Jellied Eels

We see ourselves through you, sweet
pickled blob, sea-smelling delicacy,
swimming into nets set East of Tower Bridge,
giving yourselves up, fair cop, for our supper.

Watery allotment, the sea-bound Thames,
scraped for cockles since memory began,
your long fast swimmers mimic the current
dashing like morse code along the wires.

Part salty fish, part tangy aspic, green 
glazing for the bombed-to-shell cathedrals
alms for the clever, the slippery quick,
stretched ghost, half visible to the eye.

Part meat, part skin, part isinglass,
fleshy translucence, oiled quivering pane.

in all dimensions, spreading its arms.
Here is the city's grit-flecked embrace.
"...been dying since the day I was born."

Part your lips, and breathe in slowly
drawing up the sweet, unhealthy air.

Smoke Ring

I. New Scotland Yard

The ghost remains, black shadows cast
on brick, mist over wet cobblestones.
Hard Road is playing in the pub next door: 
"There must be something in the air..."

The tailpipe of a Hackney carriage
respires to the beat of a diesel drum.
In from the glowing tip, it lulls
then curls from a working girls' nostrils.

Visible, the air seems at ease, lounging
in all dimensions, spreading its arms.
Here is the city's grit-flecked embrace.
"...been dying since the day I was born."

Part your lips, and breathe in slowly
drawing up the sweet, unhealthy air.

II. Brick Lane

Drawing up the sweet, unhealthy air
from sizzling woks, flat bubbling crepes
we ogle falafel, smirk at t-shirt sayings,
finger the dyed silks and leather bags.

Huguenot chapel turned Russian synagogue,
now a Bangladeshi Mosque, the moon and star
winks down at our worldly commerce
from the smokestack of its silver minaret.

Every brick a different shape and shade,
pecked by the acrid air, specked with colour
from a rattling can, even graffiti is for sale--
"Street art area: pay a fee or close your eyes."

Burning ghee and mustard oil, hissing paint.
Close both eyes, and follow from the scent.

III. Docklands

Close both eyes, and follow from the scent

7 November 13

III. Docklands

Close both eyes, and follow from the scent
of sea grass, salt rope, barnacled wood.
Oil lamps puff, pipe down their silvery light
tusk-like, whale ribs embrace a building site.

Spire of Narwhal, great barge up-ended,
sea monsters rise up smooth, in cubic glass--
the streets scrubbed clean of tidal mud,
the Thames runs clear as lymph without its blood

New brick, poured cement, transparent swim bladders,

8 November 13

cranes pick the horizon where gulls pecked the sand.

French perfume goes waft-about when tester gets spil't.

From a top-story balcony, an underwriter plans his grave
while admiring the skyline, its rich amber haze.

IV. Kite Hill

While admiring the skyline, its rich amber haze,
we watch cloud shadows skate uneven ground.
"You take the rough with the smooth" someone resigns,
winding in his kite string, dragging it aground.

We want to be held. We want to be held aloft.

9 November 13

We want to see ourselves squinting up into the sun,
inspect nests at the tops of the trees, catch air
under under our arms, and tug the string for fun.

We have been looked down upon all day. Now we look
like the disembodied flag of hope everyman longs
to salute, a sail without its ship, we surge up
and dip, catching the breeze in a humming song.

The moment is so simple, even the grass wants to smile.
Look to the horizon with the clear eyes of a child.

IV. Blackheath

While admiring the skyline, its rich amber haze,
sun scalds the mist into an interpretation of light
reminding us that the ocean is never far, reminding us,
like Turner, like Messiaen, in saturated tones.

Street lamps peer over us, considering our gait, where once
the gibbet posts dangled a peepshow of bodies into the gloam,
the open air turned treacherous, picking from rag to bone,
betraying the flesh to bake and rot its caramelised smell.

Beneath our dew-specked feet, the earth also grinds away
at the dark plague pits, chewing hundreds to tar,
sealed away like embers in the furnace of the heath,
the pocked bodies smelt, give off their obsidian glow.

Over the vale, the mist descends in sherbet and blues.
Beneath the iridescent surface, something darker stirs.

V. Home Office, Croydon

Beneath the iridescent surface, something darker stirs,
as the milk steamer froths up my second latte this hour,
sighing visibly upward into the air-conditioned atmosphere
of what could be an airport lounge, international terminal.

The man in a turban beside me taps his pen on the plastic seat,
three short, three long, three short, then a breath,
while his daughters in the designated play area build homes
from brightly coloured bricks, stacking with practiced care.

The clerks do likewise: red passport, blue passport, green passport, 
brown, shuffling my paperwork in front of me with casual cheer,
jobsworth elves in Santa's workshop who have already seen the list
of who gets presents, who gets coal--fate, their red-suited employer.

My number flashes up, and I transmit a tight-lipped smile.
"Should I stay or should I go?" sings The Clash inside my mind.

cosmetic amoeba cougar balcony phonograph caribou gone rotten answer absently
bittersweet crunch positive absentness hell blankly coercion confuse healer paper

VI. Clapham Junction

"Should I stay or should I go?" sings The Clash inside my mind,
while the train tweets it's doors-closing sound.
"I'd beg for some forgiveness, but begging's not my business..."
as The Squeeze shuts the doors with a rubberized thud.

Here it all comes together, and here it splits wide apart,
as the train glides the fanning delta on a branch line.
"This was never our destination," explains a dad to son,
tugging him through the slipstream to Olympia.

The electric rail sparks, the steam long puffed-out,
the matrix pages points, the people wrinkle-browed
pace up and down the junction like spectral counterparts,
flat-capped, coal-smudged, trying to get somewhere.

the train is gone, the moment past, but still
the ghost remains, black shadows cast.

19 Nov 13

Only the tourists wait for him, unsure which way

Municipal Water

I think of you when I turn the tap, the alleged
seven others who have drunk this water before me,
or so the urban lore has run since Tudor times.

You must have been further up the Thames, the chain,
the lollipop lady in Richmond, ushering her ducklings,
takes breakfast after the school bell, sips Earl Grey.

The boy pocking his mother's carpet with muddy cleats
drops orange powder into a glass, a jet from the tap,
swirls it and gulps it, repeats, then up to the loo.

None of you could have been in diapers, either infants
or those near the end of their life--each was a vessel
through which this human wetness, shared water passed.

Some part of you is part of me now, drinking it.
The river is cold, but the body is warm, a fact
that makes the mammal in us wary, makes the kettle

an ally in the mist-damp globe of this floating island.
There was no moisture in my childhood, except in cactus.
An open river of sewage etched through sandstone.

Standing at the railing on Blackfriar's Bridge, I lean
into the mud-scented wind like a ship's figurehead.
So much water. So many people. We thirst for our selves.

The (All-New and Improved) Green Man

Only the foreign tourists wait for him, uncertain
which way to look for cars, they stand at attention
before the red man, snapping gum and photographs.

He is nothing like he used to be, omnipresent as CCTV,
reduced to angles, posing mid-stride in his armoured 
black box, like a celebrity in a limousine.

His predecessor still leers down from cornices, pub signs,
a face for mischief, cheeky as resin, common as grass--
vines flare from his nostrils, buds crown his hairline.

One gives the starting signal to a pack of Italian teenagers;
the other drives the vita through a sapling in Hyde Park.
Green is for beginning, and our man, our men, begin it.

3 December 13

The blue man is a clown, the white man stole the brown man's
land, but the Old Green Man goes on reclaiming the pavement,
rotting floor boards, spearing up scions through the tarmac.

Go will keep on going, long after we have crossed the street.
Old Green man's buddy Neptune inches up the waterline, cracking
ice in his teeth, speckling the beach huts with barnacles.

Old Green man fights the chainsaw with pungent sap,
catches boots on looped tree roots, sends birds to bomb
the shit out of the inside of upturned hard hats.

New green says you can't stop progress, and signs the paperwork.
He obeys all posted notices, even when they conflict, switches off
before curfew, says he knows his limits, the clean lines where he ends.

4 Dec 13

cosmetic amoeba cougar balcony phonograph caribou gone rotten answer absently

The Knowledge

Bright as a hurricane lantern, flammable as paper,
you roll a cigarette absently, eyeing the balconies.
Something rotten wafts up bittersweet from a grate.
You are fulcrum here now, the healer who healed himself,
crunching down alleys on an unsteady L-plate motorbike,
turning out memory's tricks, patting walls for coat hooks--
Lamb's Conduit and Buttery Mews, Occupation Road,
coercing new wrinkles into the brain, on either side
of a deep ridge called "The River", flowing sin-black,
gorgeous, the crease in the book between Alpha and Omega,
A and Zed, dividing sea from sky and light from dark,
you sleep with a Blue Book under your pillow, under a map
pinned to the ceiling, more accurate than planets or stars.
This is the contentment you were after, tapping ashes
through the slits into hell, your casual propriety simmering
a goulash of rat-runs and corner-cuts, faster by fifteen pence,
and this thought makes the pot lid tip to reveal a smile, the one 
Eve first saw on Adam's face, wiping the juice from his chin.



A bin bag mouths the air like an undersea amoeba.
The conversations waft, needled by an wobbly phonograph.


Consistently upstaged by rats at the Troubadour

which way to look for cars, they stand at attention
before the red man, snapping gum and photographs.

on its bones,

18 Dec 13

Revised "Smoke Ring"



An Anglicised American in Holyrood Court

Call me "mate", call me "pal",
asking "friend or foe?" in a glance.
Even the stones want to know
what kind of shoes they scrape.

Speak softly but carry a sgian dubh
in your sock. Warm to the man from
California, second Eden, glisten 
black ice for his London-born wife.

Cross my blue heart and hope to die,
something in me loves walls, bristling
with lichen and moss, older the better.
Castles, too, but not their necessity.

19 Dec 13

We draw weapons of class destruction
for selfies at every quaint plaid nook
brandishing smartphones like family crests
capturing the moment, the people, the town.

Something in me loves the sharp white tern
flashing across the cerulean sky,
faster than lenses, higher than turrets,
aloft, independent, stranger arcing home.

Speak softly, walk slowly, hope to live.
Carry a cross near your bristling heart.
Love phones, and stones, their necessity.
Call me "mate". Call me your pal.

28 Dec 13

Home Office, Croydon

Beneath the surface, darker matter stirs,
steaming up my third latte this hour,
gasping into the air-conditioned lounge
of what could be an airport terminal.
The man wearing a topi beside me
forgets to breathe, then gasps, repeats,
while his daughters in the play area
carefully build homes from coloured bricks.
The clerks shuffle paperwork cheerfully--
red passport, blue passport, green passport,
brown, jobsworth elves who know the list
of who gets Christmas, who gets coal.
My number up, I flash a tight-lipped smile,
Should I stay or should I go? stuck in my mind.

Clapham Junction

"Should I stay or should I go?" stuck in my mind,
the doors tweet shut with a rubbery thud.
"I'd beg for some forgiveness, but begging's
not my business" as the train glides away,
to float its fanning delta of branch lines.
Too little, too late, in the middle of a place
never meant to be anyone's final destination.
Here it all comes together, here it splits 
wide apart. "One more change," explains a dad 
to son, tugging him upstream across the platform.
Crowds weave together, and people disappear.
I step backward from the edge, into the slipstream.
The train is gone, the moment past, but still
the ghosts remain, black shadows cast.

Soho

The ghosts remain, black shadows cast
on brick, mist over neon-lit cobblestones.
Hard Road is playing in the bar next door--
"There must be something in the air..."
The exhaust pipe of a Hackney carriage
respires to the beat of its diesel drum.
In from the glowing tip, it lulls
then curls from a working girl's nostrils.
Visibly at ease, the smoke lounges
in all directions, spreading its arms.
Here is the city's grit-flecked embrace.
"...been dying since the day I was born."
Part your lips, and breathe in slowly,
drawing up the sweet, unhealthy air.

Brick Lane Market

Drawing up the sweet, unhealthy air
from sizzling woks, flat bubbling crepes
we ogle falafel, smirk at t-shirt slogans,
finger the dyed silks and leather bags.
Huguenot chapel turned Russian synagogue,
now a Bangladeshi Mosque, the moon and star
wink down at our worldly commerce
from the smokestack of a silver minaret.
Every brick a different shape and shade,
pecked by the acrid air, specked with colour
from a rattling can, even graffiti is for sale—
"Street art area: pay a fee or close your eyes."
Burning ghee and mustard oil, hissing paint.
Close both eyes, and follow the scent.
 
Canary Wharf

Close both eyes, and follow the scent
of marsh grass, salt rope, barnacled wood.
Oil lamps puff, pipe down their leaden light.
Tusk-like, whale ribs embrace a building site.
Spire of Narwhal, great barge up-ended, now
sea monsters rise up smooth, in cubic glass—
the streets scrubbed clean of tidal mud,
the Thames runs clear as lymph without its blood.
New brick, poured cement, tarmac's dull sheen,
cranes pick the horizon where gulls pocked the sand.
Shoe black, suit cleaners, flower shop for guilt,
French perfume goes waft-about when a tester gets spilled.
From a top-story balcony, an underwriter plans his grave
while admiring the skyline, its rich amber haze.

 
Blackheath

While admiring the skyline, its rich amber haze,
sun scalds the mist in an oil slick of light
reminding us the ocean is never far, reminding us,
like Turner, like Messiaen, in saturated tones.
Street lamps peer over us, considering our gait, where once
the gibbet posts dangled a peepshow of bodies,
the gloaming air turned treacherous, picking rag from bone,
betraying the flesh to bake and rot its caramelised smell.
Beneath our dew-spotted feet, the earth grinds away
at the plague pits, chewing ancestors to tar,
sealed away like embers in the furnace of the heath,
the pocked bodies smelt, give off obsidian heat.
Over the vale, the mist descends, sherbet and blue.
Beneath the surface, darker matter stirs.

21 Jan 14

Green Men

I. 

Only the foreign tourists wait for him, uncertain
which way to look for cars, they stand at attention
before the red man, snapping gum and photographs.

Omnipresent as CCTV, reduced to angles, posing mid-stride 
in his armoured black box, surveying us like a celebrity 
from his limousine. He is nothing like he used to be.

Old Green Man still leers down from cornices, pub signs,
a face for trouble, cheeky as resin, common as grass--
vines flare from his nostrils, buds crown his hairline.

One gives the starting signal to a pack of Italian teenagers;
the other drives the vita through a sapling in Hyde Park.
Green is for beginning, and our man, our men, begin it.

II. 

The blue man is a clown, the white man stole the brown man's
land, but the Old Green Man goes on reclaiming the pavement,
rotting floor boards, spearing up shoots through the tarmac.

Old Green man's buddy Neptune inches up the waterline, cracking
ice in his teeth, speckling the beach huts with barnacles.
Go will keep on going, long after we have crossed the street.

Old Green man fights the chainsaw with pungent sap,
catches boots on looped tree roots, sends birds to bomb
the shit out of the inside of upturned hard hats.

New Green Man says you can't stop progress, and signs the paperwork.
He obeys all posted notices, even when they conflict, switches off
before curfew, says he knows his limits, the clean lines where he ends.

III.

Ampelmann strides in from the east, hat crushed down on his apple head,
which tilts up from his ample physique, admiring the horizon
on its fast approach as he falls forward, pinwheeling into the day.

When he finally stops, he's a martyr, blood-red, arms stretched wide,
saying "Stop", saying, "Enough is enough", saying "Please, don't shoot".
Fists balled, with pedestal feet, his hat becomes a trench helmet.

We moved in, one limb at a time.
The walls were damp, and the pipework
thumped, crossbeams drilled with lice.
Roof tiles were padded with moss, bricks 
blood-damp, the paving stones wept.
I sat at the kitchen table, snapping
twigs with my thumbs like chicken bones, 
peeling apples skins in long spirals.
Outside, the willow slept, bent double,
her fingertips dangling in water.

27 Jan 14

Human fingers are a delicacy in China. They incinerate
their own children for warmth in snowy North Korea.
You won't believe what happens next. Go on, one click

to register your interest in a ship full of cannibal rats.
Click to light on fire. Click to behead. Read commentary
on current events from certified netherworld trolls.

We longed for the day when anything would be possible.
The nerve endings open and close like spiked anemones,
tweeting like chicks in a tangled nest, hungry for more.

So much depends upon: the right pixels in the right order.
I would like to thank free market psychology, that costly
art, and also these magic beans, which I have been watering.

So much depends on where you look, as the man pulls coins
from behind our ears to deposit in his offshore account.
I would like to thank loose sleeves for creating the internet.

The grey sponge laps it up like spilled kerosene. We scroll
in our sleep, through hyperlinked dreams, Photoshopped
for effect. Remember. This is what we said we always wanted.

The Doorway from Portugal
    after Eduardo Souto de Moura

Of course there is a doorway, but 
you do not have to go through it.
There are no walls to hold it up.
So why go through instead of around?

It is the Brandenburg Gate in no-man's land,
a claim jumper's mark in the Wild West, 
entrance to an invisible garden, ruined city,
portal folding space-time like a page.

Make of the arch a body, the body an arch,
bringing the fingertips together to pray.
Here is a marker as useful as you make it.
It leads to anywhere, can you believe?

It is a suspension bridge from floor to floor,
two buttresses flying into each other at speed,
one man's seriously heavy inside joke--
have you heard the one about the doorway?

How do we know space? As the thing that's not.
The no-thing taking place behind your eyes.
Who will you be when you have passed through?
Ask your loved who they want on the other side.

When you are ready, an archway will emerge,
one day, somewhere you least expect it.
Close your eyes and walk into the centre,
stand there, chanting quietly: I am the door. 

It is the neck of the womb, gap in the armour,
the crack in the mirror that reveals the trick,
mark of welcome, and way through the rock,
start and end of the tunnel, all at once.

Pass through, from the somewhere you were, 
to the somewhere that has been waiting.
Yes, there is a doorway here and yes,
some day, you will have to go through it.

The Doorway from Portugal
    after the installation by Eduardo Souto de Moura

Of course there is a doorway, but 
you do not have to go through it.

Exhibit: Childhood
    after the installation by Pezo von Ellrichshausen

Come, let's build a better treehouse 
for the children past their prime,

indoors, on stilts, inside the gilt-
trimmed tearooms of old Empire.

Lift us up from the marble floor,
into a pine-scented elsewhere,

eye-to-eye with entertained angels,
tweeze us from mid-life into mid-air.

Here we can see ourselves from afar,
look down on our own balding heads.

Come play with me, and be my friend,
stranded on a ship within a room.

No girls allowed. No boys allowed.
You have to know the password to get in.

We sand the rails with our fingerprints.
We leave our younger self to guard the door.

5 Feb  14

The Dance
    after the installation by Kengo Kuma

"...it seems to me the beautiful uncut hair of graves"
    -Walt Whitman

What do you get when you remove the flame's heat
from its outline? What is the shape of fire 
without fire? Bend your wire spectacles
into ellipses, mandorlas. Make a chain-
link fence fron scented bamboo. These
are the questions, lit from beneath.
Here is a patchwork quilt made only
from stitching, the edges remember
their serpentine, almondine shapes.
If wood could belly-dance, see
here the strings of the lute,
sheen of a net on water,
steam from a grate,
ghosts from below
tangled in
sizzling
light.

# # #

Underworld
    after the installation by Grafton Architects

Under the white blade of the guillotine,
under the meat hammer's flat insistence,
we amble, making mincemeat of the light.

Under the watchtower's square black eye,
pacing our laps in the rectangular yard,
we tread each other's shadows in a line.

Pour me a basilica, mould me a bunker,
chip right angles into rockface defiance,
square pot for light and shade to germinate.

Under the cathedral's brutal restructure,
we stand and spread our arms to crucifix,
divide four panes reflected on the floor.

We are the lunar eclipse at well-bottom,
pebble dropped into the sewer's grate, we 
turn our face, and everything fades to black.


Banshee Tubes
    after the installation by Diébédo Francis Kéré

So here's where all those plastic straws
discarded from my boyhood sugar drinks
finally found a place they could retire.

Bent and pinched, looped and bundled up,
they decorate the honeycomb cave in a feat
of crowd-sourced, multi-colour porcupinery.

Someone made a spiderweb in the corner.
Another built a pitch fork all in tubes.
The birth canal is spiked in rainbow hues.

An infant gums the straws on the other side,
her eyes burn aqua, lit by new experience,
while tweenies wrap wrists in pink and blue.

At night, the straws convene secret communities,
swap places in small but hard-to-prove ways,
tell stories of the old days, sing folk tunes.

Sometimes they catch the breeze of an open vent,
and whistle like reed beds, these lithe discarded 
choristers, bright wailing rubbish, banshee tubes.


# # #


Please Do Not Walk on the Stones
    after the installation by Li Xiaodong

Suspended in Ewok prison,
war camp of the tree people,
we walk on sky light, branch
slats around each corner.

Lost in a woodland in London,
we put on commuter demeanour,
stand right, stride up left,
corner the labyrinth quietly.

At the room's impossible end,
a reflecting garden of stones
flaunt their tangible crunch
underfoot, but are prohibited:

"Please, do not walk on the stones."
These are special stones, symbolic
stones, unlike you, they are not
to be disturbed, as they watch

themselves do stony things in the 
floor-to-ceiling made-for-stones-
only mirror. Sometimes matter
matters more than mind, and this 

time, stones win. Turn back into
the forest that is the middle
of your life, the flaking bark
and mossy smell you navigate,

turn back with a pebble's knowledge
in your shoe, shuffling your way
into tunnels and bus queues,
wearing your face like a stone.

# # # 

Snowblindness

It is hard to know how I got here,
now that we cut the sled dogs loose,
and went out separate ways for help,
hard as pack ice in the footsteps
I crunch into, wondering whose they are,
following a herd of anxious commuters
doubtless on their way to warmth,
raising what look like pitch forks
against the white buildings ahead,
their black tongues crying, "Murder"
as I laugh into the snow-licked wind,
glad not to be the foreman on that rig,
glad to see the thousand-pair kind eyes
blinking out in front of me, soft-nosed
welcome party, parting ways as I approach
the city centre, flushed and sweating,
under this maniacal sun, I skip forward, 
breathing heavily, pulling off my clothes.

23 Feb 14

Learning Scottish

Call me "mate", call me "pal",
asking "friend or foe?" in a glance
even the pavement wants to know
what kind of shoes it scrapes.

Say, "Aye, right", double
positive, biding time
while the mind cancels out
both words like a bad cheque.

"Ken" what you can of me,
speaking gentle, manly words,
while a dagger cools your ankle
and a whetstone keens your tongue.

2 March 14

Buttons

Buttons themselves are a kind of love token,
they fasten your coat to keep out the damp,
and love is each stitch sewn tight and unbroken
sticking them down like a well-licked stamp.

Buttons make eyes for your stuffed toys to see,
which bulge when you squeeze them right up
but love holds them on through the teddybear tea
or they'd fall with a splash--in your cup!

The things that we love we keep close as we can
sewn into our pockets and stitched on our sleeve
but one day, time's tick-tock will unravel our plans
and a button will fall down, roll on, and just leave.

Buttons make eyes for your stuffed toys to see,
they fasten your coat to keep out the breeze,
the things that we love we keep close as can be,
but sometimes our love means we let them go free.

So, goodbye to the buttons, both pearly and black,
you fastened our trousers, you picked up the slack,
we will miss your bright shine, and miss your click-clack
we love you, goodbye now, and we hope you come back.

Octopus

I am the octopus, liquid, dragging
my limbs behind me. I am the bogeyman
crawling the cloudy sea floor. I am
an artist. I am in costume, really.
Do you have an appointment? Would you
like to play? Come into my cellblock,
