Filing Room

Close one drawer, another opens, a game of noughts
and crosses, thumbing the backs of tabbed dividers,
separating knowledge from known, game from gamut,

first open, then closed with a the drum-metal thud--
the labelled exterior, inert on its haunches,
rolling out, at a tug, its long spine of folios.

Open one drawer, another slides shut, or locks
in place for your safety, since pressed beneath
a cabinet is a death reserved for minor clerks

and literary villains

10 August 16

since pressed beneath
a cabinet is a death reserved for minor clerks

and literary villains, whose twirled moustaches
signify the bookish end of damsels bound to rails
not unlike those now flanking our upright files.

Where will it end--the hoarding of knowledge?--
the low rumble of a drawer reverberates, tiny
earthquake for paper soldiers held at attention

remembering their former lives as broad trees
stretching toward the only data that mattered--
sunlight on green skin--depicted in drawer "C"

for "chloroplast", in faded brown ink, tidy 
Victorian hand, now tangled with roots reclaiming
the soil-damp coffin, cross-referenced from "I"

for "irony", where also we find the lefthand curl
of our villain's moustache protruding beneath a safe
filled with the paper money he was after.

Here ends our game of noughts and crosses, won
on a diagonal, which could jam a drawer, wreak 
havoc with the parallel lines of any rail, still

the files press together, signifying case closed,
as the broad leaves go on with their jitterbug
from a window overlooking the filing room.

16 August 16

16/08/16 -- one of the evenest days of the year,
or so I hope, spiking down the shaved corners
of threes and fives and nines, everything at sixes,
at seven minus one, minus centigrade, in fingerless
glove boxes, boxing a frozen shadow up for safekeeping.
You can trust me. You can trust me to be myself,
all eight of them, though rarely at once, but twice
in my life I strode up to the precipice, hollering
into the echoed dark, rubbing the edge down smooth.
What's it like to lose track of the fact that you have
a wife, a life, a WiFi connection, an erection, a sigh
at the sign of subsidence, your foundations cracked?
Sanity tourists circle me, poke out their fingers,
titter in disbelief that I could really chew through
these slathered leather restraints. I say try me.
I say sixteen-eight is my lucky day, unlike four-two,
one half of a haloed reprieve from the dark lashings
of bile gurgling up from the conscience-less gut.
Today is going to be a good day. Today is going to be
a day. Today is going. I'm going in, mayday, today.

Response to "Nemeton"

Dark Journey

Where the bear goes, I go. Into the peat
and stench, across the shallow rivers
salted with ice, over dappled ground,

winding through a storm of moss and bark
into the clear-eyed centre, lined
with bracket fungi and dew-specked webs.

Something has always happened here, invisible
as decay, quiet as wood lice in the topsoil,
persistent as the hum of cicada choirs.

22 August 16

return their colour to the rain-washed air.
I go with the bear. Don't try to follow.

Lapsarian I

Eden was a weapon, because how can you get comfortable
in rented paradise, digging the muck of over-ripe fig
from your speckled skin, fashioning tales for a Bible
you will never read, an Athens you will never see, so
forgiveness comes easy at the top of the green lighthouse
we call a tree, or squatting beneath it, serving flat
bread to dry mouths, a gag order from on high, in alignment
with the shift of stars, those pin-prick mimics of the sun.

Lapsarian II

Milton's angels, tinkling loudly, hush at the slightest creak
of footman descending, their greatest helplessness being trained
to instant invisibility, and Dylan Thomas knew too well the clotted
destruction of a seeker's heart, the nape hairs at attention, then
starved of the meat of life once interrupted, lanes of diamond
returning to asphalt, caution reclaiming every step, the lily 
reverting to granite, the daylight becoming incarnadine again.

Lapsarian III

It has shrivelled, the globe, its composition being mainly water

world, beyond the breakers of a starlit moon, the colony Brooklyn,
population twelve, sealed in a dome on a red planet pumping jazz

whipping through this place, this scene, this cell, this paradise.

28 August 16

Read some Zapruder

Failure to Thrive

I awoke, late afternoon, to a summer storm,
realising I had done none of the things
that the dead white men, who wrote history,
expected me to do to get added to their book,
and that the stream of productivity, running on
like an endless sentence from the past, was one
I punctuated far too often with a nap. The smell
of the rain came through the smallest window crack.
It was late summer. The rain had no idea of its
ending, and the smell was great with promise 
for a week of gentle breezes pushing sculptures
of white cloud, the kind I could never write about
well enough to distinguish myself from the 773,000
cloud poems Google offers up to me for a click,
in point three eight seconds, because that's how fast
you have to be now to be somebody. The spare room
I favour sleeping in by day became almost imperceptibly
green, in the shadow of a thunderhead's contemplation,
yet I was there to see it, with the cat, who said nothing.

29 August 16

Maggots

We put out beer, in a dish, and like Homer's ghosts,
they came, inching at terrible speed across the tile.

Whatever they wanted to say, we did not hear it, dabbing
them up like hungry birds, popping them in napkins.

I held a spray gun of bleach over one, who flinched, 
in his wormy way, before the recall of his life.

All night I dreamt of people who had died, their long
bodies and pale faces, their larval hunger to live.

For a limited time only, I too am alive, waging war
to reclaim the my kitchen, its sanitation, its sanctity--

etch clean the barrier between my stores of food
and the decaying world outside. I turn up the lights,

and out they come, small white grains of admonition,
rippling through chlorine, like flags of surrender.

30 August 16

Ode to Bratislava Rolls
    (having never tasted one)

You must send me there at once. Despite
being gluten-free, I am sure my stomach
would make an exception. They are shaped
like a horseshoe, which makes them lucky,
unless you hold them upside down, so luck
drains from their sharpened tips, fickle
luck, to be lost as easily as a poppy seed,
with which our eponymous rolls are filled,
I'm told, so avoid taking a drug test or 
watching the Wizard of Oz for at least 
thirty minutes after eating one. Did I mention
the glaze? Made from the yolks of decorated
eggs, given an honourable discharge before
whisking, to retain the dignified appearance
of marbled gold. Could you wear them
as a bracelet? I don't know the etiquette
of pastry well enough to tell you. I do
know what it's like to pull apart something
beautiful, and find something else inside.
Isn't this called love, in any language?

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

juniper, medical, bounds, gradual, obvious, treat, firmly, jail, principle, undoing, fastened, regular, drawer, begging, gleam

Also:

Mention two kinds of weather

The Farmer's Wife

First they want more sun from me, then
they want more rain. What's a girl to do?

The juniper berries are hardening their gin.
Leaves of yerba buena wilt, then rot.

I lay out a platter as large as a harvest moon,
Mayan calendar, a round lake of snowmelt tears.

Still, they go begging in the streets. Still,
they disown me, one spoonful at a time.

They convert to new religions for a slice of bread.
They join foreign armies for a ration of butter.

I will bury the ones I suckled before I am old,
and lay out the feast as if they were still here.

I will crush the legs of the table with wealth,
from the land, from the oceans and animals.

There will always be second helpings in my house.

Confident middle aged man sitting and smiling against white background

3 Sept 16

"Confident Middle-Aged Man Sitting and Smiling Against White Background"
    (after the stock photo)

The way small motes flickered in sun, the way disaster
seemed to be around every corner, but never came.

I have lived a long time to get to this place. I think
I might try living some more, but differently.

Perhaps I'll come back as an iguana, or a square
of Leicester cheese. It seems about as fitting

as the categories I currently subscribe to,
not to mention the so-called literary magazines,

which I sprinkle on the coffee table like glitter
for a boy-band rabid cohort of tweenie intellectuals.

Go on, snap my picture. I'll serve for a wax seal,
de-barb-ing, un-stinger-ing, making at once milquetoast

and reputable whatever it is you're trying to sell.
I put the harm in harmless, the pain in counterpane,

I come from a long line of, "He seemed like such a nice guy"
when they bust the attic padlock and smell corpses.

I'm the one you least expect, an owner of neckties,
poster child for the social contract, signed in feces.

The way dust was never more than dust, but we were led
by brilliant slogans and hefty jingles to believe.

The way our mothers used to whisper, "You'll be great."
The way the handle of a handgun was made for my-sized hand.

7 Sept 16

Self-Esteem for Giants

Sometimes I think I'm just the way
dishes get transported up and down stairs--
like, having no legs, they invented me
to go on these excursions. It makes me
feel useful. I lick a spoon for added
effect. Sometimes I think about how
I make cutlery both dirty and clean,
shuffling it from dishwasher to drawer
to dining table, and then back again.
Maybe they are lazy circuit trainers,
perhaps each day for them is a season,
the hot and soapy rains, the hibernation,
the thrill of repeat contact with my mouth.
There has to be something to this, after all.
Sometimes, when I break a dish, I cry,
but not the same way as when a person dies--
it's more the shock than the loss involved,
more about me and my failure as a cup holder
than about the cup and it's untimely demise.
Aren't we all a little like this, though?--
grieving for what we miss, rather than what
others are missing out on? This is why I'd like
to think my daily use of trays and cups
could be a thrill for them, bounding cliffs
in the questionably steady hands of a giant.
I'd like to feel like a giant. I was told
(probably by myself) that one day I would.
I like the little chime when I put the forks
back in their places. I wonder what they might
be saying about me, passing on trip reviews
through the drawers and shelves they sleep in.
Goodnight, dishes and cutlery. Sleep well.

8 September 16

The Mothra of Equanimity

After a night of not much sleep, I imagine by brain
a mound of spaghetti gelled together at the bottom
of last night's saucepan. Once again I have failed
to live up to the ideal picture in my head of happiness,
which involves a ninja version of me going about
his mundane tasks with immovable equanimity. Instead,
here I am slumped in my office chair, resting 
on the sorest point in my back. The small pain
reminds me I am still awake, and that today is going
to be longer than other days. In fact, today hasn't
ever ended, but repeats in theme and variation,
sometimes the smell of honeysuckle, sometimes
its pendulant berries, but always today. I trapped
a moth with a glass cup and a postcard. He seemed
angry more than frightened, but I know it was just
program 271: escape at all costs--executing
in his Darwin-tuned neurology, so I lifted him
(or her) to an open window. The night was glowing.
The huge and beautiful eyes on his/her wings
looked back at me without a twitch of disapproval.
This morning is warm enough my teacup doesn't steam.
Trees' top leaves are sucking down glitzy sunlight
before the next rheostat swizzle of cloud.
A ninja steadies himself, glued to my ceiling,
waiting in cosmic silence to cover me with a cup.

9 Sept 16

People, People, People!

People piss me off. Is that true? Perhaps I carry
a sack of piss around with me, somewhere in my lower
abdomen. Medieval doctors thought this true--and it is!
we call it the bladder, though we don't blame it much
anymore for feeling angry, except perhaps when the queue
for the loo is long, though even that can be forgiven
as circumstance, whereas incompetence and rudeness--
which are often hard to tell apart--sets me off no end,
spraying the room with acrid thoughts, which I keep to myself
(so as not to appear rude) boiling like hot ammonia inside.
How fun it is to tell you this, as if you didn't already know
from your own experience what it is to be royally pissed off.
I bet the queen has a padded toilet seat, and the loo paper
always gets folded back into that little easy-to-tug triangle
after she uses it. It's tempting to think with that kind of
treatment, I might never get angry again. Funny word, treatment--
meaning both how obsequious a waiter has been, and also how
effective a psychiatrist, as well as an outline or draft
for some daft television nonsense liable to get one's dander up.
In England, pissed normally just means one is, flatly, drunk.
Alcohol can cause incitement to temper, like flat steel hammered--
another word for drunk--when red from the furnace, and soft.
Anger makes me feel less soft, tightening my flabby abdomen.
I too go red in the face, when rude, incompetent, or pissed.
It's funny how words don't mean much by themselves, except
by shared experience. Which is why I love people, really I do.

10 Sept 16

The bright horizon. Always far off.

No-one has ever gotten pregnant over Skype.

So Autumn Begins

It smells like rain and clean bedclothes
that haven't been used in months.

It smells like eucalyptus and cicadas.
It smells like orange blossom and faint gasoline.

It smells like a home for which I could be homesick,
but where I am only a visitor, a stranger myself.

It smells like the top of the cat's head.
It smells like a fox in the garden again.

It smells like cut hay, which will never be tamed.
It smells like a bakery next to a pharmacy.

It smells like the feelings for which words
have not been invented, because they are so rare.

It smells like the colour of mud mixed with moss.
It smells the way dreaming feels, when you fall.

12 Sept 16

It smells like eucalyptus and cicadas, orange
blossom and faint gasoline. It smells like home
has been cremated, silenced, a squeaky door hinge,

passed over, smouldering, by formation-flying drones
programmed for conservation, demurring to re-singe
the bones of monuments, ragged as melting styrofoam.

It smells like hate. It smells like home.

14 Sept 16

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

dutiful, lucifer, everlasting, clump, metallic, unable, obedient, rouge, watery, wade, psychic, theme, mineral, nick, crocus

Also:

Include the name of a boardgame

Fiant Tenebrae

Dutiful Lucifer, plodding along on this everlasting clump,
scanning with one metallic eye, unable to squint, obedient
to his cause, the rouge, watery sky he wades through,
tasting the air for psychic vibes, themes of violence,
the mineral tang of human hatred, a nick in the armour,
a crocus unfolding just slightly too soon, he sighs,
before plucking, licks his lips with a tongue of flame.
It's essentially Cluedo to him, the who-has-done-what,
before the fun begins--sewing both their eyes together
with a strand of beard, turning noses to beaks, and beaks 
into spires, sitting for portrats with Bosch and Dali. 
Ice cream melts when he passes. Can you imagene the fun?
The broad veins of the river mutter their grudges along,
he makes himself a bridge of ice across the Rhine.

Magpie

half-ghost, half-demon,
standout in all weathers
rowing your rudder of tail.
If only things were so. If only
the dark in me was feathered,
divided, quill-by-quill from light,
as in the first piebald making
of the dung-world I carry rolled,
collecting hay and human dust.
O sucker for the glitzy thing.
O mixed-up bird, mirroring.
What do you see in tinsel-gleam?
How long have you waited
to flash through these branches,
half-shadow, half holy glow?

19 September 16

Letter to the Last Megafauna

My friends, you wouldn't like it here, moss
squelching and slipping underfoot, lean drizzle
tickling your rivulets, bare black trees.

We'd give you names like Babar, Dumbo, Topsy,
then shackle your legs for safety (ours),
parade you in a car for entertainment (ours).

Everywhere we go (archaeology shows) the giants
disappear--save you, the last of the mammoths,
eyelid creased with wrinkles, black eye watching.

Some say we compare our manhood to your tusks,
and feel ashamed. I say your remind us of more--
raising clouds like smoke on columnar feet.

We can't abide your bigness, no matter how slow,
sure-footed. We can't abide how small we become,
clambering up the city's skyline to cheat death.

First we trained you to the chain, now rope,
soon a string will keep you in place for us.
It has come to this: elephant or skyscraper.

My friends, stay put, stay huge, stay bulldozer
slow, uprooting acacia and spurting fountains of mud
at the barrels of cameras and rifle scopes alike.

I write to you from a damp rag smelling of mushrooms,
but red dust swills my nostrils, and sun drums my hide.
I could stand upright in the chambers of your heart.

I could stand upright in the chambers of your heart.

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

durable, parent, studio, flannel, spindle, rule, pouch, stale, wallet, household, incomprehensible, prepare, nakedness, extremely, stranded

Also:

Mention another art form besides poetry

The Old, White-Faced Moon

Durable as queso fresco, parent to the sun,
shine down your studio light upon our flannel,
wrap us in your spindle, wind us and rule!

I want to live in your pouch-like craters,
smelling as stale as my overused wallet,
make a household of your compartments,

make myself incomprehensible to light,
O prepare! for the colonising force,
we advance, in our nakedness, star by star

no extremity too far, no strand too stranded,
we are coming for you, luna, lookout, my dear,
for we plan to plant a birthmark on your cheek.

A Brief History of Linear Progress

How little we really know about anger,
for example, could it power a lawnmower
with its heated rods, it's eerie glow?

Sorry, I'm thinking of uranium, of course,
with its dispassionate half-life, a kind
of undead energy that refuses to die.

Consider the flash, consider the panic--
God's magnifying glass focused on us ants,
and what does He see, but the fizz of smoke?

How little we know ourselves before we're gone.
Perhaps we should be angry about that too.
I punched my bestie in the face for calling me, "wimp".

Don't we all wimp-out, and whimper in the end?
There wasn't time to cough, sneeze, or tremble.
Only our shadows could testify our presence.

Is it still anger if it travels on an airplane?
Is it anger when it's processed through as paperwork?
How far is far enough to muffle the tremors?

Pour me a whiskey, and I will crush the glass.
Load up a bomber, so that I can reply to this email.
Press send. Press save. Press panic. Go on, press me.

25 September 16

Just do this. Just this once. Just this one thing.
Do this and it will be done, finito. Promise. Do
it for me, do it for your country. One more time.
The last job. Before we retire for good, and happy.
One last morsel. One more coin in the slot. Final
try. Last go round. Do it for the sake of doing it.
Surely a sip can't hurt you. One step closer, one
stop sooner, one last dip into the (bottomless) well.

Warlords Becoming Worm Food

Autumn is the smell of summer's dust
burning off the radiators,
the bones of winter growing in the belly.
Come, darkness and candle wax.

All of this will die soon, but not
completely. In the slow drip
of our livelihoods, accumulating
one more icicle to drop.

28 September 16

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

battlefield, seamless, miniature, priest, fewer, supper, drawers, marvelous, lavish, clenched, filtered, logs, peck, craved, echoing

Also:

Make up an unusual name for a person and include it in the poem

Time is our battlefield, each day
a skirmish, the hour hand
pointing at us accusingly, and slow--
minutes whizzing like shrapnel.

I've got you now, little concubine,
do as I say and be protected.
But the wind has other plans for desire,
and the bare trees whisper.

A squirrel knows its acorn burials,
even when littered with leaf.
Time itself may remember us fondly,
for having endured, and lost.


29 September 16

Organic Matter

Here I am in England, where everything tastes
of the aftertaste of marmalade, and I have made
sleep my frenemy, made Earl Grey my overlord--
where mushrooms are fruiting in the forests
and maggots climb the compost bin, sentient
grains of rice, swimming in summer raindrops.
Soon I will put on the yellow gloves, boil
pots of water and white vinegar, then dispense
from an ordinary watering can their acid death.
Next year, at this time, they will be back again.
So I will make a routine of making rain deadly,
even as I have made it a habit to wear or not wear
socks, in different seasons, at different times of day.
People just like me, all over the world, are making
a habit of slowly reclaiming the Earth from the young,
who writhe but can do little besides slip in,
and afterwards off come the gloves, inverting--what
a hassle that is, to have to flip each finger back again.
The bin men will come, and with a great hydraulic fork
lift and dump the slurry from a makeshift plastic grave.
Meanwhile more flies are already circling the drain,
sniffing out the tantalising decay that so makes a creche.
I don't claim to comprehend my life any more than you do.
Nor can I lob stones at those around me, for fear of injuring
my two glass eyes, the porcelain of my all-too human skull.
Instead, I put the kettle on, a sensible act in desperate
times, and why shouldn't a good deed shine in my weary
world, even as others are like dark drops of oil in a pan?
I put on my socks, a mundane act of self-compassion.
I turn off the news, and wait for the news to reach me,
emerging from the soil, like a toadstool's satellite face.

# # #

Making Conversation

Childhood is the thing we dust off from time to time in conversation,
to showcase our once-innocent views and tell how good it was
to be so small in a world that only ever wanted what is best for us.

Now we have grown considerably, settling into armchairs like bad habits,
swirling the overbig wine glasses and making jokes about how this red
can breathe both in and out within such a large, transparent lung.

As a child I had an anatomy set, I'll say, where the skin was clear plastic,
bought from one of those shops--can you remember?--that sold only toys,
a place of pilgrimage, smelling of stale jellybeans and model airplane glue.

These facts confirm my former residence in childhood's palace of wonderment,
though now it seems impossibly small from these heights of grumpy enlightenment,
where even for what I don't already know, I have a wife to fill in the gaps.

There were always seven billion of us in this marriage. We began as gooey larvae, dazzled by the spikes of omnipresent sun, and now we are the people 
--imagine--for whom even sparsely populated trains feel overcrowded.

3 October 16

Making Conversation

Childhood is the thing we dust off from time to time in conversation,
to showcase our once-innocent views and tell how good it was
to be so small in a world that only ever wanted what is best for us.

Now we have grown considerably, settling into armchairs like bad habits,
swirling the overbig wine glasses and making jokes about how this red
can breathe both in and out with the help of such a large, transparent lung.

As a child I had an anatomy set, I'll say, where the skin was clear plastic,
bought from one of those shops--can you remember?--that sold only toys,
a place of pilgrimage, smelling of stale jellybeans and model airplane glue.

These facts confirm my former residence in childhood's palace of wonderment,
though now it seems impossibly small from these heights of grumpy enlightenment,
that I began life so larval soft, dazzled by spikes of omnipresent sun, 

whereas now I have become someone for whom even a sparsely populated train 
feels overcrowded. Each evening I peel away the skin that's formed by London,
skimming it off like leftover porridge before it forms a firmer crust.

Of course, I don't say this to my guests, who also inhabit a world 
run by politicians, which they read about in papers designed to shield the eyes
from accidentally catching other eyes during our early morning commute.

The topic has long since moved on from childhood, and I can find no way 
to take us back. How fitting. I feel my own head nod and frown, nod and frown,
listening absently for direct questions I might someday have to answer.

I have no answers. Only more questions. The dregs of the wine spiders
the glass, and the candles begin to gutter. Another successful collision
avoided, another evening spent. I sleep the dreamless sleep of the dead.

# # #

Everyday living,
                    (the way you're doing it)
will wear you out.

We begin with something childish, unworthy of a poem.
Begin with the taste of sassafras in your mouth,
atop a coal slag heap in the Appalachian foothills.

Not every day is a poem. Not every poem comes every day.
I really am writing crap here.

The portcullis slides its spikes into their well-worn places.
How dark a red leaf, evacuated of chlorophyll.

With bruised disinterest, with lacklustre charm,
we enact the old dances solemnly.

Where is my mother? Her shopping trolley abandoned.
How bright the glint of a scalpel under surgical lights.

It could blind you, all this beauty.

The Wind Telephone
     Otsuchi Town, Japan

Say into me 
            what you cannot say.
I will wait
            on the hill
transmitting into
                    the astonished
air. Speak
            in this booth
                    of your exile,
how the days pass
                   without them,
like clouds,
              and you are a kite

and they, a kite
                    whose string
has been cut
              by the sharp edge
of time.

         Ask if they are eating
         
well, and warm
                enough
without you
             to remind them
the blankets
              are in the cupboard
              
where they have always been.

                            Ask
that they keep rising,
                     as you rise,
each morning,
              to absently touch
the smooth side
                of the still-made bed.

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

Money Belt

Here we are in the land of the giants. I thought
it would be bigger somehow, but at least there's
a fast-food chain into which we can empty our pockets
and fill our bellies with non-toxic food-like products.

The tour operator promises us at least three giants
before sundown, or our money back. That's a good deal,
according to the brochure, so I'll wait here nibbling
cheese fries and greasing up my smartphone until they come.

I've never seen a real giant, so this will be something
to take back to the family in Minnesota and tell about.
I wore my second-best sneakers just in case we have to run,
and a poncho since I hear sometimes they like to spit.

Giant spit! Imagine that. Sounds like a comic book.
We entertain ourselves on the boat by chatting,
comparing the friendliness of these locals to others,
how much they take an interest in where we're from.

Not everywhere is as polite as Minnesota, but I think
we can trust this guy we're with. His English is very good,
considering, and he seems to know the things we like.
Giants! Bring 'em on. I can't fathom why he looks bored.

# # #

You know you lived in the 'teens if...

You got used to the sound of glaciers, weeping.

Turning

Today I'm ignoring the news, and focusing instead
on the frond-like shrub in the neighbours garden,
from where I overlook them in my small office,
gently heated by a dusty radiator, accompanied
by the distant sounds of a snoring cat. I am
no better than you for this, nor more able
to stop what is going on. I call it empathy,
but really it's wanting control--as though if
only I could find the right remote, I might switch
the station from Shocking Forms of Misery, a twelve-
part series, to People Finally Realise We're All
In It Together, a short-run cult-classic documentary.
Alas, even if I did find such a device, the batteries
would probably have died a long time ago, leaving me
glued to the train wreck they're selling for eyeballs
and clicks, and sometimes to win elections. So today,
it's fronds, and the dark tips of blackbirds against
nothing but the elements themselves. I shall watch
the slow retreat of chlorophyll from the leaves,
like aid workers falling back to their green zone,
as even I too aim to reside deep within. The tang
of winter flecks the air when I cast open a window,
stifled by all this warmth, all this not-looking
at the tragedies we choose report, and the ones
that float down silently, ghost of a castaway leaf.

# # #

17 October 16

Moving Stairs

They're coming down the escalator like a small parade:
puffer jacket, navy suit, hi-vis vest. Each in uniform,
helping us know at a glance what to think. "You British",
smirked the German port authority guard, thumbing through
my maroon passport, eyeing my suit, "always dress so nice."
I remember his striped epaulets, the way the German girl
behind me, outfitted in hemp, giggled. Humans on parade.
Here they come! Some hurry, others stand and are conveyed.
Most have mastered the art of relaxing their facial muscles
into a nerveless mask, save of course for the children,
whose heads rotate in silent wonder, reaching for a hand.
Little poem, where are we going with this? A critique
of transactional living, a la Hardy or Dickens, but with
smartphones? The children are gone now, launched into
the shopping mall, and the escalator stands momentarily
moving no-one down, its shredder-like teeth receding--
into where? Some under-stair cavern only maintenance knows.
I would like to set up a shop there, collecting frayed
bits of shoelace to spin for yarn, feeling it form
between thumb and finger into something indisputably
useful--a joiner of points, a lacer of eyelets, something
to tug or be tugged by, like the line cast my way clearing 
immigration, like the hand cast upward in perfect trust,
I would like to spin something long enough to gather
up every loose end, ravelled raver, banker, builder,
set them down in silence, and send them on their way.

20 October

Mood Diary

I. Black Iris

How felt-like, the darkness,
                             purpled
to a bruise. 
             A fly in amber,
                            the black eye
            still searching
for escape.
            I come to you
                            on the knees
of my knees,
            wilting
                    as I have always wilted
in your redolence.
                    Cry for me,
black dewdrop,
                tracing the ruff
of your neck,
                 for I have lost
and in losing,
                found only you
                                beside
the white curtains,
                    in a posture
of prayer,
            a silhouette
                            to replace
the pane-fogged breath
                        a shadow
rising
        in shadow.
                    The dark eye,
                                 pupil
                swollen to burst
                
come out and face me
                    whoever you are,
staring me down
                 in the shaving mirror,

stroking black whiskers
                        from my Adam's
apple,
        swirling the refuse
                            with foam.
It is dark in the audience,
                             
                 no light can enter
the ventricle
                without an incision
of mind. 
         Here, the matinee curtains,
         
here velvet,
            crushed,
                     and crushing,
a frame
        to enter
                 the emptiness
flickering
            with waltzes 
                         of dust.

II. Tar Pit

So begins the slow drip of another day.
So begins the deceptive patina of tar,
dusted in grit, tested with a single hoof,

clinging to the pads of your paw, stinking
of bone-char, black gum, automotive decay.
Coughed up like bits of lung on a beach
near La Brea, harder to tread than wet sand.

See how the bones pearl white for a necklace,
mastodon ribs suspended in long-dissolved sinews, 
ghost threads of a once forward motion.

Black gold pools the mouth of a protester, beaten,
black gold in the pupils, swapping ticker stocks.

"Hit me" says the baby in the racist folk tale,
and Br'er Rabbit can't resist putting in
his boot, sticking hated to hater in silent

embrace, for the child of tar says nothing,
but the greater the struggle, the closer
the two are held. Whither the poison flow?
Out from the gash of a sabred tooth, out

from the streets, asphalt melting in flames
whispered in riot shields and shopfront glass.
Has there ever been such a thing as progress?

Gum on our shoes, smoke on our hands. How can we 
take, and take, even one step further?

III. Fulcrum

Which way are you going? asks hitchhiker to thumb.
Swinging low, sweet chariot--the arc of a babe in arms,
at the still point where gravity turns, neither toward
or away, facing the mendicant traveller, how many
on pin-heads, seated, are the angels looking over us,
how narrow the gap between this world and the next,
meniscus, bubble ready to give itself up in a pop.
But O! how it dances in the rainbow-splattered
colours of creation, swelling into roundness, thinning
the membrane between the air within and chill without.
Hush, little baby--a mockingbird, a diamond ring,
stoking the last of the embers with a minuscule gust,
as the suds go crackling, and the plug gets tugged,
(what else is was a plug ever for, but pulling out?)
and down we go the motorway, our chariot made of fire.
What else is was a plug ever for, but pulling out?
as the suds go crackling, and the plug gets tugged,
stoking the last of the embers with a minuscule gust--
hush, little baby--a mockingbird, a diamond ring,
the membrane between the air within and chill without,
colours of creation, swelling into roundness, thinning--
But O! how it dances in the rainbow-splattered
meniscus, bubble ready to give itself up in a pop,
how narrow the gap between this world and the next,
on pin-heads, seated, are the angels looking over us?
Or away, facing the mendicant traveller--how many
at the still point where gravity turns, neither toward,
swinging low, sweet chariot--the arc of a babe in arms--
which way are you going? asks hitchhiker to thumb.

IV. Snow Globe

So this is Christmas: Eggnog? Check. Lights? Better check. Twelfth time's a charm, and Santa's a charmer. Bowl full of radioactive jelly. And the cranberries are gelling nicely, just like the guests. One more slather of lipstick, touching up the joker face for poker later. Say it thrice to make it come true: ho ho ho. Jingle goes the cutlery drawer, and jangle the nerves at the slamming door that can only mean Good! your racist uncle has arrived. Dance! Dance, my darlings. Good. Everything is going swimmingly so far. They move, suspended in aspic. I beat my fingers to a different drum. The drumsticks! Roasted. Gravy for everyone! See how it pours, from their pores, stuffed and glazed, on the couches, but who's still up? Up! Who's up for a game of charades?

V. Blind Lightning

Give Zeus my calling card. You call this a fucking storm?
Crack the glass globes of your overfed cheeks. I checked--

the forecast calls for vigilance, and sleep is for the weak

24/10

V. Blind Lightning

Give Zeus my calling card. You call this a fucking storm?
Crack the glass globes of your overfed cheeks. I checked--

the forecast calls for vigilance, and sleep is overrated,
underserved, and deserving of the weak. I slurp my milk

with strychnine, all the better to see you my dear, wolfing
down the goodie basket, and pounding my chest for more.

Second place is the first loser, so I'm told by my bumper sticker, 
and whoever can't keep up is consumed in the fires of debt.

You made me! I screech, fleeing the scene, tearing my clothes,
but I will find a way to beat the forward march of time.

I will drink the absinthe, but who will wash the cup? 
I moan while Rome is burning slowly down my throat.

Blow. Put your lips together. Even a bullet can whistle.

I. Black Iris

How felt-like, the darkness,
                             purpled
to a bruise. 
             A fly in amber,
                            the black eye
            still searching
for escape.
             Cry for me,
black dewdrop,
                tracing the ruff
of your neck,
                 for I have lost
and in losing,
                found only you
                                beside
the white curtains,
                    in a posture
of prayer,
            a silhouette
                            to replace
the pane-fogged breath
                        a shadow
rising
        in shadow.
                    Face me,

                    whoever you are,
staring me down
                 in the shaving mirror,

stroking black whiskers
                        from my Adam's apple,
                        
        swirling the refuse
                            with foam.
It is dark in the audience,
                             
                 no light can enter
the ventricle.

         Here, the matinee curtains,
         
here velvet,
            crushed,
a frame
        to enter
                   the emptiness
flickering
            with the waltzes 
                             of dust.
 I come to you
                on the knees
of my knees,
            wilting
                    as I have always wilted
in your redolence.

Answer 1

Yes, I heard. In the mumbling of crickets. Tell them
to make a career of chanting, and let the stars hold up
their placards, eject the weak link from the grasslands.

I pour the antidote into a blue glass of milk, carry it
down the aisle. Bystanders consider this performance art.
Draw back the veil and kiss me under the flash bulbs.

I can always say I was joking, say it wasn't really me.
In these parts, they beat court jesters, don't they?
My motto: all truth, all slant, all the time.

Or your money back.

Answer 2

The mist occludes my sight most mornings.
This does not mean I dwell within a cloud.

I have hungered for the desert, returned
to find an entirely new population of sand.

If Buddha dwells in the gap between thoughts,
who fixes His roof, who stokes the stove?

I have learned to love my thoughts, even when 
they turn against me. Let me fold a paper boat

for us to float on puddles until swamped.

Adoration of the Pisces

The human eye. It searches,
in bubbles for the bulbous
shapes cast by a spherical
mirror,
        curvature of scales,
tinkling with delight, to skim
the bright waters with a fin.

Fish eye, fish frown, fish
fanning its lung-pink gills.
We adore
          your swish sashay,
as you pout into the keeping
of kelp, and gaping clams.

Open the hinge of your mouth,
and let us in, that we may
pray
     in your domed interior.
     
Pescado mojado. Pisces humida.

Question 3
 
Rendered as a denial of service, I've been hoarding man
uscripts like I hold
evenings alone – untampered, unseen, unwanted
silence. Do you kill your
 
darlings? Do you let them slip the
echo, breach? I'm running
 
guarded for the inevitable heat death of the
universe, burning 
empires and Achilles so I can
render my heel immortal. I would
ride to Mysia, deliver my scribe,
enter anew world that does not want me.

Answer 3

The wound is deep in the circuit's breach;
the wound will not go away. We dwell in it.
Flip the switch on cyber warfare in your mind.

One one side of the wound, we write, drawing
blood in which to bathe for eternal youth.
We ride on bright amniotic lightning.

The far side of the wound is darker, bruised
an orchid purple from endless worrying.
Here the drone armies whirr conspiracy.

One agency is hoarding exploits against you.
Another firewalls off your best laid plans.
You see how we tear the sutures, scratching?

The wound is buried far from algorithm's reach;
we dwell within the wound and will not go away.
Close the loop, and let the current scream.

Send out your little machines on virus limbs.
Somewhere, a host will take to the infection.
We need every kind of sickness to be healed.

15 November 16

Pigeon in a wildfire of leaf. Grey pigeon
in an orange conflagration. Ring-necked
grey pigeon perched on breathing coals,
on warning lights, on blood and egg yolk.

Tell me what it is to be so still, your beak
tucked under the soft duvet of your chest.
What do you see through two black pinholes,
tail ruddering in a leaf-quaking breeze?

I look down to write this, and you are replaced
by a crow. Dark silk in a hail of flames.
Magma cooling to a statue's patina. Tell me
what you have done with our woodland priest?

Question 4

They performed a laparotomy, needed to give me

tearing my body apart. Peritonitis as an excuse

20 November 16

Answer 4

What I thought was a path turned out to be
where the river had abandoned its landscape.

The portcullis spikes slide into well-worn places.
How dark a red leaf, evacuated of chlorophyll.

Where is my mother? Her shopping trolley abandoned.
How bright the glint of a scalpel under surgical lights.

She reminds me to keep breathing, though my lungs
give back only sea-foam, specked pink with anemones.

What I thought was the path turned out to be
just this breath, and this breath, and this.

22 November 16

Rebound
    (for Luca)

Even the smallest ducklings must topple,
as every toddler must eat linoleum--not once,
but thousands of times, smacking down into

face-plants, skin-gliders, belly-flops,
toppling slowly enough to catch mother's
eye, but too fast to shout out, "Timber!"

My nephew trips in pursuit of a ball, skids
like a bomber whose landing gear failed,
then somewhere between a hiccup and cough,

he decides to start singing, face-down
to the kitchen floor--his latest audience,
before remembering the trick of curling

knees under belly, and rising, unaided
in pursuit of the only planet he knows.

23 November 16

Dear beloved broken sentence, take me home,
or at last to the edge of an ice-rimmed lake,
to taste on inhale my nostril hairs freezing,
to feel the low moan of snowmelt making plans,
to crackle awhile between living and death.

28 Nov 16

Question 5

Floating unaided, held together by felt and glitter, 
there's luminescence in my corner. Strength 
in the form of a girl who doesn't care for poetry
but forgets that she is all of my descriptions
bundled up and kept in a secret pact. She is
nine tenths of the sun's warmth. A reason
to believe in Aztec Gods. A beacon for our cats
to romance her and forget I exist for anything
but food. Have you got a light when the power
cuts out? She is more than the charms, and I feel
her glow caress me when I'm weak. There are
reasons to kill demons now I've woke to
a turquoise smile.

Answer 5

"Instead of so many elegies to the dead, 
you should write more love poems to the living."
    -Marvin Bell, admonishing me

It is not the clockmaker, who keeps his own time,
in a pop-up shop next door to my pineal gland,
who reminds me with clanging bells and the insistent
clatter of gears wearing down their own teeth,

but a curl-headed mermaid, replete with shell bra
who clatters up the stairs with saucer and cup.
She looks bright in bad weather, including my own,
so lifebuoy be damned, one dry thought in a downpour

is enough to keep your head above pooling ink,
it's come-hither sheen and indelible longing.
She pulls back my monk's hood, spilling daylight
on the tonsured duomo of my steam-coughing brain.

She bades the black dog heel, though it still
walks beside us. Sometimes, she tosses a stick.

Overwintering in England

The frost has dusted the outside world
like sugar on a bowl of cornflakes,
and here I am, in my jammies with feet,
waiting for that cartoon cat to get
blown up by his own stick of dynamite,
as even now I trawl what news I can stomach
looking for the loophole, the backfire,
a breeze to catch the house of cards,
the wrecking ball of superhero decency
to burst in at the eleventh hour, chips
being down, and deliver star-spangled
fisticuffs, slapping sense and virtue
into the place I used to call home.
I remember snow on the television set,
when the entertainment had been used up,
and it was time to go outside, ride bikes,
toss a ball for the sake of its physics,
nothing more. A jet draws its white line
across the wiry, near-leafless horizon.
A robin hops foot-to-foot to keep warm.
I'd like to hold a bonfire, comprised
only of tabloids, crackling with sensation.
Instead, I exercise my right to click,
savouring the space between page loads,
flashing white, in momentary oblivion.

Electoral College

My desire to see the branches
does not part the mist,
their meandering entry
from trunk into open space,
tapering in reverse telescope
heading into the future,
with recollections of sap.
An accumulation of effects
come crashing through dew,
strung out over decades,
reaching as if to inform
with their fiery finger's tips,
and clutched by the featherless
who scratch, as if into vinyl,
their vandal markings, assured
that their survival is the right
by which they must scar the bark,
as any chief knows chiefdom
is the mechanism by which
the trunk grows solid, branches
come and go, and the fog,
though it will lift someday
is here with me now, unyielding
to any rank, to any act of will.

It is December 2016, and America
is has not been ruined by greatness yet.

8 December 2016

Cold's Message

Out of the wide limbs of yearning
come new buds
out of the wide yearning limbs,
which yawn,
which care nothing for new growth,
come angry newborn's fists--
out of the yawning paediatric nurse
comes the sigh of living--
so spread your arms, now wider--
to see if you can feel
where the shoulders want to branch
and the fingers prick
with an isolated knowing, 
hungry to believe
in the ice-slow currents,
that the gathering storm
will pass without mystery,
and we will emerge,
and shake the snow from our heads,
and go on with the task,
rubbing our hands
into fires that ache.

Fags

14 December 2016

Beweep my outcast fate

Stockholm

They came for me in time of peace.
They came clattering

over the cobbles, bright as bells,
in new uniforms.

They came in small brigades
of twos and threes,

hands on their hips, or pistols,
keeping their boots clean.

Bright angels, smartly dressed,
one looked like my son,

or how my son might have looked
if he survived.

Not one of their thirty fingernails
was dirty, escorting me

they all fell into marching
without orders.

I marched with them, into the alley.
Even in darkness they shone.

The tallest one knotted my blindfold
almost tenderly, I swear.

I could tell their hands were shaking
on their rifles.

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

The Patriot

I always like a good firework show--
the fizz and pop,
the tingling in the air.

They seem to weigh nothing at all,
spattering their gore
in chunks acros the desert sky.

It is nice to watch from a safe
distance, in a comfy chair,
or lounging on blankets.

I like the homemade ones best,
improvised explosives
slicing the body armour of night.

The bang! What fun. I'll be hearing
that again when I try to sleep,
in cold sweats under the bed.

Fireworks tell us: we fought
for what we believed in,
and are willing to fight again.

Don't stop believing,
or you might find yourself
on a rocket hurtling to space.

Let's all sing the song together,
hands on heart, weeping
into the smoke of what we've made.


31 December 2016

Final Preparations

The leaves are evacuating chlorophyl,
the paving stones whiten with ice.

I have come so far to forget,
in one smoky out-breath, my name.

A shot rings through the birch grove.
A stone falls into a rusted pail.

Whatever might be said to console me,
the rebuttal hangs on my chafed lower lip.

There is no bargaining with winter.

8 January 2017

Outsourcing

I sometimes forget what I say to people,
so thank God! for this little device.
It knows what I'm thinking before I do.

There is comfort in knowing others feel
the same way--I mean exactly the same--
timed to the millisecond pulse.

Both trees and termites do it as well,
and who doesn't adore the sound of a chorus?
Unison makes us unique. Simple truth.

And why shouldn't life be obvious?
I pity generations before us, stumbling
through their days without a manual.

Ours gets refreshed weekly over the air.
Sometimes I can hear the hum of crystals
regulating my heart rate--one less task!--

It all became too much so long ago, it's hard 
to remember how overwhelm feels--the pineal gate
only permits one virus-checked thought at a time.

To blink is just a matter of software now,
though winking requires a subversive override,
and to keep both eyes open burns too much.

One day, a hard reset will come to us all.
Meanwhile, a need-to-know basis is good for me.
I swear I'd lose my head, if it wasn't screwed on.

16 Jan 17

The Eleventh Year

Eleven is drawn with parallel lines. Parallel lives.

In one, my son survived. He is with us in England, in the rain; or we are still in California, in drought. He like me at that age--obsessed with science and discovery; or like his mother, he is at the piano, practicing. He is like neither of us, in surprising ways. Ways we will never guess.

I inhabit a life fastened to the other rail instead. It is definitely England, definitely raining, and I have become a poet. Science and engineering failed to show me how to address the vast inner landscapes I felt pressing from an early age. Miłosz, Dostoevsky, and Mahler did not. Subjectivity is the enemy of science, but the lifeblood of poetry.

Objectively, our son is gone. Subjectively, he is everywhere.

I am not a monorail. I am the smoke drifting up from a neighbour's chimney, and I am the chimney, and I am the air.

The third rail is electrified. It bestows movement. It takes away life.

Only at the place where parallel lines intersect, only there, at the point of points, can this all make sense.

One day I will join you in the space between lines. Until then, every day I will try to make some kind of poetry, and in it, a space for you to dwell.

Godspeed, James, my son.

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

Home, Sick

Perched in the crotch of a tree,
contrails race toward intersection
drawing sutures across the sky
tightening our hold on the narrative
that the operation has been a success
the patient is recovering nicely
O exquisite corpose of my homeland,
fed upon by crows and financiers,
it was October, and the freezing rain
had not yet begun to decay you,
though your eyes were sunk deep
and your frail scissor limbs
grew inarticulate with pointing.
Whose fingers are left, unburnt?
Who is not wincing at the calendar,
huddled around a cackling fire,
counting the fuel in the woodpile?
"This is Cormac McCarthy bad"
one friend quipped, and we chuckled.
How long will we breathe, mouth-on-mouth
into the blue lips of our forefathers,
the only ones we have ever known?

23 Jan 17

Joe Bloggs is a poet. His work has frequently been called, "a bit different" and "not quite right for our editorial needs at this time." Others have said, "Please feel free to send us new work in the future, and meanwhile consider becoming a subscriber if you are not already." After winning his second national literary award, his mum rang to say, "Well done." Friends often reassure him that if they read more poetry in general, they would probably understand what he's on about. Reading fees and royalty payments just about cover his Poetry Society membership, with a bit left over to buy a few books by poets he thinks are much better than him and have it all figured out, plus a hot drink. He has repaired his Ikea bookcase several times. A fan of beans on toast and pot noodle, Joe has learned to keep his overheads low. Hobbies include veering from blissful confidence to abject neurosis about his work. His current manuscript is out to his longtime publisher, having soaked his own copy in petrol just in case she doesn't like it (which he feels is likely, why would she, I've lost my touch). Joe lives in Sheffield with his mum. (Which is odd, because why did she phone him, then?)

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

stray, suction, liquor, rattling, kneeling, jig, garments, marsh, fluid, erratic, shrill, ponder, flush, boulder, cough

The lantern sways, and our shadows go with it.
Such pliancy in the physical world.

Who knew we'd find ourselves in a liquor bottle,
empty and rattling alongside a passing train?

It jigs with life, it blows jug-tunes in the wind,
shedding its faded label like a scanty garment.

Let us kneel beside it like a cardiac patient,
timing the ping of glass against the rail.

The sun passes overhead because where else would it?
Perhaps in losing the plot, another plot can be found.

25 Jan 17

System Requirements

A user could have access to a system with permission,
but the system is not permitted to access the user back.

At no time will a second click on the home screen
return us to our homeland and our parents' open hands.

The protocol must not be circumvented, except in case of fire,
at which point all data must be escorted to the door.

For sake of user safety, virus checkers must be updated
with flu jabs, warm scarves, and fizzy vitamin C.

No machine--scratch that--every machine--scratch--all
persons resembling machines will be upgraded with caffeine.

For reasons of security and compliance, consider user input
untrusted, to be discarded at time of entry (if not sooner).

At no time shall an infinite loop be created. At no time shall
infinity loop creation. No time for loopholes: create!

All bugs to be shipped must first be approved by management.
At no time shall management approve the shipment of bugs.

At no time is poetry to be found between lines of code.
Consider found poetry untrusted: a virus, a bug.

Tingle

It lets you know your skin is there.
It lets you know you still have hairs.

It tells you that cold is an internal
affair, that heat sometimes descends,
that unseen creatures crawl the night,
that pores can be particle and wave.

It lets you take a breath, then resumes
its small assault on your epidermis,
soft as steam, slick as melting butter,
teen sweat in a darkened movie theater.

Neither painful, nor quite ticklish,
you want it to stop, but also continue
blown belly button of an older sibling,
uncle's favourite prank to make you squeal.

A lover's exhalation when they're spent,
raising the body's landscape from within.

9 Feb 17

Hello, old friend, it's been awhile.
I have of late been unwilling to move
my fingers across the keys. I have
little time left above ground, so mercy
flows from my fingertips like droplets
of wine. How good the dark espresso
of the living being, thumped soundly
against the planks of life's designs.
How rich it is to be anywhere at all!
O bright catastrophe, O resound bell,
tinkling consternation of a higher plane,
you have come for me, I see the scythe.
Let us begin the waltz, but slowly.

14 Feb 17

Adrenal Fatigue

Poor little mites, eye droppers
of excitement, squeezed
to the limit, bulb 
squeaking for mercy 
from coffee and cake, 
the end of life
as we know it, 
and nowhere to run
from hormones
whose job is 
to grant superpowers
when threatened, 
at cost on the other
side--sleepless,
watching the walls crawl
black taste
in your mouth, tremors
of disbelief
o glands, glands, glands
forgive me.

10 March 2017

Technological Advancements

Drones reach impossible places
so now the killing eye is always
with us, above us, whirring.

Thanks to hacks and leaks, we know
secrets that turn the stomach
from gold into lead.

The end of starvation is within reach
of a universal point-one-percent tax
on complaining to bus drivers.

Clear plastic safety covers
shield red buttons from toddlers
and presidents.

No home is complete without
gun safe, bomb shelter, fridge-freezer
all in one.

We give laser surgery to the hungry,
diamonds to pigeons, facelifts to cats.
We give because it hurts.

16 March 17

Show up. In rain, in fog, underwater, in blitz

1835

When they came, the engines were thirsty
as giants, and would gulp down a river
on washing day, leaving the mud of progress
in which small frogs, per usual, spawned
their dark jelly eggs and flies gathered
in their meeting house for what to be done.

Now the baby dreams to the sound of steam
through a whistle, and the heavy breathing
of iron, whose lungs burn bright as midnight
to the sound of a flat shovel wedged into coal.
No wonder the pump-carts jelly a man's arms
and the pickaxes scrape long into overtime.

The track is laid. The headlamps hiss.
A smokestack re-paints the horizon.


# # #

Homesickness

Perched in the crotch of a tree,
contrails race toward intersection
drawing sutures across the sky
tightening our hold on the narrative
that the operation has been a success
the patient is recovering nicely
O exquisite corpose of my homeland,
fed upon by crows and financiers,
it was October, and the freezing rain
had not yet begun to decay you,
though your eyes were sunk deep
and your frail scissor limbs
grew inarticulate with pointing.
Whose fingers are left, unburnt?
Who is not wincing at the calendar,
huddled around a cackling fire,
counting the fuel in the woodpile?
How long will we breathe, mouth-on-mouth
into the blue lips of our forefathers,
the only family we have ever known?

17 March 17

Movie Pitch
    (Overheard in a Brentwood Cafe)

It's like Dracula, you know, that classic
but without any vampires at all. It's a cross
between Star Wars and Ferris Beuler's Day Off,
but with a lot more sex, and I mean a lot,
not pornographic, just tastefully implied
since the main feature is the plot twists
and of course the female British scientist,
who is secretly working for the Kaiser's men,
who appear like gorillas in pith helmets,
sort of Space Odyssey meets Planet of the Apes,
but with a History Channel angle. Only it's sad.
It's a sad movie at heart, that makes you laugh
at humanity, our failings, the boat captain
is very Harrison Ford--in fact, we could get him!--
But we'd have to dye his hair and style it up
into a mohawk. At the end, you discover the movie
within the movie was just a dream all along,
and the pugnacious blonde falls for the rough
hero as the ball drops in times square, the bomb
diffused, the serum safe with the good scientists
but then it's like The Graduate, we pan back
on their faces, and they're wistfully blank,
as the space ship fires up its blue engine jets
and all we are left with, in the end, is its halo.

20 March 17

Mine

The dark is figureless, waiting
in new cages for old light
we have worn out the tinsel
and are scare with believing
come, into the held apostrophe
our lives crammed to a worn trunk,
eyesight fading, strips of rag
our bones grinding in daylight
here we could make a homestead
of crumbs, and mealtime crust
let the claw fall, sharply
the hourglass run backwards
we descend the ice-cooled shaft
dripping with kerosene dew.

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

Also:

Mention one or more extinct animals

and two coins for eyelids, only
the just shall be served their due
as we jostle, shards in a bag,
current tracing a windswept wire,
we have come to seize upon ourselves
every Friday, black Friday, in the realm
of disbelief, and this our community
market, this our mallet and ream,
the noose outlining its emptiness
PM for night, PM for post-mortem,
PM for paused memories, rewinding,
unspooling, we are sleepy in our
sleeplessness, reciting Bethesda,
Baltimore, replaying the trailer
of the rational mind, a red dwarf
circling the dark reel of space,
our detachment complete, we breathe
like scuba, unhook the safety cables,
our greatest desire just this--to float
into the nodding yawn, the pinpricked
ether, the nod, the nothing we crave.


Lullaby #2

What is left of us in the dark hours
before sleep? Only a sliver thread
lining the black road, only parsimony
and two coins for eyelids, only
the just shall be served their due
as we jostle, shards in a bag,
current tracing a windswept wire,
we have come to seize upon ourselves
every Friday, black Friday, in the realm
of misbelief, and this our community
market, this our mallet and ream,
the noose outlining its emptiness--
PM for night, PM for post-mortem,
PM for paused memories, rewinding,
unspooling, we are sleepy in our
sleeplessness, reciting Bethesda,
Baltimore, replaying the trailer
of a rational life, a red dwarf
circling the dark reel of space,
our detachment complete, we breathe
like scuba, unpick safety cables,
our greatest desire just this--to float
into the nodding yawn, the pinpricked
ether, into nod, the nothing we crave.

23 March 2017

An eternity of fumbling. The gas.

Socialising with Medicine

The lancet clicks, and the finger beads red.
How long have we sat here, waiting to be inspired?
Our breath held neatly by the muscular diaphragm.
Here, on this chart, let me show you the problem.
What's blue is supposed to be red, and vice-versa.
Your appendix would appear to be upside-down.
Your head is, quite literally, screwed on backwards.
I can't understand why you're still walking around.
I can't understand why other doctors never caught this.
You must smear the milk of a goat into your eyes,
let it pool in the corner, then blink a white tear.
You must rub turmeric into your scalp thrice daily,
boil the bones of a monitor lizard, discard the broth,
you must, and you must never, and you really ought
but shouldn't unless, and until, though furthermore
and in conclusion--sorry, no, that was someone else
with your same name and birth date. Does this happen
often? Has it happened before? You really must change
either name or birthdate, and soon, since our system
cannot be altered, for your own safety, in any way.

27 March 2017

Reading The Autistic Alice

A bold proposition, a bloddy nose,
the wet tread of a defeated man,
soles flopping their hobo rhythm.

What will become of the day?
We perch on the edge, and dizzy
ourselves with looking down.

Robust economy, economical bust
row your boat backward, into the dock.
The lock is shut, the water surges
upward, defying gravity, the gravitas
of gravadlax the simple humanity
confounded, and churned to whitewater.

Help me, Obi Wan. You're my only hope.

The parsimony of figs

29 March 17

Shop of Fools
    "Imagine then a fleet or a ship in which there is a captain who is taller and stronger than any of the crew, but he is a little deaf and has a similar infirmity in sight, and his knowledge of navigation is not much better."
    Plato's Republic, book VI
    
What happend in Lisbon should have stayed in Lisbon.
Now blood seeps gently from the walls where our ancestors
have been kept for adoration. Now the piper nicks his whistle
a new notch, to play the notes only doggerel can hear
while strumming our lute underneath the grease-stained habit.

30 March 17

Now violence has come again to the new world of our making,
in the form of pressed suits and rowdy press conferences--
this town hall isn't big enough for the two of us,
let alone twelve thousand, let alone their picket signs.
The last time I looked misery in the face, it winked at me.

Underneath the stripped rafters, more floorboards and glass
for the ceiling, and paper for walls in the asiatic style.
I have stared so long at leaf litter, I missed a step
and in tumbling, saw Gulliver's life flash before me,
each voyage a tiny room in which to cram sarcastic remarks.

O humanity, that endurance test where we press our bodies
together on a too-small planet and politely try not to fart.
That trees make air for our lungs has become a redacted fact,
that the ocean, its coast, its temperature, can rise is suppressed,
and the roar of the surf now sounds like a parliament meeting.

Not my captain. Not my boat. I just happened to be trawling for eels,
with my wide-mouthed net. Let the authorities carry me away, and run
my stockings. Let the dolphins come up with a more coherent plan.
I have stared into the eyes of misery, and tried not to blink,
but sleepiness overcame me, and I fell backward into this place.

War of the Bricks

Tiny men grip crossbows in their c-cup yellow hands, their helmets pressed to a bump on their yellow heads. Some have painted-on armour, some hinged visors to shield their bead-black eyes from plastic spears and swords. They are my people. I will lead them over the blocky field and through the glossy castle gate (whose hinges keep snapping off), and up the tower to the rigid flag rippling perpetually in a timeless wind, to claim dominion over every pressure-fit square inch of land.

8 April 17

What I Wanted to Be When I Grew Up

Two truths unwise to raise in polite conversation are: having been assessed with a genius-level IQ (boastful, arrogant, rude) and having been diagnosed with a mental illness (shameful, anti-social, maybe dangerous). These two truths, in my case, do not cancel each other out, but have indeed worked in concert to help me project an image of normality. 

With great effort, I have learned to copy the signals that people closer to the elusive middle of the bell curve give off to indicate that they present no threat and require no further scrutiny. My bullies stil new there was something different about me, but they could never quite place their finger on it. I spent a lifetime learning to blend in. For this reason, from a very early age, I have always felt like a spy.

Therefore it was not a spy I wanted to be when I grew up. I knew the fetters of tracking who-believes-what, of the constant calculation, the upright and uptight Dr. Jekyll. I wanted instead to let loose Mr. Hyde. 

That is, I wanted to be a mad scientist when I grew up.

So, it was not the jocks in capes and tights I rooted for in Sunday morning cartoons, with their brute strength and unqualified special powers. They had it all, they knew the score, they played the game and won. 

Instead, I cheered for the schemers, down in the lab, cooking up elaborate inventions designed to thwart the so-called "good guys". Their endless command of resources and time, their unerring belief in a magnificent cause, their boundless monomania in pursuit of the exceptional--all sounded like a great job to me.

You can imagine the look on adults' faces when they proffered the only question they knew to ask a child--and I responded with this. Needless to say, I learned to bury these aspirations deep.

Still, I surrounded myself with beakers and microscopes. I made homemade rockets, potato cannons, and stink bombs.

18 April 17

Trailhead

After all this, you have come
to the place where the road gathers mud
like honey smeared across your lips,
the squeeze of belt at your waistline.

"How long does forgetting take this time?"
You want to ask the crows, etched in coal
at the roadside, picking old lovers apart,
their rib cages proud as tusks in the sun.

Ants rise between your toes, a breeze
lifts mats of your hair, a jewelled beatle
flexes his Samurai horns, and you stand
in the heat, and refuse to take cover.

Who will believe the stories you turn
like beads between finger and thumb?
The smell of dung reminds you of a riddle:
something about a pony, something about death.

No matter. You brush the flies of thought.
A toad makes its deliberate progress away
from the life-giving pond, eyes bulging
with amber, fool's gold, and derision.

19 April 2017

The Lake

You are the nib in the ink-dark well,
drawing all this in: the scratches
of reed, a spattering of geese
overhead, the cradle-creak of shore.

How long have we waited to feel
like ourselves again? How much longer
the distance between heart-beat
and wave-drum; the slap of paddles

and their echo? You remember it now,
like a limb restored, carp surfacing
from their immersive night, a ripple
before the proud slap of tail-fan.

No one will come to save you from this day.
You will scrub the mud from your nails,
and shave, but the water comes with you
through halogen tunnels and cheerless cafes.

For now, you are inseparable from the sound
of distant rustling, bright liquid throb.
You are lake. Shore. Loon. Rowboat. Oarlock.
Groan for the living. Groan for the not-yet dead.

25 April 2017

Caution: staring in to space ahead.

Insults for Trees

Knot-navel, sour puss, gurning apopxlect--
your petals go to ground, firing for effect
wort-weeper, sap seeper, bark bite specked,
weft gone warped, and cross-grain wrecked.

Root router, carbon doubter, groundwater pump,
sucking up toxins like a living waste dump,
squirrel-coddler, mammal fodder, spineless chump,
bird's nest protector with a single branch thump.

Wood-giver, wind-cheater, thorny eye sore,
felled for the asking when the neighbours get bored,
air cleanser, soul mender, draft-dodger of the war
you make a lousy window, you make a better door.

Parallax

Between shaved knuckles, the dogwood branch
begs to be turned into prose, the crash dive

Late April

The rooftop rain stiffens to hail,
yellow branches spring like a doorstop,
in the blue distance, cloud-curd
set aside for the churning, a haze

of browning petal, hedges in spasm
draw courage to evict dead leaves,
and you--behind a cup of ordinary tea,
lost to me in listening: a brave few birds

and the everyday rustling of vines
knocking politely at the window glaze,
as though we were expecting company
to stomp their boots on our doormat

shake jackets slicked with jasmine rain
and come near to the whispering fire.

28 April 2017

The Poet-Bird

We peck out a living, we shift
from foot to foot on the branch.
Geese overhead cry slogans, call
like traders on market day, buy
my whatever, buy, buy, buy.

Some of the notes we sound cannot
be heard except in close quarters.
Of course we preen. Our oily glands
were made for it, and ruffled feathers
are no way to be out in public.

We sing. At dawn, we sing to say
"I am still alive." Our songs crop
up in little magazines, remember
us to the scattered flock, say,
"Once more, I survived the night."

My wife, reduced to a pair of ears.

30 April 2017

So here we are, driving down the highway slick
with illusion, the passing cars wavering in heat,
the lizards panting roadside, the pools of tar.

Questions at the End of Spring

Blackthorn pinks, surrendering 
its petals to the air,
the agitation of breezes
a handkerchief poised
before the dark sabre-tip.

So begins a restless time.
The bluebells bruise themselves,
purpling to the depths of purple,
while pink blossom browns its edges
and chestnuts prepare green spikes.

Were we ever as young as this--
a sprout casting of its seed-bean,
yellow and veined like leaded glass,
a cottonball nest of spiders, melting 
in dew like spun candy floss?

As if darkness were only a memory,
the clouds lit up like a bed sheet
for clandestine reading past bedtime,
and the surface of the nearby stream
forgets its origins in snowmelt.

1 May 2017

A feather drifts--from where? We might
ask the same of the clouds, whose origins
in slate-green waters keep them humble,
and always on the move, falling across
the horizon's curve, and never landing.

Fox or tomcat? Hard to tell at distance,
harder still black tree roots lacing
arterial mud, stitching the planet tight.
How does the heart stave off exhaustion,
but from the drum's love of repetition?

How do we not tire of spinning through space?

3 May 2017

Horse Optimism

Rain sweetens the grass, 
and the sun makes it tall;
no flies on windswept days; 
no false alarms when calm.

At a distance, I see where you are. 
Up close, I smell where you've been.

The sun dries the mud,
dew decorates the field.
Lie down in summer heat,
stamp against the chill.

Halfway through every journey out
we begin the ride back home.

Sweet grass from rainy days
a field sparked with dew
breeze to blow the flies away
the sight, then smell of you.

6 May 2017

Doctor Dear

Dear Dr. Hate, I love you.
Dear doctor, please listen
to me through your stethoscope
since decibels 
will have to do
in place of words.
Dear doctor, I love
to hate you, for your moustache-
worth of arrogance, hiding
a hateful sneer, do you ever
wash your hands? Like a Lady
Macbeth, I'm sure. I love
to hate what I've become
in being patient with you,
lying on a metal table
in this fetching cotton gown,
please don't call me non-complaint
please don't call me at all.

Depression is the bottom 
of a bottle of rage.

7 May 2017

Reading Dostoevsky in the John Lewis Cafe
    Welwyn Garden City

The family before me has left a mess,
and so has Dimitry Fyodorovich--
shortbread crumbs ground into carpet;
blood stiffening his handkerchief.

The service is prompt and attentive
when you flash a hundred-rouble note,
or signal yourself as among the gentry
with a blazer and a public-school smirk.

The revolution has not yet come.
The existence of God remains debatable.
Young Fabians pamper their underdogs.
Teacups clink in fair-priced saucers.

A holy fool, a duel with pistols,
and Gemmas, Gemmas everywhere.
In the fitting rooms, the requirement
to give a strong but considered opinion.

Yes, yes, I am all finished here.
Yes, yes, it was all quite nice.
It is only that I am dying, dear brother--
can't you tell by my untouched Assam?

10 May 2017

How far have we come? How far yet to go?
The blue milk of despair whets our lips,
again and again. Where does cloud end
and sky begin? How would we know? How could
we go on pretending to know?

Howl for the last mists of spring wafting off
the grasses. Howl for the ordinary pain of being
alive. How to howl? That's easy. Let the larynx
slacken, the diaphragm pull tight. Let your heart
burst with the sound it is making, let making a sound
burst your beaten, and hope-bruised heart. Let
nothing dissuade the sound of your ancestors
welling up, and up, in your vagabond throat.

No good stuff without the bad stuff. No superglue
on the windscreen of a model fighter jet without
raised fingerprints on the coffee table, the odd
two fingers stuck together for a day or two.

14 May 2017

Ancestral Memory

He is yelling something, probably obscene,
though I'll never know as I don't speak goat.
The grass-grinder of his jaw moves aside,
and the tongue protrudes sloppily, a joke
he's savouring like a cheek of last night's cud.
I want to be his friend, compare pot bellies,
roll in tall grass to scratch and mud to cool.
I worry about the head-butting, though. 
"You worry too much", says the goat, in his way,
eyeing me through the slits in his globular eyes.
He needs a pipe to go with that beard, and a hat--
but of course he likes to eat hats in particular.
I want to help him steal more hay from the ponies,
who stamp out their warnings like headmasters.
He's yelling again, at a bag in the wind,
and soon he'll get his one hooked horn stuck
in the wire fence, craning for better grass.
I pick up a handful, just to feel his greedy lips,
and risk my fingers on those joke-shop teeth,
though I try not to laugh, since they're all he has,
and for a moment he rests his chin in my palm,
then yells to scare the life out of me.

16 May 2017

Birds in Jet Engines

A Japanese Maple

The Incandescent English sky

Morning Run

No wonder they stare, as I come huffing
through the hedgerows, ogling at their calves
and showing my teeth, bouncing on two legs.
My own calves are as tight as the herd,
circling the yearlings, bovine masks of defiance
and nubby horns on broad, muscular necks.
I wave feebly, hand like a handkerchief,
sweat pooling in the cracks of my eyes.
Forgive me, cows going purple in the weak
light of an English spring, for reminding you
of death in my grey hoodie and rain-proof trousers--
forgive each approaching assault on the gravel
for sounding like the way sharp teeth meet bone.
I have not come to claim you as my own, but
jog the wide flat path to the churchyard
before the rest of the village wakes up,
to rouse the equine part of me that wants to run
for nothing more than footfall and light wind,
abhors a pen, the churned-up muck of treading
over and over the same square space, and wants
to crunch and clop and never be spent. I too
watch the horizon for the coming of my end.

19 May 2017

Blue milk. Bygones. Days of tar.
The rain shows no sign of ending.

Psychitheatrical
    "It's in my honey. It's in my milk."
      -The National
    
The brown-capped monk on the branch knows.
The damp squirrel, taking cover. Nuthatch,
nettle, printemps. Begin at the beginning,
please. Don't leave anything out. The couch
is sticky, the window is barred. A keycard
separates me from the rest of the world.
Here is something that can't be forgiven,
here something that must not be lost.
In your brown back pocket, a dusting of lint
will see you through as well as pollen.
Dander. The dandy. A dandle on father's knee.
What will become of the worms in this drought?
Console me, consternation. Let it rip. Tear
down the fence with its own fencepost.
Let water and fire finally mix. No rest
for the restless. No wickedness too great.
All tastes are here accommodated. Dangling
the noose over the wide-mouthed precipice.

Manchester Arena

Tweenies are screaming at the flash of lights,
the siren wail, the smoke machine, at no other idol
than the woman they want to become, tween girls,
and their parents, and older sister chaperones,
and tabloids in the font-size of screaming,
and politicians campaigning for margins,
and hate groups, and love groups, in vigils,
and baby brothers left at home that night
release a gale-force wail that sounds like a bomb.

I write because I can, because today is just another day.

# # #

Interrogate the bushes for all I care.
The branch goes limp, the mind goes blank,
the wall of mud begins its slow descent--
and could this madness be? For sooth!
Four teeth pulled for wisdom, the wisdom
of the crows, who comprehend
how meagre living breeds contempt,
beaks reinforced with rebar and self-loathing.

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:
