# # #

Where does the liquid yearning go
when the ventricles constrict
and the blood we once feared
and cherished goes back to dust?

They wander through the light-
stained night, eyes quivering.

I have seen it coming--the dawn
I can no more welcome than prevent,
reddening the foam on the shoreline,
whispering in the tops of the trees.

It speaks to me in bone splinter,
in dancing leaf, in scattered twigs.
I spin, I scry, I prophesy.
I was never doing it for my kin.

O bright headlamp of self-pity
guide me down the mine
into the ash, the potash,
the burning sulphur of mind.

The room has gone quiet
with the sound of their delight
falling like a forced smile,
eyes overfed repenting the meal.

# # #

By the station even the dogs don't like the smell
of last night's excess cabbage and the piss
of friends becoming strangers as they stare
into the grates past darkness into darkness
and the still, sulphuric waters going green.
Even the old men by the station in their suits
adjust their sunglasses agains the morning glare.
Imagine--a poet actually stares out of a window.
A green-masked man dances to the old tunes alone.

Liqueur the red of martyrs' stained-glass blood
and the green between plates on countertops.
The shine of zinc and bistro brass, the clean,
worn padded booths in red or green, trimmed
in oak, the varnish softened gummy with age.
Men of an era who still read newspapers read
here as they always have, their usual breakfast
laid patiently before them like a waiting dog.
Good-smelling tobacco enjoys itself indoors
as the matron turns an eye and smirk aside.
They fold themselves into the nation's affairs,
appearing unmoved behind the terrible news.

# # #

To live anywhere else would be the same,
but different, the gearbox shifting, the coin
in the slot--to feel this deeply is an unwrapped
gift, too easily mistaken as an afterthought,
or meant for another, left on the bus discarded
into the cleansing fires of daily ritual.

Unquestioned at the border, unrecognised at home.

My last breakup was a rage quit.

23 October 2018

Self-Portrait in Smartphone

The thumbs up, the purple heart,
and a man drooling for tacos
are my three most-used emojis.

But enough about me, let's see
some photos taken at arms length
in places you'll wish you'd been.

I do it without protection--
no plastic case, just glass
and metal against my skin.

I cover my screen, however--
a thin hard film prevents
scuffles, breakups, and glare.

My favourite apps let me chat
without raising my voice--
conversations end with a swipe.

That's me on the toilet, 
tapping whatever remains unread
to clear my conscience.

Sometimes Freud autocorrects
the words I wish I'd typed:
"Home sorry. Please want."

# # #

Tiny Flying Helicopters

Contrary to popular belief, the waiter is doing just fine.
Look at him, with his bored hauteur, his coffin-nail eyes,
the way he lays the beef on the plate on the table, almost sexual
yet also graveyard-like, the scoop of parmesan redolent of souls.

I was thinking the waft of cheese is the scent of the dead,
when out from my malaise popped a more hopeful thought indeed:
perhaps, in heaven, fondue year-round beside a tinfoil fire,
our ski-ankles aching from a day of making snow-angels for real.

But then, of course, it was back to dismay, my old baseline note,
that drone--and not like tiny flying helicopters--that plays
as I abrade life's surface: a teflon pan with plastic bristles,
my knee on concrete (a classic), Marmite against toast.

I almost wrote "a teflon plan", the holy grail of boardrooms.
I am not a businessman, but I'd like to think there's something
that can glide as if permanently buttered through the viscous mud
so tugging at my quotidian ankles (not the ski-lift kind), even now.

I am also not a waiter, though I appreciate their service to mankind,
even whilst lightly ribbing the ones who feign un-death to survive.
Ironic, isn't it? How we do so many un-living things to keep alive?
I, for example, stuff my corpse self into a blue suit and too-small shoes.

I have been through boardrooms and restaurants, and know which I prefer.
Bring the good-smelling basket of bread, lined with a white serviette.
Bring the profit-and-loss statements decorated in black and red.
Bring me a person who can honestly say: "Yes, I am doing just fine."


# # #

What it Says on the Tin

We're fresh out of pity, I'm afraid.
It's normally aisle four, top shelf,
kept out of reach of small children.

Erotic Dutchess I think was the brand,
a sentimental noun, brined with romance
and a hint of graveyard flavouring.

Don't let the scent of pie disprove
your blurry notion of unsavouriness,
the world o'er knows it as rot.

Uncle Sam's Blazing Pistol is another,
gun sights cooling on your stern gaze
as you scan the ingredients carefully.

Then there's the generic blue-label,
not quite the same--bean-scented, mealy,
it sticks to the pan like semen.

Be sure to refrigerate after opening.
It won't keep long, and in the end,
once soured, it's best served cold.

6 November 2018

Vox Pop

The people have spoken, and the word was, "Hate."
It was not yet a crime since even war has its rules.
In their hearts the people knew they deserved better--
and only a trigger would quiet the ones on their knees.

They spoke their minds for once, and out came tar.
They said what they meant, and it burned on contact.
The people were pleased not to be the ones on trial.
They felt safe in the knowledge their kind was not next.

The people crushed stones in their teeth, in their hands.
They marvelled how red the blood, and cited tradition.
In the night, small groups gathered to bandage the wounds.
But soon the people found them, and justice cut quick.

The people snuffed the stars with their calloused thumbs.
The people hoisted up gallows, and the ropes didn't burn.
All this the people built on the stones of their ancestors,
whose good intentions they muttered through gritted teeth.

The people laced up their boots and went to work.
They pulled down temple brick and lit wooden chapels.
They gave themselves permission to dream even bigger.
All this from a word on a ballot written in pencil.

The people will not stop marching to the beat of their drum.
The drum will not stop beating, though some shield their head.
To cover your ears is to signal you're not of the people.
Such people get caught under foot; progress is like that.

The people spoke, the people listened, the people worked.
Their hands grew thicker, their executions routine.
The people knew their mind, and wadded the ballots.
They loaded pencils into their guns, and that was that.

The people clasped their huge hands together and shook.
They had written the new traditions together in ink.
Other words  became inferior, then immoral, then illegal.
In their hearts the people knew their kind could be next.

Doce Razones para Perseverar
    para M.B.
    
1.   El gato picandome los tobillos cuando salgo del baño.
2.   Las notas arriba de las notas en la canción del mirlo.
3.   El momento antes de quedarse dormido, cuando estás libre. 
4.   La manera en que las bromas malas me duele el costado.
5.   La geometría agradable de los azulejos de baño.
6.   Café de lujo que sabe a bourbon.
7.   Esa paloma valiente que no será espantada.
8.   Cabinas telefónicas rojas en un ambiente de café y gris.
9.   Números irracionales partiendo de risa a nuestras sumas.
10. Árboles más viejos que yo, o cualquier persona viviente.
11. Metiendo un brazo en mi abrigo y encontrando una bufanda.
12. Las farolas rodeados en la neblina como fanales chinas.


Twelve Reasons to Go On
	for M.B.

1.   The cat nipping my ankles when I emerge from the bath.
2.   The notes above the notes in blackbird song.
3.   The moment before falling asleep, when you are free.
4.   The way bad jokes make my side hurt when I laugh.
5.   The pleasant geometry of bathroom tiles.
6.   Fancy coffee that tastes like bourbon.
7.   That one brave pigeon that won't be shooed.
8.   Red phone booths in a wash of beige and grey.
9.   Irrational numbers thumbing their noses at our sums.
10. Trees much older than me, or any person alive.
11. Sticking an arm in my coat and finding a scarf.
12. Street lamps globed in mist like Chinese lanterns.

Kassandra

I have seen it coming--the dawn I can no more 
welcome than prevent, reddening the foam 
on the shoreline, whispering in the high trees.

"No moon without darkness," my father would say.
So the eclipse washes over us, like ink
from a bottle, spreading its test of sanity.

"What do you see?" the beard-strokers ask.
Their pencils grow excited, shaping phrases:
"On mental state examination the seer was kempt."

"Dress as good as you feel," my father would say.
I have run out of black blouses and spiked chokers.
So I gild the Matryoshka doll with glittering soot.

Naturally, the shell will be cracked from within.
So too the membrane between believer and believed.
For my voice is shrill, and there are exit polls.

Let us walk the line where the sand goes dark.
The ocean smells of mucus, tears, and blood.
While the silver waves promise only: "We come."

Biploar Bear

My pawprints crunch the newly-laid snow.

12 November 2018

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

sensation, reverse, mildew, ay, rubble, david, honk, purgatory, bower, circus, tufted, anew, brisk, waist, cows

Also:

Refer to several different beverages

Narwhal

Neither whale, nor walrus, rhinoceros of the sea.
I have spared the toothy details, tusk-tusked
the nay-sayers, probed with my tongue for spurs,
while you slice the dark purgatory beneath.

Deep drinker of the froth where air meets sea,
spend-thrift spindrift, parsimonious porpoise,
bighorn cattle, sans symmetry, plus fin--
let the harpooners get the point for once.

A true patriot would hoist a flag up there,
say the men in dark shirts making policy,
who would have you wrapped in domestic cable,
and hoisted onto the shores of the fatherland.

Instead, you suckle young in the open sea
defiant of sharks, their multi-rowed teeth,
gathering barnacles as a mother would,
weaklings flashing at your flanks.

Go north, young horse of the deep.
Crest once for yes, and never for no.
Say, "nay", say "nar", stay safe awhile
slicing through nets and plastic rings.

Nor war, nor waste, nor wanton flabbergast
can turn your flab to butter on our plates,
light you up via lamps in smoky bulwarks,
for you belong, while you can, to the sea.

19 November 2018

I Lie and Say I’m Canadian

Migration 2018

The geese are brexiting for winter,
dragging long dark coats across the sky.

They swoop low, mimicking the pound,
and cackle like politicians over brunch.

I can’t help but love their long throats,
want to wrap them in European pearls,

wonder if they will survive the crossing
or sink like self-pity into the sea.

I myself am running out of neck to extend,
wonder how many journyes I have left.

The down comfort of a duvet is calling me
smooth as a cloud weeping over the land.

Their dark eyes watch me as I sleep,
their wing beats quiet as breath.

23 Nov 18

worked on the above

1 Dec 18

Frostbitten

How long have we worshipped
the furnace of the sun?

Yet we stand on solid water
making shade with our boots.

Watch, in the blue crevasse
how the shadows gather weight

testing in creaks and groans
sharpening in silver.

It will be ice that overtakes me.
My eyes will freeze, but open.

Until then, the cackle of snow
gives back the print of my foot.

Until then, a dance of shivers,
and the blanket of dusk coming low.

3 Dec 2018

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

clutching, marvel, dreamy, conceal, kenneth, psalm, wife's, wrought, cuffs, squeeze, gutter, misfortune, lighted, forsaken, meets

Also:

Include a musical instrument.

If clutching or squeezing would work on our wheezing
I’d marvel at the dreamy concealment of Kenneth’s 
wet palm, turning pages of psalms like a wife
wringing starch from the cuffs, and distraught
at the squish of what she has wrung to the gutter
like well-lit misfortune, forsaken when wrought,
meets like a tambourine, shaking while wept
in tear-light the quiver, no salt but for staining,
so on goes the steam, hissing its lies at the air.

O bright inquisition of the darkening flame,
sputter-music of the last-gasping waxworks
how timid, how beautifully shy, our prayers
flung up on powdered wings, chasing dawn.
Murk and milkweed coming on, wild garlic
hung deep in the lungs of the forest,
mushrooms pushing up against the peat.

# # #

Last Time in the Garden

A couple rakes the unbearable weight
of fallen leaves, the last remains
they gather the languid bodies
softened and black with rain.

Like bills, they pile up patiently,
like words unsayable and unsaid,
leaves lie back and dream of chlorophyll,
unable to accept the waterlogged sun.

The couple scrapes roots with metal teeth,
scoops with sleeved arms and gloved hands.
They will shower in separate bathrooms,
regard the lawn, as if tilled by artillery,

while peat warms in the brown wheelie bins
chewing to mulch and gassy regrets.

wild garlic

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

engagement, ankle, wife, storm, hardship, item, jealousy, belongings, cellar, photograph, increment, surtax, prosperity, lice, slush

Also:

Micropayment

Prosperity is a hardship to art,
the jealous lice slushing blood
from the ankle flesh of a wife--

surtax unfelt, increment drain,
the engagement so short an item
the storm so easily blown past,

filling its belly like a cellar
with her most intimate belonging,
fast as a photograph’s flash.

10 December 2018

Micropayment

Prosperity is a burden to drain,
so the crafty flea sips longing
from the ankle flesh of a mum--

surtax unfelt, increment tap,
the engagement short but slaking
barrier so deftly blown past,

filling its belly bite-by-byte
with her most intimate belonging,
then gone in a double-tap flash.

Fortnite

For fourteen days, we wandered
chanting the new F-word
under our visible breaths.

From nowhere, shots were fired
so we dove for cartoon cover
building escape ramps in the air.

“Just five more minutes”, we shouted
to our compadres bleeding out
but homework waits for no one.

11 December 2018

12 December 2018

Theme One: Reputation Management in the Digital Age

Hamlet Deletes His Twitter Account

I am ill, Horatio, and punch
the air not in hard-rock enthusiasm
but to sear ghosts with my fists--
tourists in the corporeal realm,
secretly but deliberately replacing
forks with knives, words with deeds.
Who else can save us from their downy
brush-offs and light winds upending
the blood-specked corners of tabloids?
In the eyes of the victors, panic.
In the "ayes" of the mob, a chorus
to sing sweet Ophelia to sleep--
for better to dredge lake bottom
than surface with mud for a face.


Personal Brand

After all this, you have come
to the place where the road gathers mud
like honey smeared across your lips,
the squeeze of belt at your waistline.

"How long does forgetting take this time?"
You want to ask the crows, etched in coal
picking old lovers apart by the roadside,
rib cages proud as tusks in the sun.

Ants rise between your toes, a breeze
lifts mats of your hair, a jewelled beetle
flexes his samurai horns, and you stand
in the heat, and refuse to take cover.

"Don't you know who I am?" you ask.
"Don't you know who I used to be?"


Theme Two: Inevitability of Climate Change

The Art of Denial

I never noticed how black the trees
until they were edged with snow,
nor how wide the high street lay
until its horizon was rubbed.

The plum blossom doesn't mind the rain,
nor does the dog mind the plum blossom;
the man walking the dog doesn't notice me,
nor do I notice the snail I just trod.

We mammals too are made of water, evolved 
from tubular stomachs filtering the sea.
So began our love of bias, ejecting
the indigestible, rippling doubt away.

Even now, we wave our goodbyes: tidal kelp,
breezy wheat, streamers on victory day.


Damage Control

I don't know what to tell you.
The dark will continue to rise
in the street corners, like ink.

The world has tired of compassion,
and like a barrel drained of wine,
our tin cups scrape only wood.

Who has not had to choose between sons?
Who has a lifetime supply of quick fixes?
These are ones rich in possibility.

Engineless, we haggle the joystick,
waterless, we back-burn the forest,
from chaos, we make chaos (barely) less.

Let us bow our heads like penguins
resigned to a featherless fall.

The lie-in in winter

My comrades, the hour grows late.
We know these are the good old days.
We know these days won't come again.

The cruelty of nectar, it's dropwise
torment, how even the elephants
lift up their prayers to the rain.

I have studied the long vowels of night,
the glottal stops of the guttering rain.

A lord under sail is a serf to the wind,
its cruel pronouncements exhaled in a cough.

Here is the prime number we cannot compute,
and here the engineered contentment of meds.

The refrigerator hums, forgetting the words.
The air grows thin with barometric self-hate.

In knitting, the rhythm of needles and hands;
in sewing, a puncture that binds as it wounds.

2 January 2019

The oyster's mistake: a shell 
like a ring box, implying--
since whatever is fortified
tempts us to pry it apart.

The elephants lift their tusks
to the rain; the beetle clacks;
ants stream in disorderly rows
and the air grows sourly-sweet.

Remains

Ghost-self of a cicada, stuck 
to rough bark like an admonition--
intact but for the bursting hatch,
the transparent eyes agog--

remains left on the gibbet post,
gallows, or crucifix; abandoned
jalopy; flesh-form transcended;
glum bug given hum and wings--

who could deny your rite of passage--
self-bound in insect plexiglass,
organs boiling metamorphosis,
secreting a death-row meal--

no less than you deserve to gain
a voice that fills the summer.

5 January 2019

The oily promise of a deep-bore drill
blackening the white balance sheet.

The fractured half-truths of natural gas
escaping like ghosts from their tomb.

The glare of panels. The growl of paddles.
The endless horizon of turning fans.

At what wattage does power corrupt absolutely?
No electron has ever disobeyed its command.

With a key on a kite in a storm in the night
the amoral lightning reached out its hand.

Prometheus Tased. The headlines in shock.
To stay current stings in static-cling times.

A spark is a spike; the powerlines wound
the transformer is humming a popular tune.

16 Jan 19

Reading Judith Beveridge's Sun Music

Halfway
    for Luca, age 3

He sings when he gets tired,
bobbing in the backseat of a car,
arcing at the end of a swing.

Halfway between our world and his,
lifted, like words from a page,
the foil-gold self begins to tremble.

Then just like that, he is music,
in a bright world behind dark lids,
and we are left to cradle his warmth,

to feel his breath on our arm's hair,
and watch the puppet-body pitch and loll
as a boat whose captain is overboard.

A cherub for the cherubs to admire,
gilt wings folded over a shining lute,
all "go" or "stop", and how he's stopped

and with him, the singing stopped too.

Remember: life was hard
as two stones pressed for water,
the low-bent bough
of a boy at quitting time,
pissing on his hands
to rinse the coal.

18 Jan 19

Remember: there was no light
but for squinting
so we cupped the darkness
in our crackling hands,
then closed them in prayer
to the god of emptiness.

Remember: food was scarce
as grains between floorboards,
bones boiled to sticks.
So for minerals, we caught
our tears in a thimble 
since death eyed the wasteful.

Remember sand in our pockets
cobwebs in our mouths,
kneeling on broken stones.
All this we did in silence
blinking into the wind,
pointing our toes at the future.

Aterial mud, as it dregs the shoelace
of time, backward, ever, into the hedgerow
as into a hedgehog--the tallow smoke
tapering ever upward into our face.

Grime. That's all I could ever call it.
The dark pupils swivelled open and shut,
the cat went on plying its dark trade
and shapeshifters mistook us for shadows.

The heart of coral is black, not white.
All things rot; all things rise from rot.
What unforgiving circumstance is next?
Surely the stillness of water protests.

Only in dreaming are we free from the heft
of bodies desiring return to dark earth
while a pocket-full of smooth-rubbed stones
clink beneath the music of our stride.

24 Jan 2019

Anniversary
    for JVP

I look to young relations--on my side,
on his mothers side, for clues--
but he is more than a composite
of speculations, more than one knee
scraped in the grass kissed better
by a sallow breeze, more tender
than first rain following the drought
that withers my pointing finger
as I say, "Him" with a capital "H"
and no longer know if I mean
my own son or everyone lost at sea.
It spreads through the treetops,
branch to lightly-touching branch,
as the spark that first turned over life
in the dark mulch of this world,
grief spreads--if that is the word
we have left after so many goodbyes
to call it by name like a good
dog eager to walk by our side.
Who doesn't love the animal self
personified in paw-mud and slobber?
My young relations run toward them
arms flung as an airplane landing,
and I look for him in such unguarded
gestures, so unlike the sonograms--
curled inward, resisting this world.

Just for the Sound of It

I have studied the long vowels of night,
the glottal stops of the guttering rain.

Surely the stillness of water protests,
while a pocket-full of smooth-rubbed stones
clinks beneath the music of our stride.

In knitting, the rhythm of needles and hands;
in sewing, a puncture that binds as it wounds.

Only in dreaming are we free from the heft
of bodies desiring a return to dark earth
while our prayers fly up on powdered wings.

I have chanted with sages in squalid caves
I have kicked the can to hear it confess.

The heart of coral is black, not white.
All things rot; all things rise from rot.
What unforgiving circumstance is next?

The refrigerator hums, forgetting the words.
The air grows thin with barometric self-hate.

Just for the Sound of It

I have studied the long vowels of night,
the glottal stops of the guttering rain.

Surely the stillness of water protests,
while a pocket-full of smooth-rubbed stones
clinks beneath the music of our stride.

the clack of a beetle's green wings.

Just for the Sound of It

I have studied the long vowels of night,
the glottal stops of the guttering rain.

Surely the stillness of water protests,
while a pocket-full of smooth-rubbed stones
clinks beneath the music of our stride.

In knitting, the rhythm of needles and hands;
in sewing, a puncture that binds as it wounds.

Only in dreaming are we free from the heft
of the drum-body beating its march toward earth
while our prayers fly up on powdered wings.

I have chanted with sages in squalid caves
I have kicked the can to hear it confess.

In a snow-boot's instep, a tumble of grain,
in the sputter-song of a candle's last gasp,
how still the air becomes expecting snow--

I have loved the notes, and adored the rests
in the clack of a beetle's green wings.

The Deer

The body darkened the hedgerow. I sensed
the animal, its heft, before I knew it was
a deer -- fur in points from the rain,
hooves drawn together. I did not stop.

I suspect it was the same young one 
who crossed  my path before, watching 
from the trailside, still as death. 
Face outlined in leaf. Close enough.

I have seen the foxes take the pheasants.
I have heard the blasts, trod shotgun shells.
Whatever killed the deer did not pursue.
Caged in branches, she lay on her side.

# # #

15 February 2019

Warmth rose from the body, pushing
mist aside in a final protective act,
the creature globed in clear light,
dispersing its unseen end.

That night, she came to me,
on quiet hooves, the endless black eyes, 
a telescope pointed at the starless sky, 
breath warming my hand, soft nostrils.

I can not tell you why I woke up crying.
Perhaps because I was alive, warming
the bedsheets as dawn ran her fingers
over the ribcage, already sunken.

Perhaps I envied the deer, transformed
into the air I can only keep breathing
long after my thoughts turn from her
to other small griefs in my pocket.

So night turned over into day,
and I went about this human business,
though I walked more carefully now,
testing the ground beneath me.

22 February 2019

Vox Pop

The people spoke, and the word was, "Hate."
For once they spoke their minds, and out came tar.
They said what they meant, and it burned on contact.
It was not yet a crime since even war has its rules.

The people laced up their boots and went to work.
They pulled down temple brick and lit wooden chapels.
They gave themselves permission to dream even bigger.
All this from a word on a ballot written in pencil.

Undaunted, the people crushed stones in their hands.
They marvelled how red the blood, and cited tradition.
They hoisted up gallows on the bones of their ancestors,
whose good intentions they muttered through gritted teeth.

The people knew their mind, and wadded the ballots.
They loaded pencils into their guns, and that was that.
Those who asked for a recount were not taken lightly.
Such people get caught under foot; progress is like that.

The people clasped their huge hands and shook.
They had written the new traditions together in ink.
Other words  became inferior, immoral, illegal.
In their hearts the people knew their kind could be next.

The Deer

The body darkened the hedgerow. I sensed
the animal, its heft, before I knew it was
a deer -- fur in points from the rain,
hooves drawn together. I did not stop.

I suspect it was the same young one 
who crossed  my path before, watching 
from the trailside, still as death. 
Face outlined in leaf. Close enough.

I have seen the foxes take the pheasants.
I have heard the blasts, trod shotgun shells.
Whatever killed the deer did not pursue.
Caged in branches, she lay on her side.

Warmth rose from the stiffening carcass, 
dispersing its unseen end. That night, 
she came to me, her black eyes endless,
soft nostrils, warm breath on my hand.

I can not tell you why I woke up crying.
Perhaps because I was alive, incubated
in bedsheets as dawn ran her fingers
over the ribcage, already sunken.

Perhaps I envied the deer, transformed
into the air I can only keep breathing
long after my thoughts turn from her
to other small griefs in my pocket.

So night turned over into day,
and I went about my human business,
though I walked a little more quietly,
testing the ground under foot.

Review poems for Tom Gatti

1 March

3 March

March Still Feels like February

The rain comes down like maternity ward lights
and the puddles fill up like blisters.

The rain comes down like news you never dreamed,
and the gutters spit back I-told-you-sos.

Even here the tinkling bell of high hopes
even here the thundercloud warming up.

The leafless branches feather out into sky
for Winter walks next to me always.

Once I held a fatherless child called "myself"
as did my father, and his father.

The rain taps at the glass like a stopwatch
we are all of us orphans in the making.

11 March 2019



The bruise takes on its ink, the blister domes.
All that we've become will come again, branches
against the white sky like capillaries crawling
through fat, toward bone, knitting its lace.

The body tends its losses. Each ventricle
a shushing mouth, the lungs massaging
salve into that salaryman the liver, home
late again, collapsed into bed with his tie.

How many of us are left? Ask the red cells,
racing the white through dark passages,
children on bikes in a disused train tunnel,
their yells and splashes echoing.

We have come a long way; we have a long way
to go. No rest for the dudoenum, clitoris,
thorax--each swollen word a world itself,
each piece in commerce, murmuring.

the smell of money

White Girls with Ukuleles on Instagram

The pale blue of the dime-store ukulele matches your eyes
and the glitter around your eyes, and the canvass shoes 
you chose on a whim from a charity shop, not an outlet,
and threw on after a breakfast of cornflakes and dreams.

This poem did not set out to be bitchy, but here we are--
a black disc off camera shielding your pricey microphone
like a satellite, tuned right in to your pixie-dust vibe,
fed to a hand-me-down smartphone in a kitty-cat case.

Like a good solider, you were only doing what you were told
would gather up followers to you, and your message--
which is: Be cute, and strum un-threatening-ly, wafting
through our feed on a harmless scatter-brained cloud.


21 March 2019

Dysthymia

I don't really want to do anything --
not peep through the small doors
at mice living out unimaginative lives
or run a finger over the beaded skin
of a rattling snake, coaxing venom.

In my mind I could be anywhere, and yet--
out of the piles of driftwood, small crabs
are fighting for their well-armoured lives,
and so I stoop with my flaming stick
and prod the wet sand, and it sizzles.

I can't even be bothered to autocorrect.
The wet lips of the python beckon from a tree,
and isn't it always that tree to blame,
as though Knowledge itself corrupted us,
rather than we ourselves with its mis-use.

The dice fall beneath the crucifix.
A bet is a bet, and the spear goes in.
The rules made up in childhood we follow
like a sleepwalker throughout our life,
for it is dangerous, they say, to wake us.

7 May 2019

Lift up your trunks to the rain.
There are few of us left to squabble
toward or against, laying our stones
in untidy rows to be washed over.

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

gust, offers, blooms, performance, panda, siding, motionless, hip, factor, emperor, shimmer, preferred, misses, swirls, virginity

Also:

Include the name of a board game

Motionless emperor, back curled
like a panda against spectators,
you offer up virginity like soap
bubbles bursting in hard rain,
and we take your performance
for a missed chance at hilarity,
grumbling at your inward thoughts.

Lift up your trunks to the rain, comrades.
It is given as freely as seed over land.

In the reddening treetops, a signpost
of warning, our love receding from sun.

How dark the wandering clot, seeking
strangulation's bottleneck.

Like a dark eye the cosmos looks onward,
into the open hands of a virgin bride.

In a gust, the blooms are stripped naked,
awaiting cold water and interrogation lights.

What is film for, but clinging?
Wet clothes define and hide.

Bored of games, we keep on competing
against our own time on the clock,
running down the excuses tick-wise
circling in time-lapse, then gone.

Who will raise their hand above the parapet?
Who values their fingers so little as this?

to provide you with relevant content.

may or may not constitute an IQ test.
At no time is a contract being implied
between the mice in your floorboard
and our cat, now sharpening its claws.
From time to time, we may use arsenic
to ensure compatibility with the afterlife.
By reading these terms you agree to be bound
by these terms, for the rest of your life, 
or until your next click--whichever comes first.

What Was Handed Down

Lift up your limbs to the rain, comrades.
It is given as freely as seed over land.

In the reddening treetops, a signpost
of warning, our love receding from sun.

Like a dark eye the cosmos looks onward,

Bistro della Stazione

Here even the dogs don't like the smell
of last night's strangers as they piss
into the grates past darkness into darkness.
The old men propping up the wall in wrinkled suits
adjust their sunglasses against the glare.
Imagine--a poet actually stares out of a window.
Inside they sip liqueur the red of martyrs' blood, 
on green plates of glass, on zinc and bistro brass, 
in worn padded booths with sticky varnished oak.
Men of an age who still buy newspapers read them
here as they always have, their usual breakfast
laid patiently before them like a waiting dog.
Their good-smelling tobacco enjoys itself.
Our matron looks away. They fold themselves 
into the nation's affairs, unmoved by the news.

 They bottle it in factories.

With a nick in the ear, with a folded-down corner.

Even the stars disgust me now.

24 June 2019

"I See the Moon, and the Moon Sees Me"

There is nothing left to write about
since I fell away from the world
I don't mean existentially--
like a balloon, the globe escaped
and drifted into the star-pricked dark
oer perhaps it was I, filled with
the good air of hopefulness,
tied tightly by a mother's hand.
Among the satellites, I was alone
cozying up to space debris, cartons
of fast-food left by astronauts,
the Earth glowing gently beside me,
wrapped like an invalid in cotton wool.
I was told it would be dangerous
but the air had already been used up
in the ongoing political debate
and the chill I found refreshing
after the seas turned to bath water.
I wouldn't say the vacuum abhorred me,
though I felt its persistent tug.
Something is to be said for drifting
after a lifetime believing in gravity.
Sometimes I miss the small-scale things
here with the Milky Way behind me,
but even if I wanted to now,
I could not pull it all back.

Nothing Personal

There is no more left to write about
since I fell away from the world
I don't mean existentially--
like a balloon, the globe escaped
and drifted into the star-pricked dark
or perhaps it was I, filled with
the good air of hopefulness,

The Art of Denial

I never noticed how black the trees
until they were edged with snow,
nor how wide the high street lay
until its horizon was rubbed.

The plum blossom doesn't mind the rain,
nor does the dog mind the plum blossom;
the man walking the dog doesn't notice me,
nor do I notice the snail I just trod.

We mammals too are made of water, evolved 
from tubular stomachs filtering the sea.
So began our love of bias, ejecting
the indigestible, swooshing doubt away.

Even now, we ripple goodbye like tidal kelp,
breezy wheat, streamers at a parade.

Self-Esteem for Giants

Sometimes I think I am just the way
dishes get transported up and down stairs--
like, having no legs, they invented me
to go on these excursions. It makes me
feel useful. Sometimes I think about how
I make cutlery both dirty and clean,
shuffling it from dishwasher to drawer
to dining table, and then back again.
Sometimes, I lick a spoon for effect.
Perhaps each day for them is a season,
the soapy rains, wintering in drawers.
Sometimes, when I break a dish, I want
to cry, but it's more from shock than loss,
more about me and my failure as a cup holder
than about the cup and its untimely demise.
Each day for them is an adventure, soaring
in the questionably steady hands of a giant,
clattering into the sink with undue panache.
I'd like to feel like a giant. I was told
(probably by myself) that one day I would.
Yet here I am, savouring the chime of forks
put back in their places. I wonder what they
say about me, passing reviews of my work
through the drawers and shelves they sleep in.
Goodnight, dishes and cutlery. Dream well.

31 July 2019

Intolerable Weather

The flared sun beats its skin-tight drum,
and I think how arrogant it is to be alive.
The blueberries are cultivating their beards
at the ice-domed back of the refrigerator.
Whose silence matters more--yours or mine?

We are still, and still breathing, but barely,
as the wheat gathers her skirts by the stream.
No prosody without blood or sweat, they say.
None of us can survive this heat for long--
yet the kitchen exists, and so we cook.

The tonic is laced with just enough gin--
even as nonsense contains its backwash of truth,
and so we are given what we always wanted,
tearing through Christmas paper to get the gun
we aim at classmates, flag unfurling its "Bang!"

Pull my finger whilst I talk into this banana.
Smell my flower while I sever a plastic thumb.
No circus can compete with the headless headlines.
No freak-show so freaky as the one behind shut lids.
One day they will bottle silence, and we will pay.

For now cool breezes exist for the price of taxes,
for now the stream gathers up acceptable losses.
I haven't brought answers, just this deck of cards,
so we pass through the space between tick-marks,
mumbling through our hands a prayer for rain.

1 September 2019

The wrought iron bridge across the Seine
still brandishing its gold letter "N" 
for nuisance, nonsense, nationality,
the last narcissus abloom.

Satire comes too easily now, boxed up
and delivered by men with funny accents.

We have always been right here, on edge.

Look at how the light reflects its maker.
A poet actually stares out of a window.
From drivel, the drooling stooge utters
a truism so true we stumble on regardless.
And nobody wonders what the wind says anymore.

The Mona Lisa of Asylum Art
    -Dr. David O'Flynn, regarding Louis Wain's cats

O cat electric costumed in spurs,
you summon us with scooped eys
to the occasion of your electrocution
roadside attraction, noir centrepiece
you were destined for gummy artifice,
and look at you now--the spit of a pen
drawn into the spikes of hell's pews.
We wait for you. We ponder the mind
that made you, as Blake's tiger,
as the lambs we pretend to be beside
your fervent scribbles, cheshire
and saltire, pin-prick and puzzlement.
Who is left to comfort the agitated?
Only a pen, clutched tight and bleeding
its ink, printing whorls and paws,
wiped into the corner of your mouth
whirling upward against your frown.

psychotic deterioration

https://www.indiatoday.in/education-today/gk-current-affairs/story/unknown-endangered-species-in-the-world-311156-2016-03-03

Solenodon is a cute-looking mammal generally found in regions of Cuba. It is an animal which is highly irritable and injects venom through its teeth when annoyed. Solenodons were considered extinct before scientists found some still alive in the year 2003.

Solenodons (meaning "slotted-tooth") are venomous, nocturnal, burrowing, insectivorous mammals belonging to the family Solenodontidae. The two living solenodon species are the Cuban solenodon (Solenodon cubanus), and the Hispaniolan solenodon (Solenodon paradoxus)

The young remain with the mother for several months and initially follow the mother by hanging on to her elongated teats. Once they reach adulthood solenodons are solitary animals and rarely interact except to breed.

They are easily provoked and can fly into a frenzy of squealing and biting with no warning. This form of echolocation is the main way in which a solenodon is able to navigate as well as find its food sources, because solenodons have extremely small eyes and poor vision. Nocturnal.

A strong musty goat-like smell seeping out of a burrow. After the arrival of Europeans to the island, the solenodon's existence has been threatened by dogs, cats, mongooses, and more dense human settlement.

17 September 2019

The Mona Lisa of Asylum Art
    -Dr. David O'Flynn, regarding Louis Wain's cats

O cat electric costumed in spurs,
you summon us with scooped eys
to the occasion of your electrocution
and look at you now--the spit of a pen
drawn into the spikes of hell's pews.
We make up the mind that made you, 
as Blake's tiger, in our own image
lambing our designs into each fervent 
scribble, cheshire and saltire, 
pin-prick and puzzlement. Who is left 
to comfort the agitated? Only spilled ink
to test the mind's undercarriage--
fur raised in raw shock at what's there.
Only the grin electric, its sawtooth hum.
And then a pen, clutched into bleeding, 
printing its whorls and paws, a stray mark
swiped into the corner of your mouth
as an indelible, upturned frown.

The Peacock Dances in my Heart
    with apologies to Tagore

It is blue as a neon bell,
the braid and wrap of tubes
lay claim to tail-feathers
filled with the impulse
of sleepless glowing gas.
No Thanksgiving turkey,
its garments hum with tune,
its shadows like a stalker
bear their long teeth bitterly,
while the railroad conducts
its indigo light, cupped
in poolhall smoke, and cursed
to strut its triumph
at the wood-panelled back
of the Santa-somewhere bar.


3 October 2019

Small griefs jingled like pocket change.

The Deer

The body darkened the hedgerow. I sensed
the animal, its heft, before I knew it was
a deer -- fur in points from the rain,
hooves drawn together. I did not stop.

I suspect it was the same young one 
who crossed  my path before, watching 
from the trailside, still as death. 
Face outlined in leaf. Close enough.

I have seen the foxes take the pheasants.
I have heard the blasts, trod shotgun shells.
Whatever killed the deer did not pursue.
Caged in branches, she lay on her side.

Warmth rose from the stiffening carcass, 
dispersing its unseen end. That night, 
she came to me, her black eyes endless,
soft nostrils, warm breath on my hand.

I can not tell you why I woke up crying.
Perhaps because I was alive, incubated
in bedsheets as dawn ran her fingers
over the ribcage, already sunken.

Perhaps I envied the deer, transformed
into the air I can only keep breathing
long after my thoughts turn from her
to other small griefs in my pocket.

So night turned over into day,
and I went about my human business,
though I walked a little more quietly,
testing the ground under foot.


# # #

Morning Run

Into the furnace of my body I place
the policies of the current administration
and the young man who, defending them, spat
in my digital face, and promised to find me.

The waves of heat reach from tall grass
and panting, I have no breath to curse
the the small white flies who gag me
with their suicide dives.

It is not a race against anything.
The ground gives back with each step
explosions of chaff and dried-out dung
and my breath becomes ragged.

Now the lashes blur with vinegared sweat,
and the arms strain at their sockets.
Cows follow me with google eyes
readying their tired alarm.

I run until my the surviving thoughts
are breath, and step, each the last,
until the fire in my legs burns cold, until
I am the torch itself, carrying nothing.

26 October 2019

Question in Greek

How many blues does a dead man need
to sweep the pillars of arched horizon
and scatter the good light over the crawling waves
or deepen the underwater caverns, boiling with kelp,
where small fish peck the flaked skin of divers
and wavering nets of light trace the flat stones,
since here even the strays are fed on salt-wind
and the green lemons purse their lips in promise,
while an idling van puffs, and a regular eyes
the tourists: doughy, dun, or ablaze in the bronzed
pageant of daytime lolling, as it yields to silver
and the moon, whose blue is scorching, presides--
how much of this can the screen of the eyelids retain
when coins are laid over our sockets, covering them?

# # #

Downturn
    Isle of Crete

Their concrete arches frame the sea,
while a stray cat drowses in the shade
and wild grasses climb the staircase,
one step for each abandoned year.

Not all ruins are ancient here--
these stand as monuments to a future turned
from the ridge road, down to scrub brush,
where dust covers all things in time.

In a doorway where no person will stand
Kodachrome blues taunt the pale sky
while in the distance, craters above the bay
echo the brakers, crowded with sage,

and the flat roofs, glazed with sunlight,
compare the height of their shadows.

# # #

End of Season

Late October, two weeks before Brexit,
the silver eyes of fish heads
are left out for stray cats to hiss over
while a rooster, weary of tourists
bellows for all of us: enough.

We have learned four words for love,
filled our pockets with smooth stones.
The cats prefer the shade of a Mercedes
outside the vacant car hire stand
while fisherman sleep on moored boats.
The smell of olives follows me.

I had not begun to write in the sand
my name, a heart, my lover's initials,
when the chairs and reed umbrellas
were hauled up by tired, glowing men.
We are welcome, and welcome to go home now.

No distant lantern calls for us--only the spread
of light on gently-folding water, the black sheen
of the cashier woman's curls under fluorescent light.
Black is the natural conclusion of water so emerald blue.
We sit behind clear plastic, awaiting the salted fog.
We drink to the black moon, forgiving her tardiness.

Somnolent, we pace the boardwalk on both sides of the curtain now
as the night gradually collapses, like a curling wave, behind us.


1 November 19

Rescue Dog: Building Site

She won't go up the hill. Builders are shouting and for all my honeyed coaxing she flops and rolls, muddying her purple rain jacket, tangling the padded harness. She wants to go back to them. She wants to know if they smell like someone she must have known back home. They were either very kind or very cruel, a waypoint on her daily route through Skopje, a place I've never been. The rain dampens her head fur down. Her nose stops rooting the air. She looks up at me, pupils rolling in globes of white. They are not the ones. "OK," I say, and tousel her ears. Today is not a day to run. We trudge home through the brown puddles, landing our paws inside the door on a plush, unfamiliar towel.

Wide eyes of a stray
ready for food or violence--
watching both my hands

14 November 19

Rescue Dog: Guarding

She could bark for England. In the lonesome clatter of a moving van, the uniformed cries of schoolkids, the latch on the gate rattling like a choke-chain--she overhears disaster. Is she seeing them off, or letting us know? She is poised between our guest and us like a famous person's bodyguard. Visibly proud. Alert. And terrified. How will we teach her that the world and all that's good doesn't have to end with us, the first ones to belong to her?

Wind rattles the stove
like a burglar here on Mars
so the night howls on

15 November 19

Rescue Dog: Alpha

A dog is not a child. Every pack needs its leader. You must be that leader. She must never/always: be on the sofa, be left alone, chew to strengthen her gums, chew to express anxiety, chew until there is nothing left of your heart. She snaps at the air behind our hairdresser's fur coat. Hunting? Guarding? Seeing her off? I am no drill sergeant like my father but still when I stand she cowers down and further down, steading herself for a blow that will never come, could never come. How can I tell her this? I walk the tightrope, letting out the line before me, and inching like a worm we crawl on our bellies forward together into another blinking day.

Trying to find our place
we circle and scratch the ground
testing its firmness

20 November 2019

Rescue Dog: DNA

The double helix doesn't lie. She throws her head back, howls into a Siberian night. Thirty seven percent Samoyed. Having never seen a ball, she picks one up, forgets to bring it back. Twelve percent golden retriever. The rest is a soup of sight-hound, vermin-hunter, sheep-herder, mingling for generations on the streets of Macedonia's capital. She burrows into leaf-litter and, spotting a pheasant in a nearby field, lifts one paw, and points. Back home by the fire, her smile is drool-resistant. No icicles on you.

From the wolf she came
through thousands of past masters
to teach me new tricks

25 November 20198

Rescue Dog: With Other Dogs

She circles, sniffs, backs away slowly or sharply with a flash of tooth. Even as she pretends to ignore them she is saying something, and eloquently well. Our ballerina of the social stance, she finds her place in every pack, with the grace of a survivor. Yappers stop yapping, and jumpers set four paws on the ground. "So good with other dogs," they say, bewildered at her barking and shying from human hands. Our dog of dogs.

The logic of dog:
see, smell, respond. Life can be this
uncomplicated.

Rescue Dog: Canicross

Who is guiding whom? The scorched mud churns,
blackened by its own designs, and we foghorns
flare through the rough chewed tar of desire--
two coyotes on the trail, hopping from tiles
of flat stone and stumbling in the pre-dawn-muck,
the mournful squelch of our soul's escape
into the icy, blunt, axe-head of the morning.

26 December 2019

Rescue Dog: The Bone

Suddenly the lead goes razor-tight. She has found a nest of cellophane, and tangled in it, the smashed ball-hammer of a leg joint. There is no taking it from her now, though she will walk home with me, head aloft, mouthing the femur-end in the corner of her smile. I think of the crawling ants, themselves aflutter with microbes. Outside, in a flash I swap her bought-and-boiled antler for this newer grubby find. Sealed in the compost bin, she noses for it. Bits of sinew. Bits of blood. A real thing that was once her own.

Drinking on the run,
she dips in her snout and skims
the milk-brown puddles


27 December 2019

Rescue Dog: In the Car

She catches a dew-claw on the door frame of our hatchback with a clang. And she's off. Try again another day. So we do, and do, coaxing her into yet another den as vans rattle the high street. This is our bid for freedom, her momentary confinement, our chance to leave the village all together. The coffee shop with the dog-friendly awning, the longer walks into chalk hills farmed with (dogs on leads please) sheep--bookmarked in mind well before her arrival. Edge into reverse, then forward again. Cut the ignition. That's enough for today.

Finally she sits
muzzle above the hand brake
my backseat driver

Rescue Dog: Counter-conditioning

She's on the couch watching Empire Strikes Back with the dog walker's daughter. She in the field with a prehensile nose. She barks now in anticipation--when we put on our shoes, or rustle the food bag. She picks up a box of matches from the kindling basket, and parades through the living room. One day, she will come when we call her, ignoring the squirrel. One day, she will race toward us, not slowing down.

An old tennis ball
In a slobber-proof pocket
Ready for takeoff

Rescue Dog: The Long Lead

She's off like cast bait. She's off like a boat anchor unreeling. She's off and the sharpened strop of the extra-long lead races through the crease in my palm. Rescue Dog: The Call

She's muzzle-down on the sofa when I tap the video button next to dad's name. "I didn't understand your text," I say (the one that came in at midnight), "the part about the treatment timeframe, the eight-to-twelve-weeks." He narrows his eyes at the enemy, and I'm no longer sure if it is cancer or me. "That's how long I have to live," he barks. So begins our conversation. From time to time, she rolls her eyes up lazily, twitches the whiskers that sprout from her brow. Through the tiny speaker, my father must sound like me to her. Though I know it is the other way around.

After the heartbreak
the dog sleeps here beside me,
snores like an old man

Rescue dog: the injury

When limping turns to shaking, how are we going to get her into the car? Fortunately, she'll do anything for cheese. "I'm the same way," the tattooed vet quips. The meds. kick in that afternoon. Next morning, she doesn't understand me slipping out the door in running clothes without her. Three weeks on, we're back on the trial, but she still won't propel her back legs onto the sofa to have her breakfast beside me. She lays her jowls on the cushion, eyes the morsel just out of reach. "OK," I say, "not yet," and nudge it back into range.

Her world-weary sighs
head-down in a patch of sun
mean something? nothing?

Rescue dog: the virus

It is on the wind. It clinks the gate latch like pennies down a well. It slides through the mail slot in the middle of the day. It rules over curtain shadows. She knows the threat is more than microscopic. It is not far off now. Yet somehow too far for our small ears to hear. The force of her bark propels her on stiff legs, like a tin dog on a spring. But her coils run long and tight, and with each footfall she is winding.

Crinkly nose probing--
a green radar screen, each blip
the smell of our fear


Rescue Dog: Social distancing

The outside worlds doesn't smell the same. There is less of people and more of plants. Planes have quit rumbling overhead. When we do meet someone, they are leashed to their pet. We cut wide arcs around them. She assumes, as any outsider would, that we dislike them. That we distrust them. I smile to counteract it. She bares her teeth, and growls.



The world smells the same
Dad loves her (V)

Rescue dog: first English spring

Spring doesn't know any better, and neither does she. She leaves the sitting room behind, and here it is bluebells for carpet, and blackthorn for drapes. A room of her own, wide and cloud-free, scattered with apple bloom. It can only be called ecstasy, drawn down her snout like the curling smoke of a hookah. Her opium den. Her Valhala. The bursting world from which she sprung, and springs, and will spring forth again and again.

Coat covered in burrs
I pluck them into the bin
clinging for dear life

Rescue Dog: "Find It!"

My back pocket smells of kibble, because life is like this--you never know when you'll end up doing something right, and the game is always afoot. 12 June 2020

Buffalo ear

There is no more to the distance in life than the distance in death.

Listen: I have seen the trees bowing in obsequy 
I have felt the soft ripple of undercurrent: tug, tug
what is blazoned is becoming, through blossom, my name
speak to me, for I have not heard a human voice
listen to me in the darkness, and let the darkness speak back,
its reflective sufface glancing even the surest blow
How is it not now that we resurface? Foaming at the mouth,
feverish from the toenail to brow
So this is living. This is the life.
Nothing has changed outside the cell.
The membrane between us was thinner than we thought.
After making love, the dust motes twinkle
and the story changes with the turn of a page.
Who is left? Not many. What began as an army
hobbles homeward on stick-thin legs.
Be brave, Napoleon, and let us tar-paper your effigy
into the minds of short-lived bureaucrats everywhere.
How has it been so long? I have not let myself range
over the golden fields of Mind and mindlessness
for days, nay, daydreams and furloughs long with hay.
I am becoming more myself with each waking breath.
And at night, as I snuffle through twitchy dreams
and turn my hips toward and away from moonlight's glare
how wicked it has become to take even a single sip
of the same tapwater that has killed so many
and how few we are who remember the corn-silk waving
or the scarecrow in the field with his fingers bent
to say "this is torture" is to elevate the art form
into something we can live with, like a good chair
and fold our arms into, and slumber awhile in summer heat
or curl deeper while the rain pelts unfortunate leaves
and the clematis takes a beating, and comes up blue.
No wind for the winded, and the widest channel we seek
is the one cut through our memory, and the languish of time
as we chisel into the scar-faced rock our own beliefs
and call them aeternal, and believe they were nature-made.

Rescue Dog: Nicknames

"Walk?" I say, and her eyes flash with momentary disbelief at her good luck. She trots up the stairs beside me to fetch my socks, nuzzling my leg and bopping my knee pointedly with her nose. Our cartoon Betty Boop. Then lacing up my boots the excitement races into her jaws, and she snaps in the air, flashing her gorgeous teeth. Our rockstar Eric Clack-ton, shredding licks. Now the day is done and her jowls couldn't be heavier, so it's bedtime before the sun is down for our worn-out granny in her white lace wrap. Our Betty. Our Mildred. Our Agnes.

Ask me what I did
with my life and I will say:
a dog was happy.


Rescue Dog: The Nose

What a poo. What a rush. The dung thick with flies that scatter at her spear-like snout. Taking it in huffs. 

Rescue Dog: Play Time

They couldn't be more different--her trailing down the side passage like a silk scarf in white, and him--a pint-sized bodybuilder in a beige anorak, waddling his whole haggis frame behind her, at half the speed, on legs one-quarter as long. But they are matched. He goes down and over in the grass, and she tries to gain purchase on his neck folds in vain with her beak-like snout. She's jumping over him. He's diving through. A lion and a turtle. A rhino and a gazelle. Two dogs, a million breeders apart. Speaking a language all their own.

After endless "No"s
someone comes along with "Yes"--
finally, a dog.

24 October 2020

Rescue Dog: The Bite

It is hard to get the facts when you are shaking. They both are shaking, and soon I will be too. "That's vicious," she snarls, pointing at the animal in my arms. I play back the sounds: gate latch, bark, bark, bark, gallop of paws, a woman's voice, unhinged: "get out of here!", my own footsteps, too slow, too late. They look to me in silence. Eyes filling with tears and bewilderment. Just before the backlash. The rain lets on.

Fixing the gate hinge
to keep her in, keep them out
strangling the screws tight

Rescue Dog: The Beach

There is no horizon she cannot cross. The flats go on plainly, and the sky lasts for miles. We risk it, and unclip the leash. She goes out. She comes back. We are moving as a pack. She skims the ripples, nuzzles kelp, contrails of sand spew behind her. She flops. She rolls. She smells the sea. She smells of the sea. She is the froth itself.

Who is more happy?
The dog untied from the man
or the  man let go? 

Rescue Dog: The Nose

What a poo. What a rush. The dung thick with flies that scatter at her spear-like snout. Taking it in huffs. She is mad for the stuff. Whiffing up the aerosol, particulates, like snuff. Her fluted nose is filled to overflowing. Bottoms up.

Like a boat anchor
she drops, and pulls me up short--
lamp post of the gods

2 November 2020

The Injury

We are just off the road when it happens, on a tightrope-narrow strip of concrete. She stops, and lifts a leg, crooking the paw like an Egyptian. A wing mirror kisses the back of my anorak. I'm afraid she'll wince, and lurch, and spin on the leash as I've seen her do a thousand times on safer ground. But as I crouch and feel my way down the leg, she is motionless as a dog in bas relief at the foot of a knight's sarcophagus. I find the burr, or it finds me, and in one quick nip it's gone. She sets the paw down, and resumes her stride as though she had only been paused on a screen. A gait that says nothing happened, and nothing bad will ever happen again.

Is this gratitude?
We depend on each other
enough to hold still

3 November 2020

Rescue Dog: The Door

She flops between turning her warm belly out for a rub and snarling at whatever lies beyond the front door. I know the feeling. I know the lies beyond the front door, and I know how soft the couch. Any minute, the steel gaze of a cat. Any minute, an unknowable tapping at the window pane. So we must be minute-men, and minute-canines. Tamping our musket balls into dry powder. She has no idea what I'm talking about. But her lip curls in anticipation. And her eyes are flint on steel.

Stopped against her will
like a taxi at a light
low idling growl

5 November 2020

Rescue Dog: Election Nights

She knows what uneases smells like. Bedsheets soaked. Smoke in our clothes. Even the way we sit is not right. She roots for us with her snout, she badgers us in our burrow. She can not place a paw on it, but places her paw on us. "It's a human thing," I hear myself tell her aloud. Longing for the "dog things." But the moment still exists. And there she is.

Not oblivious
but attuned to the moment--
bark! bark! burnt saucepan

9 November 2020

Monument

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you can:

woolen, flaps, pettt, mooned, prehension, anchored, thyng, nights1944, waterfall, enwraps, elevated, dahlquist, unopposed, recalls, locating

Also:

Mention something absurd or impossible

Monument

The cows are off in space when we arrived,
the woollen flaps of sheep's ears flipped
in the moony breeze, with no apprehension
of how we would raise our muddy boot-anchors
out from the squealing mud, and march our welles
through the nights unopposed, locating our courage
in the last place we found it, which is always
the last place we'd think to look.

After making love, the dust motes twinkle
and the story changes with the turn of a page.
Who is left? Not many. What began as an army
hobbles homeward on stick-thin legs.
Be brave, Napoleon, and let us tar-paper your effigy
into the minds of short-lived bureaucrats everywhere.

I am becoming more like myself with each waking breath.

At night, I snuffle through twitchy dreams and turn 
my hips toward and away from moonlight's glare.

How wicked it has become to take even a single sip
of the same tapwater that has killed so many
and how few we are who remember the corn-silk waving
or the scarecrow in the field with his fingers bent.

To say "this is torture" is to elevate the art form
into something we can live with, like a good chair
and fold our arms into, and slumber awhile in summer heat
or curl deeper while the rain pelts unfortunate leaves
and the clematis takes a beating, and comes up blue.

No wind for the winded, and the widest channel we seek
is the one cut through our memory, and the languish of time
as we chisel into the scar-faced rock our own beliefs
and call them eternal, and believe they were nature-made.

MARVIN EXERCISE

Include as many of the following words (or variations on these words) as you like:

inferred, railway, eddy, trays, cage, relaxation, countenance, swine, jacob, descendant, yesteryear, kettle

Also:

Include a musical instrument.
Refer to a famous poet

12 November 2020

Hobo Election

The fiddle infers what the bow wants to play,
stroking its railway edge over wound lines,

the harmonica stands in for a backing choir
reeds in their trays jostling for sound,

and we in our cages practice relaxation
into whatever forms the bars will permit.

Who is not jealous of Bukowskian wit?
Eddies form in the bottle's recess

and on good days, the swine car rattles
downwind of our carriage, and yesteryear.

Like a kettle yanked from the stove,
the whistle descends, a curtain swiped

over the countenance of a bygone age, 
lids shuttered, lips going blue--

so we wrestle our better judgment, 
like Jacob, knowing that one of us 

will be thrown from the moving train.

 17 November 2020

Rescue Dog: The Memory

The little girl who taught me to play "doctor" learned it from her father. Years later, when it came out in the newspaper--what he had done to her, and the other girls in the neighbourhood--he hanged himself. He was a crop-duster pilot. We would watch as clouds of pesticide streaked from his biplane, and covered the fields like a blanket. We think he was the one who poisoned my dog, Chessy. He barked too much and dug under the fence. I named him that because my father was obsessed with chess, so much so that on the first day I was born he placed a pawn in my hand. I clutched it. On my fifth birthday, I rode home with Chessy in the back seat of our car, my arms wrapped around his neck. As a puppy he was already bigger than me. My father asked: "Are you happy, son?" I squeezed my dog again and whispered, "This is the best day of my life."

learned it from her father. Years later,

to her, and the other girls in the neighbourhood--
he hanged himself. He was a crop-duster pilot. 
We would watch as clouds of pesticide 
streaked from his biplane, and covered the fields 
like a blanket. We think he was the one 
who poisoned my dog, Chessy. He barked too much 
and dug under the fence. I named him that 
because my father was obsessed with chess, 
so much so that on the first day I was born 
he placed a pawn in my hand. I clutched it. 
On my fifth birthday, I rode home with Chessy 
in the back seat of our car, my arms wrapped 
around his neck. As a puppy he was already 
bigger than me. My father asked: "Are you happy, son?" 
I squeezed my again and whispered, 
"This is the best day of my life."

Jesus Playing Baseball


Try to include at least 10 of these words (or their variations) . . . 

panic, stack, upstairs, present, effigy, lethal, envelope, ancestor, edge, restive, steep, home, mirror, razor, glass, rupture, cure, bead, gourd, scrape, comb, compass, splinter, neighbour, museum

 — Include a historical fact (personal or collective)
 — Refer to a mythical landmark or religious monument
 — Quote something you have recently read or overheard
 
 
Jesus Playing Baseball
 
Whatever panic you feel at bat,
whatever the pitcher has sleeved
the man upstairs has got your back.

His airbrushed face glows on felt,
cross-stitched, his blonde curls bounce,
etched on a mirror, you see yourself

scraping the plate, enveloped by robe
loving your neighbour's embrace--
all four eyes on the oncoming globe--

as you smash the gourd, splinter the bat
pushing the airmail envelope's edge--
arcing over the snow caps of Ararat.

How many thrift stores and charity shops
serve as museums for Our Saviour the Sport?--
his eyes baby blue, so they pop.

Here is the dream within the dream,
our fair patriarch of Rockwellian lore
whose half-price pearl smile gleams.

Sleeveless pale father, surrogate pop
you've come a long way from Calvary
with a halo tucked under your cap.

Who wouldn't want you on their team
showing up in the dugout at every game?--
to keep your nose and language clean--

cheered 'round the bases, never alone--
running for the free world, for family
running all out for home.



23 November 2020

New prompt:

royalty, buy, parade, noble, fashion, depend, blood, uplift, daydream, figure, lose, stardust


24 November 2020

Cassiopeia

She is royalty--
a loose skirt
twirled around her hips,
a parade of moths
her noble cavalcade,
in this fashion
she walks a daydream,
a figure of loss,
of sneezed stardust,
so much depends,
and deepens, upon
the time she buys
arcing across
the broad midwinter sky
as though it were warm
and easy up there
to be a woman--
or anyone at all.

1 December 2020

Turnip-headed,
elasmotherium sibiricum

3 December 2020


Perpetually Enrolled in Clown School, I Face Another Day

Like a holy fool in the court 
of the Emperor of Ice Cream,
I take one on the forehead
for the team.

The Rocky Road keeps
the cone affixed, so I
stand her turnip-headed,
elasmotherium sibiricum,
the ur-rhino, pre-unicorn,
in a milky equivalence
of Poe's Red Death,
cream seeping from my pores.

I'll do anything
for the slap of a laugh
to wake us from inverted
slumber, we slippery bats.

On the wrong bus,
weighted by tonnage
from the endless day,
I'll strike a b-flat,
on my air xylophone
the plates in my head
go full-metal murmuration
showing off again,
making a fuss, an unseen din
for unamused commuters--
ears plugged with pods,
my congregants whose hymn
whose ever-running theme song
is a lowly diesel thud.

Scrödinger's cat is mewing--
or is it? I wrack my brain
for Pavlov's name--but it
doesn't ring a bell.

And now who's laughing?
Whether you chuckle 
or guffaw, the price
of admission is the same--
one warmed-up snowflake,
a vintage bottle of air,
salt without the sea,
or sea without the salt,
invisible confetti,
the dust in the air.

Go on then, give us a grin.

14 December 2020

jazz
monopoly

Funny Thing, Hope

Low clouds mist the branches,
reinforcements for fungal colonists
and see how quickly politics
refluxes a natural scene?

We once were martyrs, now 
monopolists, and soon
the step-stool of Democracy 
will take the swift kick it begs for.

No tinsel left in tinsel-town.
No bon-bons, no boom-booms,
as the enfant terribles of Europe
pack up their jazz-hand gloves.

Still, the mockingbird writes copy.
Still, the robin sees you off.
The dilemma of our age? To remember 
the dilemma (or forget).

The vines execute their spirals,
choking the birch for kinks
so the tree goes down like the adage,
falling into furniture.

Help me finish this poem, won't you?
Let's close our eyes and wish.
"On what?" you ask.  Not starlight.
On ash. On spit. On glass.


15 December 2020

Choose a few of these:

reek, tacit, pigeon, furious, grizzle, sycamore, cordon, marrow, cast, rupture, curfew, spinet, pedal, kiss, foreign, glint

 *Include a reference to photography

 *Include or consider the following quote: “Naming is a form of adoring” Lucie Brock-Broido
 
21 December 2020

Tired of Making Polite Introductions

Because, as the poet once said,
"Naming is a form of adoring,"
I call you pedal, spinet, rupture-kissed
curlew. I knew her, or shared
at least, a little time with her,
and her cats, who were all named.

She is gone now, in a shutter-click.

Tell me the grizzle of your marrow,
but tell it quick. My pigeon, my furious,
and complicit sycamore. My glint.

The world reeks of tacit praise,
and rises--but not to the occasion--
like overnight dough. How mealy,
how cordon-cast, the net in which
we have learned to breathe. My gill.

Tell me the long fuse burning.
Tell me the razor-short of it.

11 Jan 2021

Rescue Dog: Her Dream

Through cow parsley, nettle, and heather, she pants like a zealot, plea-bargaining with the leash to set her free. That evening, sideways on the couch, her paws rasp the air, and it undulates beneath them--soft clay, sharp grass, hard flint. Then her mind finds a hole, and her nose wanders through space. What does she find in that darkness, flashing her teeth in the light of our laptop screen? Was it worth the bargain for warmth and food? Her breathing deepens as she sinks.

Tapping the window
rain becomes insistent sleet
clear drops going grey

Rescue Dog: The Memory

The little girl who taught me to play "doctor" learned it from her father. Years later, when it came out in the newspaper--what he had done to her, and other girls in the neighbourhood--he hanged himself. He was a crop-duster pilot. Warn evenings, we would watch as pesticide streaked from his biplane, blanketing the fields. We think he was the one who poisoned my dog, Chessy. He barked too much and dug under the fence. I named him that because my father was obsessed with chess. On the day I was born, he placed a pawn in my hand. I clutched it. On my fifth birthday, I rode home from the puppy mill with Chessy in the back seat of our car, my arms wrapped around his neck. My father asked: "Are you happy, son?" I squeezed Chessy and whispered, "This is the best day of my life."

He knocks me over
paws on my shoulders, tongue out
baptism of drool

Rescue dog: Poo

We look away. The smell rises, like a b-movie ghost from a body. The warmth transmits through the plastic bag.


Her back legs tremble
in a universal pose
grunting, just like us

Rescue dog: Poo

We call it "the un-corking". The first few peach pits that greet the day. Later, she will be in flow, her eyes scanning the countryside so as not to be caught with her (proverbial) pants down. We look away. The smell rises, like a b-movie ghost from a body. She scratches at a tangent, ineffectually, pelting me as I bend down to my good-citizen duty. The warmth transmits through the plastic bag.


Her back legs tremble
in a universal pose
grunting, just like us

Rescue Dog: Electric Fence

It is the pitch. It is the overtones. A yelp of flint on slate. She goes from bush-beater to home-seeker in a honed white flash. She is past us. A cork trailing fizz. A slurry of ionised adrenaline. Her heartbeat drowning out her name, as we call, and chase her like a flag. Dear God let her not cross that street.

She stands by our gate
the last safe place in her world
flinches at the latch

31 Jan 2021

"Oops"



PROMPT

Words to throw in, if needed: spray, candle, resolve, unfamiliar, run, crush, plump, dip, project, between, idea, way, flake

1 Feb 2021

"Oops!"

A spray of candle wax. The all-too-familiar smell of coffee burning on the stove.  A dip in my step. Plump bubble-wrap crushed. She flinches. She winces. The way a creature looks between kicks. And then, the saving idea. "Oops!" I say, in my jingliest tone. And say it, and say it again. Every stupid move, every trip and bump, clacking them off like beads on a loop, counting my daily foibles in high-pitched oopsies. She drops her jowls. She lowers her lids. She flakes on the couch. Never has a creature been more relieved to know my stubbed toe, smashed glass, trip over the computer cable were not meant as acts of war. I imagine the scorched earth, the wreckage brought on my my pratfalls if it were. The post-apocalyptic history books little more than a running catalogue of my frequent, near-incessant, everyday mistakes.


"Would you like a song?"
She flops on the couch, and sighs--
ukulele time

8 Feb 2021

After pressing worm-folk into the mud, in joyful defiance of the squelching trail, we towel off and settle in for my best Bing Crosby impersonation. Her eyes go horizontal. She rests her chin on the tops of her paws. I strum the notes, and make up a croon. Like my father would, clowning at the butt-end of a drawn-out workday. Like his dad did with him. My audience is rapt. Or asleep. "Ba-Baboo."

Lover of music
rustling in her velvet seat
shushed by the usher

26 Feb 2021

Write a poem inspired by any aspect of Ferlinghetti's work. 

For funsies, here's some words from some Ferlinghetti poems, if you need these to jog your brain. I know I do sometimes.

Wordsies: dog, reality, real, newsprint, San Francisco, famous, across, American, looking, birds, quiet, I, speak, pass, stationwagon, pinball addicts

The Beat
	(i.m. Lawrence Ferlinghetti)

O bright American prospect of the whiz-whirl past where no mud has ever trampled and the pinball addicts reel through headlines like a branch devoid of thorns. I miss you like San Francisco here on the trail, one sneaker (re-dubbed trainer) slowly filling with a slurry of English mud. I tripped, and couldn't make it a dance. The dog looks down from her white coat in quiet dismay as I slide like newsprint off a rain-soaked tabloid announcing a new administration of the drug we've all been craving: birds and quiet, nobody famous, the effects of our own megalomania worn thin and in the tremens of a single blink I, covered in viscous island clay, look up through the ragged branches, and raise a bloodied hand to greet the day.

Running in the mud
she puts up with my slow pace
four legs, no problem

22 March 2021

Rescue Dog: One Year On

Where do we go from here? Damp moss pools in tree-wounds like a lake. The rings ripple, disturbed by a stone, a fish, a falling leaf. In that world, I still have a father. Beside that lake, I still have a son. In the lamplight of spring, blackthorn is spreading its linen tablecloth. The strawberry heads of tulips sharpen their spears. Soon we will be allowed to picnic again--two households, two metres, no masks. How can I miss the masks?

Sniffing my fingers
after I slice field mushrooms
the heart unclenching

10 May 2021

Rescue Dog: Her Too

She knits her brows. Hunches behind the couch, head hung low sweet Jesus. Perpetual craven. Textbook case. A calendar girl for twelve years of back-hands, rocks, and broken bottle glass. I pick up a comb, she sees scissors. I raise a leg, she sees a fist. Her gaze the paste of the checked-out limbic survivor child O what have they done? I hand my wife a spoon. She sees only the red end to her belonging. Sees only the crash about to come.

Down on the carpet
I plead with myself, and her
I am not those men

Groaning Up

O aortal brick!
It's time to put away
the childish wick,
the sprig of callow
wonder-wall
and scooch into
adulthood on your spud,
buds flicking,
tendrils curled
a spate of duties
soon to peel
and peal for freedom,
yes--you will!--
as the prick
of hooch on a
silent throat,
gone dry with yawns
and the busy dust
that seems to get
everywhere,
all at once.

13 May 2021

Rescue Dog: Nameless

High on the hooch of bramble blossom, she boops a sprig, trenches a spud, paws at the curl of a sapling tendril. Then she is gone--headlong into one side of a hedgerow, and sprung from the other in Newtonian symmetry, a young dear with the face of a fox. She has forgotten her name in the four-chambered wilderness. I scooch up the trail, still calling, but "violet", "come", and my signature whistle all land like bricks in the mud.

She knows the way home,
But stops at the fork to say,
"I am still your dog"

24 May 2021

Leaving Off the Niceties

The first calf of spring surges out,
trailing a red ribbon to congratulate,

she twists onto hooves like stilettos
she'll never take off after dancing

so the competition to deserve begins
guarding her mum from bystanders

she is purple behind a dazzling sun
in the cowherd's social media pic,

the one who "walked out" on his family,
like a mother cow packing off with her teats

like the first occasion winter will take
to suffocate the ground with snow.

20 June 2021

Words to use: 
beach, devil, whirl, moss, chalk, grit, live, island, landlocked, whip, speed, curl, leave

Bonus:
write in a way that builds momentum or decrescendos 
examine or thwart an old turn of phrase

Cliffwalkers
	Newport, West Wales

Some like to take the easy road. Not us.
The beach foams and whirls below.
We are nearly to the cairn when I hear
her go down, at speed, in the chalk-dust.

The only way out is the way we came in.
The path goes lean, goes steep, and whirls--
we hobble back, counting snails, admiring moss
to keep our spirits from looking down.

On the flat, we pick up to the speed
of a wobbly toddler, then back to shuffling,
soft-toed, as we reach the first stile, 
where I demo the gymnasts routine, elbows locked.

We develop technique. "Brace" she shouts,
like cabin crew in a disaster film,
as she pulls on my shoulders, hoisting
herself and her undead leg another step up.

She can only see my dust-caked ass,
but I know there is a waterfall below,
a devil juggling apples, a djinn whose wish
is to crack us like a clam on a rock.

Back home and bandaged, we reminisce--
how soft the marsh grasses and tangle-weed,
confess our love of bracken and foxglove
at the pace of a child enraptured by the new.

That night, my foot slips in a dream,
the kind where you wake when you hit ground,
but I do not wake, as there is no ground
only the crashing sound of water far below.

Weekly Management Remote

The camera glows like a limited-time offer

of the body corporate, oozing revenue.

began in the back row of biology class.

are the top stair-steps to my next promotion.
How good it is to be uncontroversial.

When all-other-business finally fizzles
and pops like toast ready for its spread,
we click our goodbyes, and blinking burns.
Now down to see if that cheese is still there,
and check that my neck can still turn, "No".

5 July 2021

Aquamarine is the colour
of my cold, cold heart.

Fathers' Day

The rule is two strikes and you're out.
A son without a father doesn't count.

And me? I swung hard in a hospital haze,
but only lasted as a father for three days.

The taxi idles as we say goodbye to the cat
"You obviously don't have kids," he laughs.

So here's to you, the sperm bank overdrafts,
child support plaintiffs, runaways from pap.

In the middle of my life, not a single tree
just a felled trunk, upended, crumbling free.

Those with roots and leaves can lead the way.
But given the choice, I'd rather skip today.

PROMPT
